Table 4
Preparing for Improvement Kata Practice
1. Q: Where is the best place to begin practicing the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The best starting point is usually the workplace itself. A workplace provides real processes, actual operational problems, observable activities, and opportunities for colleagues to coach one another. Beginners do not need to wait for senior management commitment or a fully developed organizational improvement programme. They can begin by selecting a manageable process, establishing the learner–coach relationship, and practicing the Improvement Kata routines. The initial objective is to develop scientific thinking through repeated practice rather than immediately deliver a major operational improvement. Once the learner develops competence, the skills can be transferred to more complex processes and other organizational settings.
Study instruction:
- Identify a practical workplace process that offers opportunities for direct observation and experimentation.
- Distinguish between developing improvement capability and achieving immediate performance results.
Key point:
- Begin practicing where real work happens.
- Developing scientific thinking is the initial priority.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Official Learning Resources
2. Q: What is the main purpose of preparing before starting Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
Preparation establishes the people, process, routines, and supporting materials needed to conduct consistent practice. It includes selecting the learner, coach, and second coach; identifying an appropriate focus process; scheduling daily coaching cycles; preparing the learner's storyboard; and obtaining the Five Coaching Kata Questions cards. These arrangements create a structured learning environment in which the learner can repeatedly practice scientific thinking. Preparation also prevents common implementation problems, such as unclear responsibilities, unsuitable processes, inconsistent coaching, and insufficient time for reflection.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a practical implementation checklist covering participants, process selection, scheduling, and learning materials.
- Explain how each preparation activity contributes to effective practice.
Key point:
- Preparation establishes the conditions for disciplined and repeatable learning.
- People, process, time, and practice materials must work together.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Getting Started
3. Q: Who should participate in the initial Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
The initial practice should involve three roles: the learner, the coach, and the second coach. The learner practices the Improvement Kata and develops scientific thinking through repeated experimentation. The coach guides the learner through structured coaching cycles, observes the learner's thinking, and provides appropriate corrective feedback. The second coach observes and supports the coach, helping ensure that the coaching approach develops the intended skills. These three roles establish a learning relationship in which both improvement capability and coaching capability can develop.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple responsibility diagram showing the three roles and their interactions.
- Explain why the learner and coach should not be treated merely as an employee and performance evaluator.
Key point:
- The three roles are learner, coach, and second coach.
- Each role contributes to developing scientific thinking.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
4. Q: What are the responsibilities of the learner in Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
The learner is responsible for understanding the focus process, practicing the Improvement Kata routines, developing the current-condition picture, establishing target conditions with appropriate guidance, identifying obstacles, conducting experiments, and documenting the results. The learner must actively participate in coaching cycles rather than passively receive instructions. The learner should explain the reasoning behind decisions, identify uncertainties, compare expectations with actual outcomes, and adjust the next experiment based on evidence. The learner also owns the storyboard and is responsible for keeping it updated.
Study instruction:
- Practice explaining a process condition using actual observations and measurements.
- Prepare an example showing how an experiment changes the learner's understanding.
Key point:
- The learner owns the improvement process and learning record.
- Decisions should be supported by observation and experimentation.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
5. Q: What are the responsibilities of the coach?
A: Show answer
The coach helps the learner develop the thinking and behavior patterns required by the Improvement Kata. The coach conducts structured coaching cycles, asks the prescribed questions, listens to the learner's explanations, identifies gaps in understanding, and provides corrective guidance. The coach also determines when the learner is ready to progress to a more complicated process or improvement challenge. During initial practice, the coach should ensure that the learner concentrates on developing the fundamental routines rather than becoming distracted by ambitious improvement targets. Coaching should develop the learner's independent problem-solving capability rather than create dependence on the coach's solutions.
Study instruction:
- Practice conducting a coaching conversation without immediately providing the solution.
- Identify examples of feedback that corrects a thinking pattern rather than criticizes an individual.
Key point:
- The coach develops the learner's capability, not merely the process result.
- Coaching should encourage evidence-based thinking and independent problem-solving.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
6. Q: Who should be selected as the second coach?
A: Show answer
The second coach is commonly the coach's manager, a peer, or a relevant staff specialist. This person provides another level of observation and support for developing effective coaching practices. Ideally, the second coach has personal experience applying the Improvement Kata and understands the coaching routines. However, organizations should not postpone practice simply because an experienced second coach is unavailable. Beginners can establish the initial practice using available personnel and progressively strengthen their coaching capability.
Study instruction:
- Identify suitable candidates for the second-coach role in your organization.
- Assess their ability to observe coaching behavior and provide constructive feedback.
Key point:
- The second coach supports the development of the coach.
- Practical experience is desirable but should not prevent starting.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Learning Resources
7. Q: What qualifications or experience should an Improvement Kata coach possess?
A: Show answer
An effective coach should understand the Improvement Kata pattern, be familiar with its Starter Kata routines, and have sufficient practical experience to recognize the learner's current level of understanding. The coach should also be able to ask structured questions, listen carefully, distinguish observations from assumptions, and provide timely corrective feedback. Ideally, the coach is the learner's manager because regular management interaction supports continuous practice. Nevertheless, peer coaching and temporary external coaching arrangements can be used when internal coaching experience is limited.
Study instruction:
- Develop a coaching competency checklist covering technical understanding, observation, questioning, and feedback.
- Identify which competencies require additional practice before coaching independently.
Key point:
- Coaching competence requires practical experience and deliberate development.
- A management position alone does not automatically establish coaching competence.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
8. Q: What is a focus process in Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
A focus process is a defined series of activities performed by an individual or group to produce an output or accomplish a particular task. It involves interactions with people, routines, materials, information, and equipment. Examples include manufacturing operations, administrative procedures, hospital activities, material handling, order entry, laboratory procedures, and customer-return processing. The focus process provides the practical environment in which the learner observes actual conditions, identifies obstacles, and conducts experiments toward a target condition. It should be sufficiently defined to permit meaningful observation and measurement.
Study instruction:
- Select one operational process and document its beginning, ending, activities, inputs, and outputs.
- Distinguish an individual process from a wider value stream.
Key point:
- The focus process is the actual work being improved.
- A clearly defined process makes experimentation more manageable.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
9. Q: How should an organization select the learner's first focus process?
A: Show answer
The first focus process should be visible, understandable, and relatively simple. Beginners should avoid complicated processes that require extensive technical knowledge or involve too many interacting activities. A straightforward process allows learners to concentrate on practicing the Improvement Kata pattern instead of becoming overwhelmed by operational complexity. The selected process should also provide opportunities to establish a baseline, identify a measurable target condition, conduct experiments, and observe their effects. If necessary, the learner can temporarily work outside their normal area or focus on a smaller component of a larger process.
Study instruction:
- Compare at least three candidate processes using visibility, complexity, repeatability, and measurability.
- Explain why the simplest suitable process may be more effective for initial learning.
Key point:
- Select a process that makes learning manageable.
- The first process should support observation and experimentation.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Improvement Kata Resources
10. Q: Why should beginners avoid selecting a complicated process for their first practice?
A: Show answer
A complicated process introduces additional technical, organizational, and operational difficulties that can distract beginners from learning the Improvement Kata. The learner may spend excessive time understanding specialized equipment, complex procedures, multiple departments, or numerous dependencies. These demands can make it difficult to recognize the underlying improvement pattern. A simpler process reduces unnecessary cognitive demands and allows learners to concentrate on observing actual conditions, establishing target conditions, testing assumptions, and learning from experiments. Complexity can be introduced progressively after the learner develops basic competence.
Study instruction:
- Identify the factors that make a process difficult for a beginner to understand.
- Practice simplifying a complex process into a manageable sub-process.
Key point:
- Initial practice should prioritize learning rather than technical difficulty.
- Process complexity should increase as the learner's competence develops.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
11. Q: Why is routine work particularly suitable for a beginner's first focus process?
A: Show answer
Routine work provides repeated opportunities to observe the same activities and evaluate the effects of changes. Processes with short, recurring cycles make it easier to understand the current condition, establish a baseline, conduct several experiments, and observe results within a relatively short period. For initial practice, a process with a cycle time of approximately 30 seconds to five minutes is particularly useful. The repetition makes differences between expected and actual outcomes easier to recognize. It also gives the learner frequent opportunities to practice the Improvement Kata rather than waiting for infrequent operational events.
Study instruction:
- Identify a repetitive activity and observe several consecutive process cycles.
- Record cycle time, variation, interruptions, and recurring difficulties.
Key point:
- Repetition creates frequent learning opportunities.
- Short-cycle work supports rapid observation and experimentation.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
12. Q: What is the recommended cycle time for routine work used in initial Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
A useful starting point is a repetitive manual process with a cycle time of approximately 30 seconds to five minutes. This range provides frequent repetitions within a normal working day, allowing the learner to observe performance and evaluate experiments without waiting for long production or service cycles. The cycle-time recommendation is a practical selection guideline rather than a universal requirement. The most important consideration is whether the process provides sufficiently frequent opportunities to observe its behavior and learn from changes.
Study instruction:
- Measure the actual cycle time of a candidate process over multiple repetitions.
- Determine whether the frequency of the process supports meaningful experimentation within the practice period.
Key point:
- Short, repeatable cycles make experiments easier to conduct.
- The recommended range is a starting guideline, not a mandatory standard.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
13. Q: What should the learner achieve during the first two weeks of Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
The primary objective is to become familiar with the four-step Improvement Kata pattern and the basic Starter Kata practice routines. The learner should develop an initial understanding of the current condition, conduct experiments toward a target condition, observe the results, and practice explaining the reasoning behind each action. A short-cycle routine process makes it possible to complete several learning cycles within this period. The objective is not necessarily to deliver a major operational transformation. It is to establish a foundation for scientific thinking and structured improvement.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a two-week practice plan with daily observation, coaching, and experimentation activities.
- Evaluate learning progress separately from operational performance improvements.
Key point:
- The first two weeks establish fundamental improvement habits.
- Learning the pattern takes priority over achieving a major result.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Improvement Kata Resources
14. Q: What should happen after the learner completes the first two weeks of practice?
A: Show answer
After the initial practice period, the coach should evaluate the learner's readiness to work with more complicated processes. As the learner becomes more familiar with the Improvement Kata routines, the focus process can increasingly be selected from the learner's normal work area. The selection should also become more closely connected to organizational priorities, strategic objectives, higher-level challenges, or future-state value stream designs. This transition ensures that improvement practice develops from a learning exercise into a meaningful contribution to organizational performance.
Study instruction:
- Define measurable criteria for assessing readiness to progress beyond the initial practice process.
- Identify an operational improvement opportunity connected to a wider organizational objective.
Key point:
- Progressively increase process complexity.
- Connect subsequent improvement practice to meaningful organizational challenges.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Improvement Kata
15. Q: How should the coach decide whether a learner is ready for a more complicated focus process?
A: Show answer
The coach should assess whether the learner can consistently demonstrate the fundamental Improvement Kata behaviors. These include describing the current condition using observations, understanding the purpose of a target condition, identifying obstacles, developing testable experiments, comparing expected and actual results, and explaining what has been learned. The coach should also assess whether the learner can participate meaningfully in coaching cycles and maintain the storyboard. Progression should be based on demonstrated capability rather than simply the passage of two weeks.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a practical competency assessment using a completed improvement exercise.
- Identify specific weaknesses that should be addressed before introducing additional complexity.
Key point:
- Readiness depends on demonstrated skill.
- Progression should be guided by the coach's assessment.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
16. Q: Why should improvement practice eventually connect to strategic organizational goals?
A: Show answer
Improvement activities should contribute to meaningful organizational challenges rather than become disconnected exercises. Connecting the focus process to a higher-level challenge helps learners understand why the process matters and how improvements contribute to broader performance objectives. It also helps management coordinate improvement priorities and prevent teams from optimizing individual activities at the expense of the wider value stream. Strategic alignment does not mean prescribing every experimental action. The learner should still investigate obstacles and determine the next experiment through evidence-based thinking.
Study instruction:
- Trace one operational improvement opportunity to a department-level and organizational-level objective.
- Evaluate whether the proposed process improvement contributes to the intended outcome.
Key point:
- Improvement practice should support meaningful organizational direction.
- Strategic alignment should guide improvement priorities without eliminating learner ownership.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Improvement Kata
17. Q: What is the difference between a focus process and a value stream?
A: Show answer
A focus process is an individual work process selected for improvement practice. A value stream consists of multiple interconnected processes that collectively deliver value to a customer or recipient. For example, processing a supplier invoice is an individual process, whereas the broader procurement-to-payment value stream includes purchasing requests, approvals, ordering, receipt of goods, invoice processing, payment, and reconciliation. Improvement Kata beginners should initially concentrate on one manageable process rather than attempting to improve an entire value stream.
Study instruction:
- Map a simple value stream and identify one individual process within it.
- Explain the relationship between local process performance and end-to-end performance.
Key point:
- A focus process is narrower than a value stream.
- Individual improvements should eventually support end-to-end performance.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
18. Q: How should an organization schedule daily coaching cycles?
A: Show answer
Each learner should have a regular coaching cycle scheduled every working day at a consistent time. A practical starting arrangement is a 20-minute session near the beginning of the workday. Early scheduling gives the learner time to carry out the next experiment during the same day. Consistency makes coaching a normal management routine instead of an occasional activity that competes with other priorities. The schedule should be visible to both the learner and coach, and the meeting should be conducted close to the actual focus process whenever practical.
Study instruction:
- Create a recurring daily coaching appointment and identify a suitable location.
- Verify that the timing allows the learner to act on the agreed next step.
Key point:
- Coaching should be scheduled, consistent, and close to the work.
- A regular routine reinforces learning and accountability.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
19. Q: Why should daily coaching cycles take place near the beginning of the workday?
A: Show answer
Scheduling the coaching cycle early gives the learner sufficient time to perform the next experiment during the same working day. It also allows the learner and coach to discuss the latest observations, agree on the next learning step, and address practical obstacles before the day becomes occupied by other activities. This arrangement shortens the time between coaching and action, strengthening the connection between reflection, experimentation, and learning.
Study instruction:
- Compare the practical consequences of scheduling coaching at the beginning and end of the workday.
- Identify how delays between coaching and experimentation affect learning opportunities.
Key point:
- Early coaching supports same-day experimentation.
- Shorter delays strengthen the connection between learning and action.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
20. Q: Why should coaching cycles be conducted close to the focus process?
A: Show answer
Conducting coaching cycles near the focus process allows the learner and coach to examine actual working conditions rather than rely entirely on memory or second-hand descriptions. They can directly observe activities, equipment, materials, information flows, interruptions, and process behavior. This supports evidence-based discussion and makes it easier to verify whether the learner's understanding reflects reality. Ideally, the coach travels to the learner's work area rather than requiring the learner to leave the process environment for every coaching conversation.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a coaching discussion at the actual workplace and compare observations with previously recorded information.
- Identify differences between reported conditions and directly observed conditions.
Key point:
- Coaching should be connected to the actual work environment.
- Direct observation improves the quality of learning.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
21. Q: What is a learner's storyboard in Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
The learner's storyboard is a visual working document that records the development of the learner's Improvement Kata practice. It provides a structured overview of the focus process, current condition, target condition, obstacles, experiments, and learning progress. The storyboard supports communication between the learner and coach during daily coaching cycles. It is a living document because information may need to be updated, corrected, or expanded as new evidence becomes available. It helps the learner organize thinking and communicate the logic behind improvement activities.
Study instruction:
- Review the standard Improvement Kata storyboard format and identify the information recorded in each section.
- Prepare a blank storyboard for a selected workplace process.
Key point:
- The storyboard makes the improvement journey visible.
- It supports structured thinking and coaching conversations.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
22. Q: What information should be recorded on the learner's storyboard?
A: Show answer
The storyboard should capture the information required to explain and guide the learner's improvement practice. This includes the direction or challenge, focus process, current condition, target condition, identified obstacles, and the learner's progress through experimentation. During the execution phase, it should support the discussion of the next experiment, expected outcome, actual result, and learning obtained. The information should be concise, organized, and sufficiently detailed to support effective coaching. The learner should update the information as the understanding of the process develops.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a sample storyboard and populate it with observations from an actual process.
- Check that the information is sufficient to explain the next improvement experiment.
Key point:
- Record evidence, direction, obstacles, and learning.
- Keep information relevant to the coaching conversation.
Source links:
- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
23. Q: Why should the learner's storyboard be treated as a living document?
A: Show answer
A living document changes as the learner gains new information and understanding. The initial picture of the process may be incomplete, measurements may need correction, and assumptions may be disproved by experiments. The storyboard must therefore reflect the latest reliable information rather than remain a static record of the original improvement plan. During coaching cycles, the learner may need to add or correct details immediately. This practice reinforces the principle that improvement decisions should evolve in response to evidence.
Study instruction:
- Review a sample storyboard and identify information that should change after an experiment.
- Practice recording an updated observation and explaining why the previous understanding was revised.
Key point:
- The storyboard must evolve with learning.
- Corrections are part of disciplined improvement practice.
Source links:
- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
24. Q: Who owns and updates the learner's storyboard?
A: Show answer
The learner owns and updates the storyboard. The coach supports the learner through questions, observations, and corrective feedback but should not take over responsibility for maintaining the improvement record. Learner ownership develops accountability and encourages active thinking. Before the next coaching cycle, the learner should update the storyboard using information from the previous experiment. If new insights arise during the coaching conversation, the learner should make the necessary corrections directly.
Study instruction:
- Practice updating a storyboard independently after an experiment.
- Explain how learner ownership differs from a coach maintaining the document on the learner's behalf.
Key point:
- The learner owns the storyboard.
- The coach guides the learning process without replacing learner responsibility.

- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
25. Q: Where should the learner's storyboard be located?
A: Show answer
Ideally, the storyboard should be positioned near the focus process so that coaching conversations occur close to the work being performed. This arrangement enables the learner and coach to refer to the visual record while observing actual conditions. It also reduces unnecessary movement and makes it easier to connect recorded information with practical process observations. The location should be accessible to the learner and coach while respecting organizational requirements concerning confidentiality, information security, and workplace safety.
Study instruction:
- Identify a suitable location for a physical or digital storyboard.
- Assess accessibility, visibility, proximity to the process, and confidentiality requirements.
Key point:
- Position the storyboard near the work whenever practical.
- Accessibility supports regular coaching and observation.

26. Q: What physical requirements should be considered when preparing a learner's storyboard?
A: Show answer
The initial storyboard should follow the established Improvement Kata format and provide enough working space to display and update information. A working area of at least approximately 90 centimeters, or three feet, in height is recommended in the chapter. The storyboard does not need to be a permanent installation during initial practice. A large printed sheet, whiteboard, or another suitable visual arrangement can be used. The important consideration is that the information remains accessible, readable, and easy to revise during coaching cycles.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a physical storyboard and assess its readability from the normal coaching position.
- Ensure that there is enough room to update information without obscuring previous observations.
Key point:
- Use a visible and practical working format.
- Flexibility is more important than investing in a permanent installation immediately.

- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
27. Q: What are the Five Coaching Kata Questions?
A: Show answer
The Five Coaching Kata Questions provide a structured sequence for guiding the learner through improvement practice. They address the target condition, the actual condition, the obstacles preventing progress, the next experiment and its expected result, and the timing for reviewing what has been learned. The sequence helps the coach understand the learner's current thinking and provide appropriate guidance. The questions are not intended to create a free-form discussion or allow the coach to dictate solutions. They establish a repeatable routine that reinforces scientific thinking.
Study instruction:
- Memorize the five questions and explain the purpose of each.
- Practice asking them in sequence during a simulated coaching cycle.
Key point:
- The five questions structure the daily coaching dialogue.
- They reinforce systematic experimentation and learning.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
28. Q: Why should the coach use the Five Coaching Kata Questions in their prescribed order?
A: Show answer
The sequence establishes a logical progression from the desired outcome to the next learning action. Understanding the target condition provides the reference point for evaluating the actual condition. Understanding the actual condition helps identify the obstacles preventing progress. Identifying the relevant obstacle supports selection of the next experiment. Finally, establishing when to review the experiment ensures that learning is evaluated rather than assumed. Skipping questions can lead to premature solutions, unsupported assumptions, or experiments that are disconnected from the improvement objective.
Study instruction:
- Practice a coaching cycle using the prescribed sequence without jumping directly to solutions.
- Identify how each question provides information needed for the next question.
Key point:
- The sequence develops disciplined reasoning.
- Each question builds on the information established by previous questions.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
29. Q: Why should the learner, coach, and second coach each have a Five Question card?
A: Show answer
Each participant should have access to the same reference so that the coaching routine is understood consistently. The learner knows the questions in advance and can prepare relevant information. The coach can use the card as a structured script to conduct the coaching cycle. The second coach can use it to observe whether the coaching dialogue follows the intended sequence. Having separate cards also makes it easier for everyone to practice the routine and identify gaps in understanding.
Study instruction:
- Prepare one card for each participant and review both sides before the first coaching cycle.
- Explain how a shared question sequence improves consistency.
Key point:
- All three participants should understand the coaching routine.
- The cards provide a common reference for structured practice.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
30. Q: Why should the coach ask the Five Coaching Kata Questions exactly as written during initial practice?
A: Show answer
Beginners benefit from practicing the prescribed questions consistently because the wording and sequence help establish the intended thinking pattern. Repeated use reduces the temptation to replace structured coaching with informal advice, leading questions, or premature problem-solving. It also helps learners become familiar with the information they must prepare before each coaching cycle. Once the routine becomes familiar and the participants develop proficiency, the questions can be supported by appropriate clarifying dialogue without losing the underlying structure.
Study instruction:
- Conduct several practice coaching cycles using the exact question wording.
- Record where participants tend to deviate into advice-giving or unstructured discussion.
Key point:
- Formal repetition helps establish the coaching habit.
- Consistency is especially important during the beginner stage.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
31. Q: Can the coach ask additional questions beyond the Five Coaching Kata Questions?
A: Show answer
Yes. The five prescribed questions provide the main structure of the coaching cycle, but the coach can ask additional clarifying questions to understand the learner's reasoning. These questions can explore how observations were obtained, why an obstacle was selected, what evidence supports an assumption, or how the expected result was determined. Additional questions should deepen understanding rather than replace the prescribed sequence or turn the coaching cycle into an unrelated discussion.
Study instruction:
- Prepare supplementary questions that investigate evidence, assumptions, and reasoning.
- Practice distinguishing useful clarification from questions that unnecessarily redirect the conversation.
Key point:
- Additional questions can improve understanding.
- They should support rather than replace the five-question structure.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
32. Q: What does it mean that the Five Coaching Kata Questions are nested?
A: Show answer
Nested questions build progressively on the information obtained from earlier questions. The target condition establishes what the learner is trying to achieve. The actual condition explains the current situation relative to that target. The obstacle question identifies what is preventing progress. The next-step question focuses on a specific experiment addressing an obstacle. The final question establishes when the result will be examined. This structure prevents the learner from jumping directly from a general problem to a proposed solution without understanding the process and the obstacle being addressed.
Study instruction:
- Draw a diagram showing how the five questions depend on one another.
- Practice explaining how an unclear answer to an earlier question affects later decisions.
Key point:
- Each question builds on the previous information.
- The sequence connects direction, observation, obstacles, experimentation, and learning.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
33. Q: What is the purpose of the first Coaching Kata Question concerning the target condition?
A: Show answer
The first question establishes the intended process condition toward which the learner is working. It ensures that the learner and coach share an understanding of the desired outcome before discussing current performance or selecting another experiment. A target condition should describe how the process is expected to operate, including relevant performance characteristics and a defined timeframe. It should provide a meaningful direction for experimentation without prescribing every action required to achieve it.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a measurable target condition for a selected process.
- Distinguish a description of the desired process condition from a vague improvement ambition.
Key point:
- The target condition establishes the direction of improvement.
- Experiments should contribute to moving toward that condition.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
34. Q: What is the purpose of the second Coaching Kata Question concerning the actual condition?
A: Show answer
The second question establishes what is happening in the process now. The learner should explain the actual condition using direct observations, relevant measurements, and supporting evidence rather than relying on assumptions or general impressions. Understanding the actual condition allows the learner to recognize the gap between present performance and the target condition. It also provides the factual basis for identifying obstacles and determining whether an experiment produces the expected effect.
Study instruction:
- Collect actual process data and identify the main performance characteristics.
- Practice explaining the current condition without using unsupported conclusions.
Key point:
- Improvement begins with understanding actual conditions.
- Evidence is more reliable than assumptions.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
35. Q: What is the purpose of identifying obstacles during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
Identifying obstacles helps the learner understand what currently prevents the process from reaching its target condition. Obstacles may involve process variability, information delays, equipment limitations, unclear responsibilities, unnecessary movement, or other conditions that require investigation. The learner should identify multiple obstacles where appropriate and then select one to address through the next experiment. This approach prevents the learner from attempting to solve every problem simultaneously and maintains a clear connection between the experiment and the target condition.
Study instruction:
- Develop an obstacle list for a selected process and prioritize one obstacle for investigation.
- Distinguish an observed obstacle from an assumed root cause.
Key point:
- Obstacles identify barriers to the target condition.
- Improvement should address them through focused experimentation.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
36. Q: Why should the next step in Improvement Kata practice be treated as an experiment?
A: Show answer
The next step should be a deliberate test of an idea rather than an action assumed to be correct. The learner identifies what will be changed or investigated, predicts what will happen, performs the experiment, and compares the actual outcome with the prediction. This approach makes uncertainty explicit and creates opportunities to learn from unexpected results. Even when an experiment does not produce the anticipated improvement, it can provide valuable information about the process and help determine the next action.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an experiment statement containing the action, prediction, and method for observing the outcome.
- Practice explaining how an unexpected result changes the next decision.
Key point:
- Every improvement step should generate learning.
- An unsuccessful prediction can still produce valuable evidence.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – PDCA Cycles
37. Q: Why should the coach establish when the learner and coach will review the results of an experiment?
A: Show answer
Establishing a review time creates a clear checkpoint for examining what happened after the experiment. Without a defined review, the learner may perform an action but fail to compare the outcome with the original prediction. A scheduled review also supports timely observation and prevents experiments from remaining open without a clear learning conclusion. The coach and learner should agree when they will return to the process and examine the evidence. The timing should be appropriate to the experiment's expected duration and the speed at which results can be observed.
Study instruction:
- Include a review date or time in every experiment record.
- Check that the proposed review interval is appropriate for the expected process effect.
Key point:
- Every experiment needs a defined learning checkpoint.
- Reviewing results closes the learning loop.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – PDCA Cycles
38. Q: What should the coach observe during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The coach should observe how the learner understands the target condition, interprets the current condition, identifies obstacles, develops experiments, and evaluates results. The coach should pay attention not only to the learner's answers but also to the reasoning and evidence supporting those answers. This helps reveal the learner's current level of knowledge and identify which skill element requires further practice. The coach can then provide targeted feedback instead of giving general advice or taking control of the improvement activity.
Study instruction:
- Prepare an observation checklist based on the five coaching questions.
- Record specific examples of strong reasoning and areas requiring correction.
Key point:
- Coaching evaluates the learner's thinking process.
- Feedback should address identifiable skill gaps.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
39. Q: What should a learner do when the coach identifies an error during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should treat the error as an opportunity to improve the relevant skill. The learner should understand what was incorrect, determine why the error occurred, and practice the appropriate adjustment. Where possible, the storyboard should be corrected immediately so that it reflects the latest understanding. The learner should then apply the correction during the next opportunity rather than merely acknowledging the feedback. Errors can reveal gaps in observation, measurement, reasoning, or experimentation that should be addressed through further practice.
Study instruction:
- Document one example of an improvement-practice error and the corrective action taken.
- Practice the corrected behavior immediately after receiving feedback.
Key point:
- Errors provide information about what needs to improve.
- Corrective feedback should lead to immediate practice where practical.
Source links:
- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
40. Q: How should learners respond when Improvement Kata practice initially feels awkward or ineffective?
A: Show answer
Learners should recognize that unfamiliar thinking and behavior patterns may initially feel unnatural because established habits influence how they approach problems. This discomfort does not necessarily indicate that the practice is ineffective. Beginners should continue practicing the routines, accept that setbacks and periods of limited progress are normal, and use coaching feedback to correct errors. If necessary, a short break can help restore concentration, but practice should resume. Repeated application gradually makes the new behaviors more familiar and automatic.
Study instruction:
- Identify established problem-solving habits that interfere with structured experimentation.
- Maintain a practice record showing difficulties, corrective feedback, and progress over time.
Key point:
- Initial discomfort is a normal part of learning.
- Consistent practice helps establish new thinking habits.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Official Learning Resources
41. Q: Why should learners concentrate on one Starter Kata at a time?
A: Show answer
Concentrating on one Starter Kata at a time reduces the complexity of learning and makes it easier to develop individual skills. Beginners should not attempt to master the entire Improvement Kata behavior sequence simultaneously. Instead, they should practice the relevant routines, recognize mistakes, receive coaching, and improve their performance before adding further complexity. Over time, the individual skills become connected and develop into a more natural scientific-thinking pattern. This progression allows learners to build competence systematically rather than attempting to memorize everything at once.
Study instruction:
- Select one Starter Kata and define a specific practice objective.
- Evaluate performance before moving to another skill element.
Key point:
- Develop fundamental skills progressively.
- Individual routines eventually combine into a connected improvement pattern.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Starter Kata Resources
42. Q: Why should beginners follow the Starter Kata routines formally before adapting them?
A: Show answer
Formal practice helps beginners develop a consistent understanding of the fundamental thinking and behavior patterns. If learners modify the routines before understanding their purpose, they may unintentionally eliminate important elements or return to familiar problem-solving habits. Repeated practice allows the fundamental patterns to become more natural. As proficiency develops, learners can apply the principles with greater flexibility and integrate the individual routines into a connected flow. Adaptation should reflect genuine competence rather than impatience with structured practice.
Study instruction:
- Practice a Starter Kata exactly as prescribed before attempting modifications.
- Explain which underlying principles must remain intact when adapting the routine.
Key point:
- Learn the fundamentals before adapting the practice.
- Flexibility should develop from competence.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Starter Kata Resources
43. Q: What is the difference between structured practice and proficient Improvement Kata application?
A: Show answer
Structured practice involves following individual Starter Kata routines deliberately and consistently to develop the underlying skills. Proficient application occurs when those skills become sufficiently familiar that practitioners can combine them naturally in response to changing circumstances. A proficient learner may recognize relationships between the challenge, current condition, target condition, and emerging obstacles without treating every activity as a separate checklist item. However, flexibility should not eliminate the fundamental principles of scientific thinking, including observation, prediction, experimentation, and learning from results.
Study instruction:
- Compare the behavior of a beginner and an experienced practitioner during an improvement exercise.
- Identify which fundamental principles remain consistent despite differences in execution.
Key point:
- Structured practice builds capability.
- Proficiency allows flexible application without abandoning fundamental principles.
Source links:
- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
44. Q: How does scientific thinking connect the different stages of the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
Scientific thinking connects the stages through continuous observation, prediction, experimentation, and adjustment. Understanding a challenge helps establish direction. Observing the current condition reveals what is happening in reality. Defining a target condition establishes a desired next state. Experimentation tests ideas about how to move toward that state. The results provide new information that may change the learner's understanding of both the current and target conditions. Therefore, the stages are interconnected rather than isolated administrative tasks.
Study instruction:
- Trace a practical improvement example from initial observation through experimentation and revised understanding.
- Identify where new evidence requires the learner to reconsider an earlier assumption.
Key point:
- Improvement is a continuous learning process.
- Each experiment can change what the learner understands.
Source links:
- Association for Manufacturing Excellence – Kata Resources
45. Q: Why should learners view uncertainty as an opportunity rather than a problem?
A: Show answer
Uncertainty indicates that the learner does not yet fully understand how the process behaves or how a proposed change will affect it. Instead of hiding this uncertainty behind assumptions, scientific thinking makes it explicit and investigates it through experimentation. Unexpected outcomes provide information that can improve the learner's understanding. This approach reduces dependence on unsupported certainty and encourages continuous exploration. Uncertainty becomes useful when the learner can formulate a prediction, conduct a controlled test, observe the result, and adjust the next action accordingly.
Study instruction:
- Identify an uncertain process assumption and develop an experiment to investigate it.
- Record what the experiment reveals, including unexpected outcomes.
Key point:
- Uncertainty creates opportunities for learning.
- Experimentation transforms uncertainty into better understanding.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Scientific Thinking Resources
46. Q: What is the difference between the Improvement Kata and the Coaching Kata?
A: Show answer
The Improvement Kata is the routine through which learners develop scientific thinking and work systematically toward target conditions. It involves understanding the direction or challenge, grasping the current condition, establishing a target condition, and experimenting through obstacles. The Coaching Kata is the routine used by coaches to develop the learner's ability to practice the Improvement Kata. It provides a structured coaching dialogue and feedback process that reinforces the intended thinking pattern. The two routines work together: one develops improvement capability, while the other develops that capability through deliberate coaching.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison showing the purpose, participants, and principal activities of each Kata.
- Explain how daily coaching supports the learner's improvement practice.
Key point:
- Improvement Kata develops scientific problem-solving capability.
- Coaching Kata develops that capability through structured guidance.
Source links:
- Toyota Kata – Improvement and Coaching Kata
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
47. Q: What is the difference between a coaching cycle and an informal management meeting?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle is a structured learning interaction designed to develop the learner's scientific thinking. It follows a defined question sequence, focuses on the target and actual conditions, examines obstacles, and establishes the next experiment and review point. An informal management meeting may cover operational updates, performance concerns, decisions, or general communication without necessarily developing the learner's improvement capability. Although both can discuss work performance, a coaching cycle has a specific educational purpose and should not become a general status meeting.
Study instruction:
- Compare the agenda and expected outcomes of a coaching cycle and a conventional management meeting.
- Practice keeping an improvement coaching conversation focused on the five questions.
Key point:
- Coaching cycles develop capability through structured dialogue.
- General management discussions do not automatically constitute Kata coaching.
Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – The Five Coaching Kata Questions
48. Q: What should an organization do if no experienced Improvement Kata coach is available?
A: Show answer
The organization should use the available resources and begin developing internal capability rather than indefinitely postponing practice. A temporary external coach or a peer-coaching arrangement can provide initial support. A manager or supervisor can also develop coaching skills through deliberate practice and appropriate learning resources. The organization should recognize that coaching competence takes time to develop and establish a process for strengthening it through observation, feedback, and repeated application.
Study instruction:
- Identify potential temporary coaches and internal candidates for future coaching responsibilities.
- Prepare a development plan for building coaching competence.
Key point:
- Lack of experienced coaches need not prevent initial practice.
- Coaching capability can be developed progressively.

- Toyota Kata – The Coaching Kata
49. Q: What should happen if the learner's storyboard contains incorrect or incomplete information?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should identify the affected information, examine the available evidence, and determine what needs to be corrected or investigated further. The learner should update the storyboard to reflect the most reliable understanding available. If the information cannot yet be verified, the uncertainty should be made explicit rather than presented as an established fact. Corrections made during coaching cycles help prevent incorrect assumptions from influencing subsequent experiments and decisions.
Study instruction:
- Review a sample storyboard containing inconsistent measurements or unsupported assumptions.
- Practice documenting corrections and identifying information that still requires verification.
Key point:
- The storyboard should reflect the best available evidence.
- Unverified information should not be presented as fact.

- Coaching Kata – Using the Learner's Storyboard
50. Q: How can an organization evaluate whether initial Improvement Kata preparation has been completed successfully?
A: Show answer
Preparation can be evaluated by checking whether the learner, coach, and second coach have been identified; an appropriate focus process has been selected; the participants understand their responsibilities; daily coaching cycles have been scheduled; a blank storyboard is available; and the Five Coaching Kata Questions cards have been prepared. The organization should also confirm that the process is accessible for observation and that the learner has sufficient time to conduct experiments. Preparation is complete when the practical conditions exist for regular, structured learning, not simply when documents have been distributed.
Study instruction:
- Develop a readiness checklist and verify each requirement before the first coaching cycle.
- Identify outstanding issues and assign responsibility for resolving them.
Key point:
- Preparation requires people, process, schedule, and learning materials.
- Operational readiness matters more than administrative completion alone.

- Toyota Kata – Getting Started
51. Q: What practical scenario demonstrates effective preparation for initial Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
Consider an organization that wants to improve its supplier invoice-processing procedure. Instead of beginning with the entire procurement-to-payment value stream, it selects the repetitive activity of checking and recording incoming invoices. An employee becomes the learner, the immediate manager becomes the coach, and another experienced manager becomes the second coach. The team schedules a daily 20-minute coaching cycle, prepares a storyboard near the processing area, and obtains the Five Coaching Kata Questions cards. The learner then observes the process, records the current condition, and practices experiments toward a manageable target condition. This arrangement provides a controlled environment for developing improvement skills.
Study instruction:
- Develop a similar preparation scenario using an actual administrative or operational process.
- Identify the three participants, process boundaries, learning materials, and daily schedule.
Key point:
- Start with a manageable real-world process.
- Structured preparation enables practical experimentation.

52. Q: What would you do if management wants immediate financial results from the learner's first Improvement Kata exercise?
A: Show answer
Explain that the initial exercise is primarily intended to develop the learner's improvement capability. Financial or operational benefits may emerge from the experiments, but they should not replace the learning objective. Management should select a process that is relevant and measurable while allowing the learner to practice the fundamental routines. Performance improvements can be recorded, but the learner's progress should also be evaluated through the quality of observations, predictions, experiments, and learning. Once basic competence develops, subsequent practice should be increasingly connected to strategic organizational goals.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short explanation distinguishing capability development from immediate performance delivery.
- Identify appropriate learning and operational measures for the initial exercise.
Key point:
- Initial practice develops improvement capability.
- Operational results and learning outcomes should be evaluated separately.

53. Q: How can an organization prevent Improvement Kata practice from becoming an unstructured collection of improvement activities?
A: Show answer
The organization should maintain a defined direction for improvement, use appropriate focus processes, conduct regular coaching cycles, and require learners to document their current conditions, target conditions, obstacles, and experiments. Coaches should ensure that improvement activities contribute to meaningful objectives rather than allowing unrelated changes to accumulate. The learner should be encouraged to explain the connection between each experiment and the obstacle being addressed. This structure provides consistency while preserving the learner's responsibility for investigation and learning.
Study instruction:
- Review an existing improvement initiative and assess whether its activities are connected to a defined target condition.
- Identify missing elements in its coaching, documentation, and experimentation routines.
Key point:
- Improvement should follow a coherent direction.
- Coaching and documented experiments prevent random activity from replacing systematic learning.

- Improvement Kata Practice Guide – Mike Rother
54. Q: How should an organization balance standardized Starter Kata routines with the need to adapt to different working environments?
A: Show answer
Organizations should initially teach and practice the fundamental routines consistently so that learners develop a reliable understanding of scientific thinking. Differences in process characteristics, organizational structures, and operating environments may eventually require practical adjustments. However, those adjustments should preserve the essential principles of observing actual conditions, establishing meaningful target conditions, identifying obstacles, testing predictions, and learning from outcomes. Experienced practitioners can integrate the routines more flexibly, but adaptation should be based on demonstrated competence rather than arbitrary changes.
Study instruction:
- Identify which elements of a Starter Kata are fundamental and which aspects may be adjusted to suit a workplace.
- Develop an example of an adaptation that preserves the underlying improvement principle.
Key point:
- Standardization supports learning.
- Adaptation becomes appropriate as competence develops, provided fundamental principles remain intact.

- Toyota Kata – Starter Kata Resources
55. Q: How can an organization sustain Improvement Kata practice after the initial learning period?
A: Show answer
Sustainable practice requires regular coaching, appropriate focus-process selection, learner ownership, continued development of coaches, and alignment with organizational challenges. Management should protect time for daily coaching cycles and ensure that learners can observe the work directly. As participants become more proficient, they should progressively apply the routines to more complex processes and meaningful improvement opportunities. The organization should also develop additional coaches so that improvement capability does not depend on one individual. Over time, scientific thinking should become part of normal operational management rather than a temporary improvement project.
Study instruction:
- Develop a 90-day plan covering coaching frequency, process progression, coach development, and organizational alignment.
- Identify indicators showing whether the routines are becoming established management habits.
Key point:
- Sustainable improvement requires repeated practice and coaching.
- Organizational capability develops when scientific thinking becomes part of everyday work.
