Rows: 68
Table 6
Establishing the Next Target Condition: Improvement Kata Step 3
1. Q: What is the next target condition in the Improvement Kata, and what does it establish?
A: Show answer
The next target condition is a description of the desired state of the focus process at a defined point in the near future, on the way toward the longer-term challenge. It states where the learner wants the process to be next, not the actions or solution that will be used to get there. It follows understanding the challenge direction and studying the current condition. A useful target condition describes a meaningful future operating state that is close enough to work toward but sufficiently beyond present knowledge to require learning. It gives the learner and coach a shared destination for the next cycle of experimentation.
Study instruction:
- "Write a target condition for a process you know and check whether it describes a future state rather than a list of tasks."
Key point:
- A target condition describes the next destination, not the route.
- It connects the current condition to the longer-term challenge.
Source links:
2. Q: How does a target condition differ from the overall challenge?
A: Show answer
The challenge describes the broader direction and a more distant performance need. A target condition is the next nearer goal point that advances the focus process toward that challenge. The challenge provides the frame for choosing the direction; the target condition translates that direction into a concrete state to work toward now. A challenge may require several target conditions before it is reached. The learner should therefore avoid treating the challenge as if it were the next immediate target. The target condition must be sized for a nearer learning cycle and should be grounded in what is known about the current process.
Study instruction:
- "Take one broad challenge and draft a nearer target condition that advances it without simply repeating the challenge."
Key point:
- The challenge sets direction; the target condition defines the next step in that direction.
- Several target conditions may be needed to reach one challenge.
Source links:
3. Q: Why does the Improvement Kata establish only one target condition at a time?
A: Show answer
Defining one target condition at a time allows the learner to use what is actually learned before deciding what should come next. A detailed roadmap for the entire journey would require predictions about conditions that have not yet been observed. Those predictions may become unreliable as experiments reveal new information. The chapter compares this to taking successive golf shots toward a distant hole: the next landing point is selected using the actual result of the previous shot. In the same way, reaching a target condition and reflecting on the learning gives a more realistic basis for choosing the next one.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a fixed long-range improvement roadmap with a sequence in which each next target is selected after reviewing actual learning."
Key point:
- Choose the next target condition using the latest evidence.
- A plan is a hypothesis to be updated, not a route to follow blindly.
Source links:
4. Q: How does a sequence of target conditions make an uncertain improvement path manageable?
A: Show answer
A distant challenge can be too large and uncertain to solve in one move. A sequence of nearer target conditions divides the journey into manageable learning intervals. Each interval gives the learner a clear destination while preserving room to discover what the process actually requires. When a target condition is reached, the learner reviews the observed result, the obstacles encountered, and the new understanding gained before selecting the next condition. This approach does not assume that the exact route is known in advance. It uses successive learning cycles to progressively clarify both the process and the path toward the challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Map a challenging improvement need into two or three possible stages, then explain why the later stages should remain provisional."
Key point:
- Near-term target conditions make a distant challenge actionable.
- The sequence should evolve as knowledge of the process improves.
Source links:
5. Q: What is meant by taking advantage of unfolding reality?
A: Show answer
Taking advantage of unfolding reality means allowing actual observations and experimental learning to inform the next target condition instead of pretending that future conditions are already known. A plan can be useful, but it is a prediction about what may happen. As work proceeds, the learner discovers facts that were not visible at the beginning. The Improvement Kata uses those discoveries to refine the understanding of the path. This makes the process adaptive without abandoning the challenge direction. The discipline is to learn from what happens, rather than force new evidence to fit an old plan.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one assumption in an improvement plan and state what new evidence would cause you to revise the next target condition."
Key point:
- Reality revealed through practice should inform the next step.
- Adaptation is evidence-led, not arbitrary.
Source links:
6. Q: How far into the future should an achieve-by date normally be set?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a usual horizon of approximately one week to three months, while warning that a longer period is often ineffective and may need to be divided into smaller target-condition increments. The appropriate interval depends on the process, the learner's skill, and the amount of learning required. For beginners, shorter periods provide more opportunities to practice all four steps of the Improvement Kata and to adjust based on what has been learned. The date should be an actual calendar date, not a vague phrase such as in two weeks. The horizon is a learning design choice, not simply a project deadline.
Study instruction:
- "Choose an actual calendar date for a proposed target condition and justify the interval by the process scope and learner experience."
Key point:
- An achieve-by date makes the target condition time-bounded.
- Shorter cycles often support faster learning, especially for beginners.
Source links:
7. Q: Why can a target condition set too far into the future become ineffective?
A: Show answer
A distant achieve-by date can make the target condition too broad, causing the learner to take on too many unknowns at once. Long horizons also delay the moment when the learner can review actual results and adjust the next target. That reduces the number of repetitions of the Improvement Kata pattern and can encourage planning based on assumptions rather than evidence. A large future state may be useful as a broader picture, but the immediate target condition should be scaled to a nearer interval. If the proposed scope cannot be meaningfully learned within the period, it should be broken into smaller target conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Review a long-range target and identify which parts could be separated into nearer learning intervals."
Key point:
- A target condition that is too distant can become too large to learn against.
- Break an oversized future state into successive target conditions.
Source links:
8. Q: How should a target condition balance challenge and achievability?
A: Show answer
A good target condition should stretch the learner just beyond the edge of current knowledge and skill, while remaining a meaningful near-term challenge. If it is too easy, the learner is mainly repeating what is already known and may gain little self-efficacy. If it is unrealistically difficult, failure may reinforce the belief that improvement is beyond reach. The coach helps calibrate the stretch by considering the learner's capability, proposing an appropriate achieve-by date, and giving feedback on the developing target condition. Frequent corrective feedback and support help the learner experience progress while confronting genuine uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a proposed target as too easy, appropriately stretching, or excessively difficult, and explain the evidence for your classification."
Key point:
- The target should stretch current capability without overwhelming the learner.
- Coaching support helps turn a difficult target into a learning opportunity.
Source links:
9. Q: Why does a positive, forward-looking mindset matter when establishing a target condition?
A: Show answer
A target condition focuses attention on a desired future state rather than only on problems that people want to escape. That orientation can mobilize ingenuity, creativity, and teamwork because participants are working toward a shared condition. It also shifts discussion away from who has the most persuasive opinion and toward what the process should look like. The target condition acts as a common frame for deciding what matters. It does not deny current problems; rather, it uses the desired state to identify which problems and obstacles are relevant to the next improvement effort.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a problem-only statement as a positive description of the process condition that should exist in the future."
Key point:
- A shared desired state gives improvement work a constructive direction.
- The target condition focuses debate on the process, not personal persuasion.
Source links:
10. Q: How does a target condition help prevent random improvement activity?
A: Show answer
Without a target condition, a team can become occupied with disconnected troubleshooting, personal preferences, and whichever improvement idea is most attractive at the moment. A target condition supplies a defined reference point for judging whether an activity is relevant. It helps the learner ask what is preventing the process from reaching the desired state and directs attention to those obstacles. It therefore constrains the search in a productive way without prescribing the countermeasure. The team is less likely to spread effort across every possible improvement and more likely to learn from focused work on what is actually in the way.
Study instruction:
- "List several possible improvement ideas for one process and use a proposed target condition to decide which are relevant now."
Key point:
- The target condition provides focus for improvement effort.
- Work on obstacles that matter to the next destination, not every possible issue.
Source links:
11. Q: How is scientific thinking common to both maintaining and improving a process?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that both maintaining a standard and raising a standard involve a gap between what is desired and what is currently happening. In either case, there is a set of desired circumstances that the learner does not yet know how to establish completely. The learner must understand the gap and learn through experimentation. Improvement is not the only setting in which this scientific approach applies; maintaining reliable performance can also involve learning how to close a gap between the expected and actual condition. The target condition makes the desired circumstances explicit so that learning can be directed toward them.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one example of maintaining a standard and one of raising a standard, then identify the desired condition and the knowledge still missing in each."
Key point:
- Both maintaining and improving involve a gap between actual and desired conditions.
- Scientific problem solving requires learning how to close that gap.
Source links:
12. Q: What are the three essential elements of a good target condition?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three elements: when the condition should exist, what outcome performance is desired, and how the focus process is expected to operate to generate that outcome. The when is the specific achieve-by date. The what is a measurable outcome target connected, where possible, to the challenge or future-state design. The how is the desired operating pattern and process characteristics that are predicted to produce the outcome. These elements work together. A date and a numerical result alone do not fully describe the process state; the operating pattern explains the conditions expected to generate the result.
Study instruction:
- "Use the when, what, and how structure to review a target condition and identify any missing element."
Key point:
- A complete target condition combines a date, an outcome, and an operating pattern.
- The operating pattern connects process behavior to outcome performance.
Source links:
13. Q: Why is an outcome metric alone not a complete target condition?
A: Show answer
An outcome metric describes a result but does not explain the process condition expected to produce it. For example, a scrap-rate figure states a performance level, but it does not describe how machines are maintained, how work steps are performed, or how quality checks occur. Outcomes are usually produced by the way a process operates and cannot generally be changed by directly demanding a different number. A target condition therefore includes both the desired outcome and the operating attributes that are predicted to generate it. The process pattern becomes the practical area in which the learner can observe, experiment, and make changes.
Study instruction:
- "Take a numerical performance goal and add the process characteristics that could plausibly generate that result."
Key point:
- An outcome target says what result is wanted.
- A target condition also describes the operating state expected to produce it.
Source links:
14. Q: What does the chapter mean by the operating pattern being the construction site for performance?
A: Show answer
The operating pattern consists of the process characteristics that directly shape how work is carried out. These characteristics are the practical conditions on which improvement activity can act. An outcome such as lower scrap, faster delivery, or a higher success rate is produced by the process rather than created by the metric itself. The learner therefore describes the desired sequence, timing, work methods, buffers, checks, or other relevant attributes of the focus process. This makes the target condition actionable as a description of the process while leaving the actual countermeasures to be discovered through experimentation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the process characteristics that influence one important outcome in your workplace and describe which ones belong in a target operating pattern."
Key point:
- The process operating pattern is where changes can be made and observed.
- Outcome performance is generated by how the process works.
Source links:
15. Q: How should the desired operating pattern be described?
A: Show answer
Describe how the focus process is expected to operate when the target condition exists. The description should be concrete enough to guide observation and later experimentation, but only as detailed as current knowledge supports. Depending on the process, it may cover the sequence and timing of work, movement of materials, work-in-process buffers, quality checks, customer interactions, or other operating characteristics. The chapter advises going deep rather than wide: develop meaningful detail about the important aspects of the focus process instead of producing a broad collection of superficial aspirations. The description should be tied to the desired outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a process description that identifies the sequence, timing, handoffs, checks, and other characteristics relevant to the desired outcome."
Key point:
- Describe the way the process should operate, not just the result.
- Depth on important process characteristics is more useful than a wide list of vague aspirations.
Source links:
16. Q: What does the instruction to go deep rather than wide mean?
A: Show answer
Going deep means developing a detailed understanding of the operating characteristics that are most important to the desired outcome. Going wide means listing many changes across the process without enough clarity about how the process should actually work. The chapter's examples show that the relevant detail differs by context: a service process may require a defined customer experience, a bakery may specify how customers are greeted and served, and manufacturing may specify work sequence, timing, buffers, and quality checks. The aim is not to describe every possible feature. It is to describe the key operating pattern with enough precision to support learning.
Study instruction:
- "Select one critical part of a process and describe its desired operating pattern in observable detail before adding other areas."
Key point:
- Prioritize precise detail about the process characteristics that drive the result.
- A broad wish list is not a substitute for an operating pattern.
Source links:
17. Q: Why should a target condition be written as if the team is already there?
A: Show answer
Writing the target condition as if the future state already exists encourages the learner to describe observable conditions rather than intentions or improvement activities. The learner imagines arriving at the achieve-by date and observing the focus process in operation. The description then states what is present: for example, a maximum cycle time or a defined work pattern. This makes the target condition a destination that can be compared with reality. It also helps separate the desired state from the actions that may later be tested to create it.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a future-oriented action statement as a description of what an observer would see when the target condition is in place."
Key point:
- Describe the future process state as an existing condition.
- Destination language is clearer than intention language.
Source links:
18. Q: Why does the chapter caution against verbs such as reduce, improve, and increase in a target condition?
A: Show answer
Verbs such as reduce, improve, and increase describe movement or an action toward a result rather than the condition that should exist at the destination. The chapter uses the contrast between reducing cycle-time variation and specifying a maximum cycle time to show the difference. The latter describes a condition that can be observed on the achieve-by date. Avoiding action verbs encourages the learner to state the future process attributes precisely. It does not mean that verbs are prohibited in every sentence in the improvement process; the guidance concerns the wording of the target condition itself.
Study instruction:
- "Find action-oriented verbs in a draft target condition and replace them with observable descriptions of the future state."
Key point:
- A target condition describes what exists, not the act of changing it.
- Use observable state descriptions instead of improvement slogans.
Source links:
19. Q: How can a learner avoid pretending to know operating-pattern details that remain uncertain?
A: Show answer
The learner should recognize the threshold of current knowledge and avoid inventing detail to make the target condition look complete. If a process characteristic is not understood, the learner can return to the focus process to observe and study it further. Alternatively, the learner can leave that part of the desired operating pattern open and add detail later as experiments in the next step reveal more. The chapter explicitly favors honest incompleteness over false precision. A target condition should reflect what is genuinely understood, while remaining open to additional detail as knowledge develops.
Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed target condition and mark each detail as observed, reasonably predicted, or still unknown."
Key point:
- Do not cross the knowledge threshold by guessing.
- Honest gaps are preferable to invented process detail.
Source links:
20. Q: What are the two choices when the learner discovers that important process details are not yet understood?
A: Show answer
The learner can stay in the target-condition step and study the focus process further to obtain the missing detail, or move into the next step and add detail while experimenting and learning. The first choice is appropriate when the missing information is necessary to define a useful next condition. The second is appropriate when the basic target condition is sufficiently clear to begin the next learning cycle. The chapter's assembly example uses the second option after the learner has identified a meaningful near-term condition around the tank-welder bottleneck but does not yet know exactly how the unload-and-load work should change.
Study instruction:
- "For an uncertain process detail, decide whether more observation is needed before proceeding or whether it can be learned safely through the next experimental cycle."
Key point:
- More study and later learning through experimentation are both legitimate choices.
- Proceed when the basic target condition is useful, not when every detail is known.
Source links:
21. Q: Can the desired operating pattern be incomplete when the learner moves to the next Improvement Kata step?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter states that the desired operating pattern can remain incomplete when the learner begins the next step. The learner should not fill gaps with guesses merely to create a polished description. As experiments reveal more about the process, the learner can add detail and improve the description. This preserves a distinction between what is currently known and what remains to be discovered. It also prevents a premature solution from being embedded in the target condition. The key requirement is that the agreed target condition provides a sufficiently clear frame for the next learning activity.
Study instruction:
- "Identify which parts of a real target condition must be specified now and which could be developed through later observation and experimentation."
Key point:
- A target condition can be useful without being fully detailed.
- Add evidence-based detail as process knowledge grows.
Source links:
22. Q: Once agreed, what may be added to a target condition and what must remain fixed?
A: Show answer
After the learner and coach agree on a target condition, further detail may be added as the learner learns more, but the target condition itself and its achieve-by date are not changed. The chapter presents this constraint as a way to encourage careful study of the current condition, thoughtful selection of the next target, and persistence in working on obstacles rather than easing the target when progress becomes difficult. The distinction is between clarifying the agreed condition and moving the goalposts. Additional understanding can make the description more precise without changing the commitment that frames the learning cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Take a target condition that gains new process detail and distinguish a legitimate clarification from a change to the agreed destination or date."
Key point:
- Add learning-based detail without moving the agreed target or date.
- A stable target protects the integrity of the learning cycle.
Source links:
23. Q: Why should a target condition not be changed simply because progress becomes difficult?
A: Show answer
Changing the target whenever work becomes difficult can hide the information contained in an unsuccessful prediction. It may also encourage the learner to reduce the challenge instead of examining obstacles and learning how the process behaves. The chapter uses the fixed target and achieve-by date to reinforce persistence, creativity, and disciplined reflection. This does not mean ignoring safety, quality, budget, or other legitimate constraints; those must be considered when the target is established. Once agreement is reached, however, difficulty should prompt investigation and learning rather than automatic adjustment of the destination.
Study instruction:
- "Review a delayed improvement effort and list the learning questions to investigate before considering any change to the agreed target."
Key point:
- Difficulty is a reason to learn about obstacles, not automatically to lower the target.
- A stable commitment makes unconfirmed predictions visible.
Source links:
24. Q: What should the learner do if the target condition is not reached by its achieve-by date?
A: Show answer
Not reaching a target condition by its date is described as normal and can still produce useful learning. The result means that a prediction was not confirmed. The learner should reflect on what actually happened, what assumptions proved inaccurate, which obstacles emerged, and what the process revealed. Those learnings inform the next target condition. If the target or date had been changed during the cycle, some of that learning could be lost or obscured. The chapter therefore treats an unmet target as information for the next cycle, not simply as a reason to conceal the miss.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a review of an unmet target that separates the original prediction, observed result, unexpected obstacles, and learning for the next cycle."
Key point:
- An unmet target can produce valuable information.
- Preserve the original prediction so that learning remains visible.
Source links:
25. Q: Why may the first target condition for a focus process be less refined than later ones?
A: Show answer
The first target condition is often established while the learner is still developing an understanding of the current condition. As the learner observes the process and practices the Improvement Kata, more accurate information becomes available. The first target may therefore be a reasonable initial step rather than the best possible description of the future process. The chapter links this to the value of a short first achieve-by date, often one or two weeks, so the learner can review results and adjust the next target sooner. Improvement in target quality is expected as the learner's knowledge of reality increases.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an initial target condition with a later revision and identify which new observations improved the learner's understanding."
Key point:
- The first target is a learning starting point, not necessarily the final design.
- Short initial cycles help the learner refine future targets sooner.
Source links:
26. Q: Why is a target condition not the same as a target solution?
A: Show answer
A target condition describes the process attributes that should exist in the future. A target solution or countermeasure describes an action that someone proposes to create those attributes. The chapter warns that beginners may arrive with strong solution ideas and mistakenly write those actions into the target condition. Doing so narrows the search too early and can prevent the learner from discovering better approaches through experimentation. The learner should first describe the intended operating state and the effect that a proposed solution is expected to have. Countermeasures are developed and tested later when obstacles are addressed.
Study instruction:
- "Take a list of proposed actions and rewrite each as the process condition or effect the action is intended to create."
Key point:
- The target condition is the desired state; the countermeasure is a possible means.
- Keep solutions out of the destination description.
Source links:
27. Q: How can a coach help when a learner prematurely proposes a solution?
A: Show answer
The coach can ask what effect the learner expects the proposed solution to have on the focus process. That question moves the discussion from the action itself toward the condition the action is intended to produce. For example, instead of accepting a statement that a new device will be installed, the coach can ask what observable process characteristic should exist after the device is in place. The resulting description may reveal the true target condition and leave room for alternative countermeasures. The coach should help the learner clarify the destination without taking over the learner's thinking or prescribing the answer.
Study instruction:
- "Practice converting a proposed countermeasure into an observable process condition by asking what effect the action is expected to create."
Key point:
- Ask about the intended process effect, not only the proposed action.
- Coaching should broaden thinking rather than prescribe a solution.
Source links:
28. Q: Why does a target condition need to extend beyond the learner's current knowledge threshold?
A: Show answer
A target that the learner already knows how to reach may amount only to rearranging existing capabilities and knowledge. The chapter argues that a meaningful target condition should confront a threshold of knowledge and require experimentation and learning. This is what distinguishes improvement from simply repeating familiar work. The stretch should still be calibrated to the learner's skill and the near-term horizon. The objective is not uncertainty for its own sake, but a condition that creates a worthwhile learning need. If the route is already fully known, the target may be an ordinary task or plan rather than a genuine learning target.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether a proposed target requires new learning or merely applies a method already understood, and explain why that distinction matters."
Key point:
- A meaningful target condition creates a genuine learning challenge.
- Known routine work may not move the learner beyond the current knowledge threshold.
Source links:
29. Q: What does it mean that target-condition thinking is more about 'and' than 'or'?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the phrase to distinguish creating a new operating possibility from choosing between familiar alternatives. A team may be asked to choose between complete inspection with more operators or sampling inspection with fewer operators. Target-condition thinking asks whether the desired future state could combine complete inspection and fewer operators, even if the team does not yet know how to achieve it. This framing challenges a false dilemma and encourages exploration beyond existing options. The team must still respect safety, quality, budget, and other constraints, but should not assume that today's alternatives exhaust what may be possible.
Study instruction:
- "Take a choice framed as either-or and formulate a more ambitious combined condition that would require learning to achieve."
Key point:
- Do not assume the only options are the familiar alternatives.
- A target condition can define a new combination that has not yet been worked out.
Source links:
30. Q: How should a learner respond when someone asks, 'How will you achieve that target condition?'
A: Show answer
The learner should be honest about not knowing the full route when the target condition deliberately crosses the current knowledge threshold. The chapter's response is that if the answer were already known, the task would not require the Improvement Kata's learning pattern. What is known is the method of working: establish the target condition, identify obstacles, and proceed through experimentation. The learner should not pretend to have a complete solution plan merely to reassure others. At the same time, the target must be considered within real constraints and supported by a disciplined learning process.
Study instruction:
- "Practice explaining why a target can be clear even when the means are uncertain, while naming the learning process that will be used."
Key point:
- Not knowing the route is compatible with having a clear destination.
- The Improvement Kata provides a disciplined way to learn the route.
Source links:
31. Q: Why should return-on-investment calculations not determine the target condition?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes the strategic question of where the process needs to be next from the later work of discovering how to get there. Selecting the target solely by the highest return or lowest risk can keep the team within familiar options and discourage the creation of a new operating possibility. The target condition should first express the next needed state in the direction of the challenge. It must still be achievable within budget and other constraints, but the route may require trial and error, ingenuity, and resourcefulness. The chapter does not say that financial considerations are irrelevant to implementation; it says they should not replace the strategic choice of the desired condition.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a target selected only by a return calculation with one selected to advance the challenge, and identify where budget constraints should be considered."
Key point:
- Choose the desired next condition strategically, not solely by ranking familiar options.
- Budget and other constraints still govern what can responsibly be pursued.
Source links:
32. Q: How should safety, quality, budget, and other fixed parameters influence a target condition?
A: Show answer
The learner should recognize parameters that cannot be changed and incorporate them as constraints when describing the desired operating pattern. The chapter explicitly notes that safety, quality, and budget may be non-negotiable. A target condition is not permission to trade away essential requirements in pursuit of a performance number. Instead, the learner should explore how the process might operate differently while preserving those constraints. If the target appears incompatible with a critical parameter, that conflict must be surfaced during discussion with the coach and resolved before agreement, rather than ignored and discovered after implementation.
Study instruction:
- "List the non-negotiable constraints for a process and test a proposed target condition against each one."
Key point:
- Stretch does not mean violating safety, quality, or budget boundaries.
- Constraints should be explicit during target-condition development.
Source links:
33. Q: What is the respective role of the learner and coach when establishing a target condition?
A: Show answer
The learner develops and proposes the future condition, using knowledge of the focus process and the challenge. The coach helps calibrate the learning stretch, proposes or guides the achieve-by horizon, asks questions about the operating pattern, and provides feedback until the two reach consensus. The coach does not simply hand the learner a solution. The learner may revise the description several times, return to observe the process, or leave some details open. This dialogue helps ensure that the target is challenging, relevant, and grounded in the learner's understanding while still encouraging learning beyond current capability.
Study instruction:
- "Role-play a target-condition discussion in which the learner proposes the state and the coach uses questions rather than supplying solutions."
Key point:
- The learner owns the proposal; the coach guides and calibrates it.
- Consensus creates a shared frame for the next learning cycle.
Source links:
34. Q: How does a coach calibrate the achieve-by date and the stretch of the target?
A: Show answer
The coach considers the learner's current Improvement Kata skill and proposes how far into the future the achieve-by date should lie. The coach also gives feedback on the details of the target condition so that it stretches the learner just beyond the current skill level. A beginner may benefit from a short interval because it provides faster feedback and more practice repetitions. A target that is too easy offers little growth, while one that is too difficult can undermine self-efficacy. The coach's role is therefore to balance challenge, learning opportunity, and a realistic chance to experience progress with support.
Study instruction:
- "For two learners with different levels of Improvement Kata experience, propose different achieve-by horizons and explain the coaching rationale."
Key point:
- The target horizon should reflect the learner's capability and learning needs.
- Effective coaching balances stretch with support and attainable progress.
Source links:
35. Q: Why should the achieve-by date be an actual calendar date?
A: Show answer
An actual calendar date makes the target condition specific and reviewable. A phrase such as in two weeks can be interpreted differently over time and may not provide a clear reference for checking progress. A date also helps the learner and coach align the learning cycle, plan review conversations, and recognize when a prediction has or has not been confirmed. The date should be agreed as part of the target condition rather than left implicit. The chapter treats the date as an essential component of the when, not an administrative detail to be added later.
Study instruction:
- "Convert relative deadlines into actual dates and explain how the date will be used in the learner's review and storyboard."
Key point:
- A target condition needs a definite achieve-by date.
- The date anchors the learning cycle and the review of predictions.
Source links:
36. Q: How should the learner review the original challenge before defining the next target condition?
A: Show answer
Before setting the target, the learner should return to the challenge established in the first step of the Improvement Kata and confirm that it is understood. The challenge provides the frame within which the focus-process target is defined. In some situations, a higher-level target condition becomes the challenge for the learner's level. In other situations, the challenge may be informed by a future-state value-stream design and the way the focus process must operate to support it. The review prevents the target condition from becoming a local improvement disconnected from the broader direction.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a proposed target condition back to the challenge or future-state design it is intended to support."
Key point:
- The target condition should be aligned with the challenge direction.
- Local process improvement must support the broader system need.
Source links:
37. Q: How is the Current Condition / Target Condition form used when establishing the next target?
A: Show answer
The learner reuses the Current Condition / Target Condition form developed during the study of the current condition. The current-state characteristics on the left provide a reference for describing the desired characteristics on the right. The learner can decide which aspects should change and which should remain the same. Not every characteristic needs to be altered within the next interval. Once the learner and coach agree on the target condition, the form is separated and each side is placed in its corresponding area of the storyboard. This keeps the current and desired states connected while making the agreed target visible.
Study instruction:
- "Complete both sides of a Current Condition / Target Condition form and mark which characteristics change and which remain stable."
Key point:
- Use the current-state analysis as the basis for defining the future state.
- The form makes continuity and intended changes visible.
Source links:
38. Q: Why does the chapter say that not everything in the process has to change?
A: Show answer
The next target condition is a nearer step toward the challenge, not a complete redesign of every process characteristic. Depending on the achieve-by date and the current condition, only one or two aspects may need to change in the next interval. Other characteristics can remain as they are. The learner may first sketch a more comprehensive desired operating pattern and then scale it back to a realistic next condition. This prevents the target from becoming too broad while preserving a view of the longer-term direction. Selective change makes the next learning cycle more focused and manageable.
Study instruction:
- "Review a current-state form and identify the few characteristics that matter most for the next target date."
Key point:
- A near-term target can change only the characteristics that matter now.
- The broader future picture can be approached through smaller increments.
Source links:
39. Q: How should the desired outcome performance be defined?
A: Show answer
The outcome performance is the measurable result expected by the achieve-by date. Either the learner or coach may propose it, but it should be connected mathematically where possible to the challenge or future-state value-stream design. In the chapter's assembly example, the outcome metric is units produced per day. The learner initially considers the daily requirement of 1,840 pieces across two shifts without overtime, then recognizes that this is too ambitious for a two-week target. The number is therefore a starting point for discussion and refinement, not a figure that should be accepted without testing its feasibility for the interval.
Study instruction:
- "Select an outcome measure, define its unit and time basis, and show how it relates to the challenge."
Key point:
- The outcome should be measurable and linked to the broader need.
- An ambitious initial figure must be refined to suit the next interval.
Source links:
40. Q: Why should an outcome target be connected to the challenge or future-state design?
A: Show answer
A measurable outcome becomes meaningful when it contributes to the broader performance need. The chapter recommends making a mathematical connection where possible between the target outcome and the challenge or future-state value-stream design. This connection helps the learner avoid choosing a local metric that looks attractive but does not advance the system direction. It also gives the coach and learner a basis for checking whether the target is relevant. The connection need not mean that every future outcome is fully predictable; it means that the proposed measure should have a clear relationship to the challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the causal or quantitative link between a process outcome and the larger challenge it is meant to support."
Key point:
- A target outcome should advance the challenge, not merely improve an isolated metric.
- A clear connection helps test the relevance of the target.
Source links:
41. Q: How does the learner define the desired operating pattern through dialogue with the coach?
A: Show answer
The learner proposes how the focus process should operate to generate the desired outcome. The coach asks what the process would need to look like and what should change or remain the same. The learner cross-references the characteristics on the current-condition form and writes the corresponding desired characteristics. The proposal is then reviewed, questioned, and refined through back-and-forth discussion until there is consensus. The learner may rewrite the target several times or return to gather more information. This dialogue is worthwhile because the target condition will frame the experiments in the next step.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare questions a coach could ask to help a learner translate an outcome target into a concrete operating pattern."
Key point:
- The operating pattern is developed through iterative learner-coach dialogue.
- Revision and renewed observation are normal parts of reaching a useful target.
Source links:
42. Q: What does the assembly example teach about translating process analysis into a target condition?
A: Show answer
The assembly example shows how the learner uses process-analysis information to select a near-term bottleneck-related target. The broader outcome is to produce 1,840 units per day with the same personnel and equipment and without overtime. The analysis shows a tank-welder cycle of 27 seconds against a planned cycle time of 25 seconds, indicating insufficient capacity. The learner identifies manual unloading and loading as a significant part of the cycle and predicts that a cycle of 22 seconds or less could remove the bottleneck. The near-term target is then scaled to at least 1,700 units per day with no more than plus 25 percent variation at tank welding.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the assembly example from outcome requirement to bottleneck evidence and then to the narrower two-week target condition."
Key point:
- Process analysis helps identify which characteristic should be addressed first.
- A nearer target can be smaller than the ultimate performance requirement.
Source links:
43. Q: Why did the learner focus on the tank-welder cycle rather than attempting every desired process change at once?
A: Show answer
The learner recognized that the current 27-second tank-welder cycle did not support the planned 25-second cycle time. That capacity bottleneck was a limiting condition, so addressing it first was a reasoned way to move toward the desired operating pattern. The chapter does not suggest changing every process feature simultaneously. It illustrates selecting a specific characteristic that appears to constrain the outcome and defining a near-term condition around it. The learner's analysis of the machine-capacity chart also revealed that manual unload-and-load time offered a more promising area to investigate than the welding time itself.
Study instruction:
- "Use a process-capacity analysis to identify one limiting characteristic and explain why it should be prioritized over less relevant changes."
Key point:
- Choose a target characteristic based on evidence about the focus process.
- A bottleneck can provide a logical starting point for the next learning cycle.
Source links:
44. Q: How should the learner handle a target condition that is initially too ambitious for the proposed date?
A: Show answer
The learner should treat the broad desired state as a starting picture, then refine it to a nearer condition that can be meaningfully pursued within the agreed horizon. In the assembly example, the initial 1,840-unit outcome is too ambitious for two weeks. The learner studies the capacity information and proposes a more limited target centered on the tank-welder cycle, daily output of at least 1,700 units, and a variation limit. This does not abandon the challenge. It creates a smaller step toward it. The coach helps the learner narrow the target without replacing it with an easy task that requires no learning.
Study instruction:
- "Take an ambitious process goal and derive a nearer target that still advances the challenge and creates a genuine learning need."
Key point:
- Scale the next target to the learning interval without losing the challenge direction.
- A smaller step should remain meaningful, not merely comfortable.
Source links:
45. Q: When should the learner return to observe the process before finalizing the target condition?
A: Show answer
The learner should return to the focus process when an important operating-pattern detail is uncertain and the missing knowledge is needed to describe a useful target. The chapter's tank-welder example identifies two possible paths: study and analyze the manual unload-and-load work further during the target-condition step, or proceed to the next step and add detail as learning develops. The appropriate choice depends on whether the basic target is already clear enough to guide useful experimentation. Returning to observation is preferable to inventing a process description that has not been grounded in reality.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a target detail that is still based on assumption and decide what direct observation would be needed to clarify it."
Key point:
- Return to the process when missing facts matter to the target description.
- Observation is preferable to filling knowledge gaps with guesses.
Source links:
46. Q: What is the obstacles parking lot?
A: Show answer
The obstacles parking lot is a simple list on the storyboard where the learner records perceived and discovered obstacles that may prevent movement toward the target condition. It is not a detailed action plan and it does not imply that every listed item must be addressed immediately. Its purpose is to acknowledge potential barriers, hold them visibly, and help the learner avoid trying to work on many obstacles simultaneously. As the learner learns more, some entries may be removed because they prove not to be problems, while new obstacles may be added. The list therefore reflects evolving understanding rather than a fixed diagnosis.
Study instruction:
- "Create an obstacles parking lot for a target condition and label each entry as perceived, observed, or newly discovered."
Key point:
- The parking lot records obstacles that may stand between current and target conditions.
- Its contents can change as evidence improves understanding.
Source links:
47. Q: Why should the obstacles parking lot not become an action-item list?
A: Show answer
An action-item list records tasks, owners, and due dates. The obstacles parking lot serves a different purpose: it records barriers and uncertainties that may need investigation. Turning it into an action list encourages premature commitment to activities before the learner understands which obstacle is most important or whether the item is actually an obstacle. The chapter cautions that working on several obstacles simultaneously can be unscientific. The parking lot helps hold possible obstacles without immediately converting every entry into work. Countermeasures and experiments are developed later in the Improvement Kata process as the learner focuses on what is in the way.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an obstacles parking lot with an action tracker and identify the different information and decisions each should contain."
Key point:
- An obstacle is not the same thing as a task to complete.
- Do not assign action plans before understanding which obstacle matters.
Source links:
48. Q: What are the two main purposes of the obstacles parking lot?
A: Show answer
First, it provides a holding place that acknowledges possible obstacles and prevents the learner from attempting to work on several at once. This supports a more focused and scientific approach. Second, it helps reveal how imperfect initial perceptions and predictions can be. Some obstacles are overcome, some suspected obstacles turn out not to be problems, and new obstacles emerge only after work begins. Maintaining the list makes those changes visible. It is therefore both a focus mechanism and a record of how the learner's understanding of the process evolves during the improvement effort.
Study instruction:
- "For a sample improvement effort, identify how the parking lot could prevent scattered effort and how its changes could document learning."
Key point:
- The parking lot supports focus and makes changing assumptions visible.
- An obstacle list is a learning record, not merely a backlog.
Source links:
49. Q: How can a learner distinguish a real obstacle from the absence of a preferred solution?
A: Show answer
A real obstacle describes a condition that currently prevents movement toward the target. The absence of a solution is not itself an obstacle; it is often a preconceived countermeasure stated in reverse. For example, saying that the process cannot improve because a particular device has not been installed assumes that the device is the answer. A more useful obstacle statement identifies what is actually unknown or preventing progress, such as not understanding the unload-and-load sequence. The learner should describe the barrier without embedding an untested solution, leaving room for other ways to overcome it.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite obstacle statements that name missing equipment, training, or proposed actions into neutral descriptions of the condition that blocks progress."
Key point:
- Describe the barrier, not the countermeasure you happen to prefer.
- A neutral obstacle statement preserves alternative ways of learning and responding.
Source links:
50. Q: Why must obstacles be stated specifically rather than vaguely?
A: Show answer
Vague obstacle statements can indicate that the learner does not yet understand the focus process well enough. The chapter advises returning to observe the process rather than inventing obstacles when specificity is missing. A useful obstacle statement identifies the concrete reason movement is currently blocked. The prompt suggested in the chapter is: 'We currently can't move closer to the target condition because...' Completing this sentence with a specific and measurable condition helps the learner distinguish facts from general frustration. Specificity also makes later investigation more focused because the learner knows what must be understood or tested.
Study instruction:
- "Take three vague obstacles and rewrite them using the sentence stem about why the process cannot move closer to the target condition."
Key point:
- Specific obstacle statements expose what is actually blocking progress.
- Vagueness is a signal to observe and understand the process more deeply.
Source links:
51. Q: How should perceived obstacles and discovered obstacles be managed over time?
A: Show answer
The learner should record initial perceptions without assuming that they are all correct. As the process is studied and experiments proceed, new obstacles may become visible, and some early concerns may prove irrelevant. Obstacles that have been overcome can be crossed off, while mistaken assumptions can be removed and new findings added. This makes the parking lot a dynamic representation of current understanding. The list should not be treated as proof that every entry is real or equally important. Its value comes from revisiting entries against evidence and allowing the understanding of the process to change.
Study instruction:
- "Simulate a review of an obstacles parking lot and identify which entries should be retained, clarified, crossed off, or newly added based on evidence."
Key point:
- Treat obstacle entries as hypotheses to be checked, not established facts.
- Update the list as the process reveals more information.
Source links:
52. Q: How does the obstacles parking lot help prevent the team from working on too many issues at once?
A: Show answer
By recording possible obstacles in one visible place, the parking lot allows the learner to acknowledge concerns without immediately acting on all of them. The learner can then focus on the obstacle most relevant to the next learning step rather than dispersing effort across a collection of unrelated tasks. This is important because simultaneous action on many obstacles can make it difficult to understand what caused a result. Focused experimentation makes cause-and-effect learning more visible. The parking lot preserves other concerns for later consideration while keeping the current work directed toward the target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one obstacle from a larger list and explain why focusing on it first would make learning more interpretable."
Key point:
- Visible parking of other concerns protects focus on the current obstacle.
- Concentrated effort makes learning from experiments clearer.
Source links:
53. Q: What does the tank-welder example illustrate about acknowledging a lack of knowledge as an obstacle?
A: Show answer
When asked what might prevent movement toward the target condition, the learner says that the main obstacle is not knowing much about the tank-welder unload-and-load process. The coach accepts this as a valid initial entry. The learner does not invent a detailed barrier or claim to know which countermeasure is needed. This illustrates that a knowledge gap can itself be a useful obstacle statement when it is specific to the process and the target. Acknowledging what is not known gives the next learning step a clear direction: observe and understand the work rather than guess at a solution.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a process-related knowledge gap and write it as a specific obstacle rather than as a request for a preferred solution."
Key point:
- A clearly identified knowledge gap can be a legitimate obstacle.
- Admitting uncertainty is more useful than pretending to know the cause.
Source links:
54. Q: How do target conditions and obstacles work together in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The target condition defines the desired process state, while the obstacles parking lot records what may prevent the learner from reaching it. The target gives the work direction; the obstacles help identify where learning and experimentation may be needed. Without a target, an obstacle list can become a collection of disconnected complaints. Without attention to obstacles, the target may remain only an aspiration. The chapter positions the parking lot as the bridge from defining the future condition to beginning the next step of the Improvement Kata. The learner uses the target to judge which obstacles matter for moving closer.
Study instruction:
- "For one process, write a target condition and list only obstacles that plausibly prevent movement toward that condition."
Key point:
- The target condition defines the destination; obstacles explain what stands in the way.
- The relationship between them keeps improvement work coherent.
Source links:
55. Q: How can a team tell whether a target condition is too broad for its achieve-by date?
A: Show answer
A target may be too broad when it includes many process characteristics that cannot be understood or changed meaningfully within the proposed interval. The chapter describes first sketching a broader desired operating pattern and then narrowing it to suit the date. In the assembly example, the overall production requirement is not accepted as the immediate two-week condition; the learner selects a bottleneck-related step and a nearer performance level. A practical review asks whether the target has a clear outcome, a defined operating pattern, and a realistic learning horizon. If not, it should be refined into a smaller target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed target against its date and identify which characteristics should be retained for the next interval and which belong to later stages."
Key point:
- Scope should match the learning horizon.
- A broad future picture can be divided into smaller target conditions.
Source links:
56. Q: What are common errors when writing a target condition?
A: Show answer
Common errors include writing an outcome metric without describing the operating pattern, using action verbs instead of describing the future state, embedding solutions in the target, guessing at process details beyond current knowledge, setting the date too far away, and choosing a target that is either too easy or too ambitious. Another error is selecting between familiar alternatives rather than defining a new condition that advances the challenge. These mistakes weaken the target as a frame for learning. A useful review checks whether the target is observable, time-bounded, linked to the challenge, sufficiently detailed, and honest about what remains unknown.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a draft target condition against the errors in this answer and rewrite any statement that describes an action or unsupported assumption."
Key point:
- A target must describe a future process state, not a solution or slogan.
- Date, stretch, evidence, and operating detail all affect target quality.
Source links:
57. Q: How can a coach respond when a learner is unable to describe an unfamiliar future condition?
A: Show answer
The coach should recognize that describing a condition beyond current knowledge can be difficult, especially for a beginner. The learner may ask how to describe a future state without knowing how to reach it. The chapter advises the coach to expect iteration and to work back and forth with the learner rather than demanding a perfect first draft. Questions about what the process should look like, what should change, and what should remain the same can help the learner develop a clearer description. The coach should support the learner's thinking and confidence while preserving the distinction between the destination and the route.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare coaching questions that help a learner describe a desired state without telling the learner which countermeasure to use."
Key point:
- An incomplete first proposal is normal in learning to define target conditions.
- Coaching should help the learner think through the future state.
Source links:
58. Q: How can the learner know whether the target condition is sufficiently defined to move into the next step?
A: Show answer
The target is sufficiently defined when the learner and coach have agreed on a meaningful near-term condition, including an achieve-by date, a measurable outcome, and a useful description of the desired operating pattern to the extent currently understood. Every detail does not have to be known. The learner should be able to explain why the target advances the challenge and identify initial obstacles that may prevent progress. If important details remain uncertain, the learner can either study the process further or plan to add detail as experiments proceed. The decision is about having a useful frame for learning, not perfect certainty.
Study instruction:
- "Use the three target elements and the alignment to the challenge to assess whether a draft is ready for the next step."
Key point:
- Move forward with a useful, agreed frame for experimentation.
- Completeness means sufficient clarity, not total knowledge.

59. Q: What should happen to the Current Condition / Target Condition form after agreement?
A: Show answer
After the learner and coach agree on the next target condition, the form is separated along the indicated vertical line. The current-condition side and target-condition side are placed in their corresponding fields on the storyboard. This makes the two states visible as separate references while preserving the fact that the target was developed by comparing it with the current condition. The storyboard then supports the next stage of work by keeping the agreed destination and the starting point accessible. The separation should occur after agreement, not before the learner and coach have settled on the next condition.
Study instruction:
- "Review a storyboard and confirm that the current and target sides of the form are placed in the appropriate fields after agreement."
Key point:
- The form becomes a visible reference for the next learning cycle.
- Separate the two sides after the target condition has been agreed.

60. Q: How does establishing a target condition contribute to learner self-efficacy?
A: Show answer
The chapter links self-efficacy to experiencing a meaningful challenge and periodically discovering that progress is possible. A target that is too easy provides little new sense of capability because the learner already knows how to achieve it. A target that is too difficult can reinforce a belief that success was impossible. A well-calibrated target is challenging and may feel uncomfortable, while the coach provides frequent corrective feedback and support. When the learner reaches a condition that initially seemed beyond reach, the experience can strengthen confidence in the ability to learn and improve through practice.
Study instruction:
- "Reflect on a challenging learning experience and identify how the level of difficulty and quality of coaching affected confidence."
Key point:
- Self-efficacy grows through supported achievement of meaningful challenges.
- Target difficulty should be calibrated, not simply maximized.

61. Q: How can a target condition be applied in service, manufacturing, and other non-manufacturing processes?
A: Show answer
The operating pattern depends on the nature of the focus process. In a service organization, it may describe the customer experience and the sequence of interactions. In a bakery, it may specify how customers are greeted, how products are displayed, and how service is provided. In manufacturing, it may describe work sequence, timing, work-in-process buffers, and quality checks. The chapter also uses examples from sport and teaching to show that a target condition can combine an outcome with a repeatable operating pattern. The method is not limited to factories; it applies wherever a process produces an outcome.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a service or knowledge-work process and define an outcome together with the observable operating pattern expected to produce it."
Key point:
- Target conditions apply to any process with a desired operating state.
- The details should reflect the actual work, not a manufacturing template.
Source links:
62. Q: How can a target condition support improvement without encouraging unsafe or unrealistic experimentation?
A: Show answer
The target condition sets a desired state but must be developed within parameters such as safety, quality, budget, and other constraints that cannot be changed. The learner and coach should identify these boundaries while defining the operating pattern. A challenging target is not a reason to bypass safeguards or assume that constraints can be ignored. The chapter emphasizes resourcefulness and experimentation within the available constraints. If a proposed condition appears to conflict with a non-negotiable requirement, the conflict should be resolved before agreement and before experiments begin. This keeps the learning effort ambitious while preserving responsible process operation.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a proposed target against safety, quality, and budget boundaries and identify any unresolved conflict that must be addressed."
Key point:
- A target condition must remain within non-negotiable process constraints.
- Creativity is required within constraints, not by ignoring them.
Source links:
63. Q: What is the relationship between the target condition and the next step of the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The target condition is the frame for the experimenting that follows. Once the learner and coach agree on the desired future state and record initial obstacles, the learner is ready to begin working toward the condition through the next step of the Improvement Kata. The target identifies what the experiments are intended to move the process toward, while the obstacle list identifies possible barriers to investigate. The learner should not confuse the definition of the target with the execution of countermeasures. Establishing the destination is necessary, but the subsequent learning process is where the learner discovers how to move toward it.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the target condition and obstacles parking lot will guide the first experiment in the next step."
Key point:
- The target condition gives experiments a defined direction.
- Countermeasures are developed through the subsequent learning process.
Source links:
64. Q: What should a learner take away from Chapter 7 about disciplined improvement?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is to define the next desired process condition carefully while accepting that the route to it is not yet fully known. The learner should connect the target to the challenge, specify an actual achieve-by date, define a measurable outcome, and describe the operating pattern as far as current knowledge allows. The target should be a genuine stretch, not a solution list or an easy rearrangement of familiar work. Once agreed, it remains stable while evidence-based detail may be added. The obstacles parking lot records possible barriers without becoming an action list. Together, these practices create a focused basis for experimentation and continued learning.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter using the three target elements, the rule about solutions, the fixed-date principle, and the two purposes of the obstacles parking lot."
Key point:
- Define a clear next condition and learn the route through practice.
- Focus, honesty about knowledge, and disciplined obstacle handling support improvement.
