Table 7
Experimenting Toward the Target Condition: Scientific Learning and the Experimenting Record
1. Q: How should you experiment toward the target condition in Step 4 of the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
Treat the route to the target condition as uncertain and build it through a sequence of deliberate experiments. Start from the latest understanding of the current condition, identify a specific obstacle, decide what you need to learn at your present threshold of knowledge, and take one testable step. Before acting, record a prediction; after acting, collect evidence, compare what happened with what was expected, and evaluate what the difference means. Use that learning to design the next step. The target condition and its achieve-by date provide direction, but the path may change as the process reveals information that was not visible in advance. The aim is both to make progress and to learn how the focus process actually behaves.
Study instruction:
- Explain the sequence obstacle → prediction → test → data → evaluation → next experiment, and apply it to one real improvement challenge.
Key point:
- Step 4 builds the route through evidence rather than a fully predetermined plan.
- Every experiment should produce learning that informs the next step.
Source links:
2. Q: Why can the path to a target condition not be fully determined in advance?
A: Show answer
A workplace process contains variables, dependencies, constraints, and interactions that may not be visible from initial analysis or discussion. Even experienced people cannot reliably anticipate every response of a particular process. A plan based only on logic, debate, benchmarking, or assumptions may therefore overlook local conditions. As experiments are conducted, the process produces evidence that may reveal hidden obstacles, invalidate assumptions, or suggest a different route. This does not mean planning is useless; it means a plan should guide learning without being treated as a complete prediction of the future. Keep the target condition clear while allowing the sequence of steps to adapt to observed results.
Study instruction:
- List the unknown variables in a process you want to improve and identify which ones require direct testing rather than further debate.
Key point:
- The target can remain stable while the path changes.
- Local evidence is more reliable than assuming a solution will transfer unchanged from another setting.
Source links:
3. Q: What is an experiment in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
An experiment is a step taken with the intent of learning something. It may involve observing a process, introducing a provisional change, or testing a specific countermeasure. The defining feature is not whether the step succeeds or produces an immediate performance gain; it is whether the learner has a question, makes an expectation explicit, and examines what the process reveals. In this chapter, the terms step and experiment are used interchangeably. A routine action becomes a learning experiment when the learner deliberately states what is being tested or learned and records evidence that can inform the next decision.
Study instruction:
- Take three routine improvement actions and rewrite each as an experiment by specifying the learning question and observable evidence.
Key point:
- An experiment is defined by the intent to learn, not by guaranteed success.
- A step without reflection loses much of its learning value.
Source links:
4. Q: What does it mean to treat an experiment as a conversation with the situation?
A: Show answer
The learner proposes a step and the focus process responds through observable consequences. The response may confirm an expectation, expose a new obstacle, or reveal that the learner misunderstood how the process works. The process is not literally speaking; its behavior supplies information that discussion alone cannot provide. This interaction resembles using a flashlight in darkness: a person sees only a limited area, takes a step, and then gains a clearer view of what lies ahead. Each experiment extends the learner's view and improves the basis for the next decision. The learner must therefore pay attention to the response rather than merely defend the original idea.
Study instruction:
- Describe a process response that changed your understanding and identify the next question that response created.
Key point:
- The process provides evidence that cannot be obtained from opinion alone.
- Each step can reveal information that changes the next step.
Source links:
5. Q: How should the target condition and the experimental path relate to one another?
A: Show answer
The target condition provides the intended future state and the basis for judging whether progress is being made. The experimental path is the evolving sequence of steps used to move toward it. Keep the target condition visible and sufficiently specific, but do not confuse commitment to the target with attachment to one proposed solution. Evidence may require changing the order, design, or type of experiments without abandoning the target. If the target itself must change because of a legitimate change in conditions, that is a separate management decision and should be made transparently rather than disguised as an experimental adjustment.
Study instruction:
- Review an improvement plan and mark which elements describe the required target condition and which are merely proposed steps.
Key point:
- Stay committed to the target while remaining flexible about the route.
- Do not turn an early solution idea into an unquestionable plan.
Source links:
6. Q: What is the threshold of knowledge in an improvement effort?
A: Show answer
The threshold of knowledge is the point at which available facts and data end and guessing or extrapolation begins. It marks the current boundary between what the learner can support with evidence and what the learner merely suspects. It is not a failure or a sign that the team lacks intelligence; it is the natural edge of current understanding. Recognizing this boundary helps identify where the next experiment should be conducted. Rather than continuing to argue about what may be beyond the boundary, the learner should design a small, useful test that produces new information.
Study instruction:
- For a current process problem, write down what is known, what is inferred, and the first question that lies beyond the available evidence.
Key point:
- The threshold of knowledge identifies the current learning edge.
- The next experiment should be designed at that edge.
Source links:
7. Q: What signs indicate that a team may have reached or crossed its threshold of knowledge?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include relying on phrases such as “I think,” “probably,” “maybe,” “most likely,” or “on average” without supporting data; proposing a large percentage change as a guess; relying on old data that may no longer represent the current process; using anecdotes as if they establish a general pattern; and arguing that something must be true without testing it. These expressions are not automatically wrong, but they should prompt the team to ask what evidence supports the statement and what remains uncertain. The aim is to distinguish measured knowledge from a plausible but unverified explanation.
Study instruction:
- Review a recent improvement discussion and highlight assumptions, estimates, anecdotes, and claims based on current measured facts.
Key point:
- Confident language does not turn an assumption into evidence.
- Recognizing uncertainty is the first step toward a useful experiment.
Source links:
8. Q: What should a learner do after acknowledging a threshold of knowledge?
A: Show answer
First, state clearly what is not yet known and resist jumping immediately to a solution. Next, identify the smallest experiment that could provide relevant information at that point. Decide what to observe or change, what result is expected, and how the result will be measured. Conduct the experiment promptly, then use the evidence to extend understanding. The learner should not try to eliminate all uncertainty through more conference-room debate. Discussion is useful for designing a test, but the actual process response is needed to see farther. Repeating this behavior develops the habit of working at the edge of current knowledge rather than concealing uncertainty with speculation.
Study instruction:
- Use a real obstacle to prepare a one-page experiment proposal that states the knowledge gap, test, prediction, measure, and safety boundary.
Key point:
- Acknowledging uncertainty should lead to a testable learning question.
- Experimentation replaces unsupported debate with evidence gathering.
Source links:
9. Q: Why is experimenting at the knowledge threshold more useful than debating competing opinions?
A: Show answer
When people debate beyond the available evidence, the discussion can reward confidence, status, or persuasive argument rather than accuracy. Participants may become more committed to their preferred explanation without learning anything new about the process. A well-designed experiment changes the question from “Whose idea is best?” to “What test would help us learn?” The team can then compare an explicit prediction with observed results. Debate still has a role in selecting a safe and informative test, but it should not substitute for the test itself. Decisions become more grounded as the team repeatedly updates its understanding from evidence.
Study instruction:
- Convert a disagreement about a process cause into one experiment that would distinguish between the competing explanations.
Key point:
- The purpose of discussion is to design learning, not to win an argument.
- Process evidence should guide the next decision.
Source links:
10. Q: What is the scientific learning cycle used in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
The cycle is prediction → test → data → evaluate. The learner first predicts what will happen if a proposed step is taken, then performs the test, collects evidence about what actually happened, and evaluates the difference between expectation and result. The evaluation informs the next prediction and experiment. This cycle is repeated to build a progressively more accurate understanding of the focus process. It is a practical way to work through the uncertain area between the current condition and the target condition. The chapter notes that this pattern is also commonly associated with Plan-Do-Check-Act or Plan-Do-Study-Adjust, while favoring plain language that makes the learning sequence clear.
Study instruction:
- Draw the four stages and apply each to a short process experiment, showing how the evaluation leads to the next prediction.
Key point:
- Prediction, test, data, and evaluation form a repeating learning cycle.
- The cycle combines forward movement with investigation.
Source links:
11. Q: Why should scientific thinking remain provisional during improvement work?
A: Show answer
A provisional view recognizes that current knowledge is incomplete and that a proposed plan is a hypothesis rather than a guaranteed description of reality. This attitude allows the learner to revise an explanation when evidence contradicts it. Without it, a team may defend a plan because it has already invested time, authority, or reputation in the idea. Provisional thinking does not mean indecision or lack of standards. It means making the best current decision, testing it, and being prepared to adjust. The chapter captures this mindset in the reminder that there may be an even better idea behind the current one.
Study instruction:
- Identify one assumption embedded in a current improvement plan and write what evidence would cause you to revise it.
Key point:
- A plan is a testable hypothesis, not a promise that reality will conform to it.
- Willingness to revise is essential to scientific learning.
Source links:
12. Q: Why is experimenting not the same as implementing a predetermined solution?
A: Show answer
Implementation usually begins with a selected solution and focuses on putting it into operation. Experimenting begins with an obstacle and a learning question, then uses a test to discover what should happen next. A proposed countermeasure may be tested, but it is not assumed to be correct merely because it appears logical or has worked elsewhere. The learner may need several experiments before a change becomes suitable for broader use. This distinction prevents the team from installing an idea, moving on immediately, and missing the information contained in the result. The objective is to build the route link by link through learning rather than execute a fixed sequence regardless of evidence.
Study instruction:
- Compare a solution implementation plan with an experiment plan and identify where prediction, measurement, and evaluation occur in each.
Key point:
- Experimenting is primarily a learning process, not simply a rollout activity.
- Do not move on before reflecting on what the step revealed.
Source links:
13. Q: Why should learners expect the path to the target condition to be nonlinear?
A: Show answer
Each experiment can reveal new variables, constraints, and obstacles that change the learner's understanding. A step may show that a proposed countermeasure is ineffective, that a different obstacle is more important, or that the process behaves differently under particular conditions. The next step may therefore differ from the original plan. A nonlinear path is not evidence of poor discipline when the target remains clear and each change is supported by learning. It is the expected result of navigating a process whose full behavior was not known at the outset. The experimental procedure remains structured even though the route it reveals is not straight.
Study instruction:
- Map an improvement effort in which the original route changed and annotate the evidence that justified each change.
Key point:
- A structured method can produce an adaptive, nonlinear path.
- Changes in direction should be explained by learning rather than convenience or impulse.
Source links:
14. Q: Must every experiment produce an immediate measurable performance improvement?
A: Show answer
No. Some experiments provide information without producing a direct performance gain, and some may temporarily worsen a measure or disprove a promising idea. The benefit of an individual step is not the sole test of its value. Progress toward the target condition comes from the accumulated sequence of learning and effective changes. An experiment that reveals why a proposed countermeasure fails may prevent a larger unsuccessful implementation later. However, experiments still consume time and resources, so the learner should ensure that each one addresses a relevant obstacle and produces evidence useful for deciding what to do next.
Study instruction:
- Identify an experiment that did not improve the process immediately but changed the team's understanding or prevented an unsuitable action.
Key point:
- Learning value and immediate performance gain are not identical.
- Each experiment should still be purposeful and proportionate to its cost.
Source links:
15. Q: What boundaries define the experimenting zone?
A: Show answer
The experimenting zone is bounded by three conditions: a measurable and non-optional target condition with a firm achieve-by date, budget constraints that must not be exceeded, and the requirement not to compromise safety or quality. These boundaries distinguish disciplined experimentation from uncontrolled trial and error. The team should pursue learning quickly, but not by ignoring the target, spending without limits, or exposing customers and employees to unacceptable risks. A proposed experiment should be assessed against all three boundaries before it is run. If a test cannot be conducted safely within them, redesign it, reduce its scale, create a buffer, or use a simulation.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed experiment, document the target and deadline, available budget, quality requirements, and safety controls before approval.
Key point:
- Experimentation takes place within explicit performance, cost, safety, and quality limits.
- Learning speed never justifies uncontrolled risk or breach of constraints.
Source links:
16. Q: Why is prediction error or surprise an important source of learning?
A: Show answer
Prediction error is the difference between what the learner expected and what actually occurred. When the result differs from the prediction, it can expose an assumption that was incomplete or incorrect and provide information that was not previously available. A result that matches the prediction is useful confirmation, but an unexpected result may reveal more about the process. The learner should therefore avoid treating surprise automatically as failure. It is a signal to investigate what happened, update the explanation, and design the next test. The chapter emphasizes that experiments should be arranged so that unexpected outcomes can be learned from without causing harm.
Study instruction:
- Write a prediction for a small process test and identify in advance what different outcomes would teach you.
Key point:
- The gap between expectation and observation is a major learning opportunity.
- Useful surprise must be managed within safe experimental limits.
Source links:
17. Q: Why can an experiment that refutes a hypothesis be more valuable than one that confirms it?
A: Show answer
A refuting result challenges the assumptions behind the proposed idea and can reveal a previously hidden feature of the process. It may show that a suspected cause is not responsible, that a countermeasure has an unintended effect, or that another variable matters more. This information helps the learner avoid investing further effort in an unsuitable solution. Confirmation is still useful because it adds evidence, but it may produce less new learning when it simply matches what the team already expected. The learner should therefore design tests to discover what is true, not merely to collect support for a favored proposal.
Study instruction:
- Take a proposed countermeasure and write one possible result that would refute it, then state how you would respond to that evidence.
Key point:
- A negative result can be a productive discovery.
- Experiments should challenge assumptions as well as test solutions.
Source links:
18. Q: Why must a prediction be written down before conducting an experiment?
A: Show answer
Recording the prediction in advance preserves what the learner actually expected before seeing the result. Without a prior record, people can unconsciously reinterpret their expectations after the event and claim that the outcome was what they anticipated. A written prediction makes comparison more objective and creates two learning opportunities: testing the specific “if we do X, then Y will happen” statement and examining the assumptions about the focus process that led to it. The prediction should be specific enough to compare with observable evidence. It should be recorded on the prediction side of the experimenting record before the test begins.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a vague expectation such as “the process should improve” into a measurable prediction with a defined observation period.
Key point:
- Record expectations before exposure to results to reduce hindsight distortion.
- A useful prediction specifies what is expected and how it can be checked.
Source links:
19. Q: How do quick and frequent experiments accelerate progress toward the target condition?
A: Show answer
A process cannot be understood beyond the current evidence until a test produces more information. Testing sooner therefore allows the learner to see farther sooner and design the next step earlier. Frequent experiments create more learning cycles within the available time, while small steps can reduce the fear and cost associated with trying a change. This is particularly important when the target condition has a firm achieve-by date. If daily coaching cycles are already in place but experiments are repeatedly delayed, the response should generally be to improve the frequency and readiness of experiments rather than reduce the value of coaching. The objective is a steady flow of learning, not activity for its own sake.
Study instruction:
- Review the elapsed time between recent experiments and identify avoidable waiting, preparation delays, or approval bottlenecks.
Key point:
- Faster learning cycles provide more opportunities to adjust before the deadline.
- Small experiments can be quick without being careless.
Source links:
20. Q: Why are small steps not necessarily slow steps?
A: Show answer
A small step can be designed, executed, and evaluated quickly, allowing the learner to make several learning cycles in the time that a large rollout would require. Small experiments also limit the consequences of a mistaken assumption and make it easier to adjust the idea before expanding it. Their cumulative effect can be substantial when they are frequent and directed toward a challenging target condition. The risk is not smallness but taking too few steps, failing to learn from them, or treating each provisional action as the final solution. Small steps should therefore be frequent, measurable, and connected to a specific obstacle.
Study instruction:
- Break a large proposed change into a sequence of smaller tests and estimate how each could be evaluated before the next step.
Key point:
- Small, rapid experiments can build substantial progress through accumulation.
- The learning cadence matters more than the apparent size of an individual step.
Source links:
21. Q: What does “hold before tape before weld” illustrate about experiment design?
A: Show answer
The phrase illustrates the preference for a provisional, easily reversible test before making a more permanent change. In a physical process, holding a component temporarily may allow a team to check fit before taping it, and taping may allow further evaluation before welding. The same logic applies outside manufacturing: use a mock-up, prototype, temporary arrangement, or limited trial before committing to a costly or difficult-to-reverse implementation. The sequence helps the team discover flaws early and adjust the idea while the consequences remain limited. It is not a universal technical instruction; it is a reminder to choose the least committed test that can answer the learning question.
Study instruction:
- Identify a permanent change in your work area and design a reversible prototype that can test its critical assumption first.
Key point:
- Use provisional tests before irreversible commitments whenever practical.
- Early adjustment is usually less costly than correcting a failed full-scale change.
Source links:
22. Q: Why should experiments be directed at one specific obstacle?
A: Show answer
Selecting a specific obstacle keeps the learning effort focused and prevents random solution searching. When an experiment is linked to a named obstacle, the learner can explain why the step is being taken, what needs to be learned, and whether the evidence changes the understanding of that obstacle. It also discourages implementing an idea and immediately moving on without reflection. The learner records the selected obstacle from the Obstacles Parking Lot on an experimenting record and works against it through a series of tests. Other obstacles remain visible in the parking lot until they are selected or become irrelevant.
Study instruction:
- Choose one obstacle from a current parking lot and write a concise statement of the obstacle and the knowledge needed to address it.
Key point:
- Experiments should be connected to a specific barrier to the target condition.
- Focus prevents scattered activity and weak learning.
Source links:
23. Q: How should a beginner choose the first obstacle to experiment against?
A: Show answer
The learner may choose any relevant obstacle to get started, because the sequence of experiments will reveal more about which obstacles matter. For beginners, the chapter recommends avoiding the biggest or most difficult obstacle initially when a more manageable one can be used to develop basic skill with the experimenting routine. The first choice is therefore a practical starting point, not a declaration that the selected obstacle is the most important. The learner should still ensure that it is genuinely connected to the target condition and that a safe, informative experiment can be designed for it.
Study instruction:
- Compare the obstacles in a parking lot and select a manageable first obstacle that offers a clear opportunity to practice prediction and evaluation.
Key point:
- Begin with a useful obstacle that supports learning the routine.
- The first obstacle selected need not be the largest or most difficult.
Source links:
24. Q: Why may several experiments be needed to overcome one obstacle sustainably?
A: Show answer
An obstacle often reflects more than one unknown or one simple cause. The first experiment may reveal the process details, the next may test a possible countermeasure, and a later experiment may show what must change to make the improvement reliable. This chain allows the learner to deepen understanding and refine the response rather than assume that one successful trial is sufficient. The chapter describes this as camping on an obstacle: stay with it long enough to learn what is required to eliminate it and advance toward the target condition. Move to a different obstacle when appropriate, but start a new experimenting record for that obstacle.
Study instruction:
- Trace a sequence of experiments against one obstacle and explain how each result shaped the next test.
Key point:
- Sustainable obstacle removal often requires a chain of experiments.
- One promising result does not automatically establish a stable solution.
Source links:
25. Q: How should the Obstacles Parking Lot be used during experimentation?
A: Show answer
The Obstacles Parking Lot keeps visible the barriers that currently stand between the process and the target condition. The learner indicates which obstacle is being addressed by moving an arrow or other marker to it and transfers that obstacle to the experimenting record. Experiments may reveal previously unnoticed obstacles, eliminate existing ones, or show that an obstacle is no longer relevant. The parking lot should therefore be updated as learning occurs. Even when new obstacles appear, the learner should generally move only one obstacle at a time onto an experimenting record so that the active learning effort remains focused.
Study instruction:
- Update a sample parking lot after a hypothetical experiment that reveals one new obstacle and removes another from relevance.
Key point:
- The parking lot is a living view of obstacles, not a fixed checklist.
- Keep the active experimenting record focused on one obstacle at a time.
Source links:
26. Q: Who owns the experimenting record and what is its purpose?
A: Show answer
The learner owns the experimenting record. It is the learner's tool for communicating reflection on the last experiment and the proposed plan for the next one. The learner completes the entries before the coaching cycle so the coach can see how the learner is thinking, what was learned, and how the next step was designed. The record is not merely a form completed for management reporting; it is a thinking aid that makes the scientific learning pattern visible and supports feedback. Ownership by the learner reinforces responsibility for the experiment and for explaining the evidence and reasoning behind the next step.
Study instruction:
- Review an experimenting record and identify which entries show the learner's own reflection and which describe the next proposed test.
Key point:
- The learner owns and prepares the record before the coaching cycle.
- The record communicates both learning from the last step and design of the next step.
Source links:
27. Q: How does the experimenting record support the Coaching Kata?
A: Show answer
The record provides a structured place for the learner to present the reflection and next-step proposal that the coach explores through the Coaching Kata questions. Its four columns correspond in order with several of those questions. During the coaching cycle, the learner points to and reads the relevant entries, making the current thinking visible rather than relying on an unstructured verbal update. The coach can then provide feedback on the learner's interpretation of the previous experiment and the design of the proposed next experiment. The record and coaching questions work together to teach systematic experimentation and develop the learner's scientific thinking capability.
Study instruction:
- Pair each column of a completed experimenting record with the coaching question it helps answer and explain how the coach can use it.
Key point:
- The record and Coaching Kata are complementary learning routines.
- Coaching feedback should address reflection and the next experiment's design.
Source links:
28. Q: When should the learner complete the experimenting record and what may happen during coaching?
A: Show answer
The learner should enter the relevant information before the coaching cycle so the coach can review the learner's reasoning. During the cycle, the coach may agree with the interpretation and proposed experiment or suggest changes to either. The learner should be prepared to revise the record during the discussion; the chapter notes that entries can be erased, adjusted, or rewritten. This is not a weakness in recordkeeping. It reflects the purpose of coaching: improve the quality of the learner's reflection and experiment design. The learner should bring the record in a form that can be updated and should retain an accurate account of the final agreed proposal.
Study instruction:
- Simulate a coaching cycle in which the coach challenges a prediction and revise the record to show the improved experiment design.
Key point:
- Prepare the record before coaching, then refine it when feedback improves the thinking.
- The record is a working learning document rather than an immutable report.
Source links:
29. Q: How should the experimenting record be read and organized?
A: Show answer
Read the record one row at a time from left to right. Each row represents one experiment or step against the current obstacle. The left side is the prediction side and is completed before the experiment; the right side is the evidence side and is completed after the experiment. This arrangement makes the sequence of expectation and observation visible and reduces the risk of mixing what was predicted with what was later observed. The learner should complete each experiment's row before moving to the next one, maintaining a clear history of how understanding evolved against the obstacle.
Study instruction:
- Take a blank record and label the prediction and evidence sides, then explain when each entry should be completed.
Key point:
- Each row records one experiment from prediction through evidence.
- Keep pre-test expectations separate from post-test observations.
Source links:
30. Q: Why should the learner stop at the “Do a Coaching Cycle” marker on the experimenting record?
A: Show answer
The marker indicates a required check-in point before proceeding. The learner should not pass it without consulting the coach because the coaching cycle is where the learner's reflection and proposed next experiment are examined. This pause provides an opportunity to correct a misunderstanding, improve the next test, or clarify what the evidence means before more action is taken. Treating the marker as optional would weaken the connection between experimenting and coaching and could allow an unsupported interpretation to shape later steps. The marker therefore functions as a routine checkpoint in the learning process.
Study instruction:
- On a sample record, identify each coaching marker and describe what must be reviewed before the learner continues.
Key point:
- The coaching marker is a deliberate review gate.
- Do not advance past it without the coaching cycle specified by the routine.
Source links:
31. Q: How should the first experiment differ from later experiments against the same obstacle?
A: Show answer
The first experiment is based on the learner's understanding of the initial current condition established through process analysis. After that, each experiment should be designed from the latest current condition and the reflection on the preceding experiment. This distinction prevents the learner from continuing to use an outdated picture of the process after a change has been made. The learner should treat new observations as the basis for the next prediction and should not assume that the original analysis remains fully valid. Each experiment changes what is known and may also change the process itself.
Study instruction:
- Compare the information used to design the first experiment with the information needed to design the second experiment after a process change.
Key point:
- The first test begins from the initial current condition.
- Subsequent tests use updated conditions and the learning from the previous experiment.
Source links:
32. Q: What should be updated on the storyboard before each coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should place the updated experimenting record in its corresponding storyboard field, update the Current Condition field to reflect the process as it exists now, and revise the Obstacles Parking Lot when obstacles have been discovered, eliminated, or become irrelevant. The Target Condition field may also be supplemented with further detail. Updating the Current Condition may require new run charts because a change to the process creates a new process condition that should be described with current evidence. During coaching, the learner should point to the relevant storyboard information while answering the coach's questions. The storyboard should therefore represent the latest state, not a historical snapshot.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a before-coaching checklist for the experimenting record, Current Condition, run charts, Obstacles Parking Lot, and Target Condition.
Key point:
- Coaching should use an up-to-date storyboard.
- A changed process requires the current condition and its evidence to be refreshed.
Source links:
33. Q: What is a “go and see” experiment?
A: Show answer
A go-and-see experiment is direct observation and data collection without changing the process. It is useful when the learner does not yet understand how the process works or when the next intervention is unclear. The learner goes to the actual place where the work occurs, observes the process for an appropriate period, and records relevant facts. It can be a genuine experiment when the learner writes a prediction about what will be observed or what is expected to be learned. Observation should be deliberate and sufficiently detailed to distinguish actual process behavior from assumptions or second-hand descriptions.
Study instruction:
- Design a go-and-see observation with a specific learning prediction, observation period, and facts to record.
Key point:
- Go and see gathers direct evidence without changing the process.
- Observation becomes an experiment when the expected learning is made explicit.
Source links:
34. Q: When is a go-and-see experiment preferable to proposing a countermeasure?
A: Show answer
Go and see is preferable when the learner lacks enough direct knowledge to define a useful change or when the process details are unclear. Acting on a poorly understood process may produce a countermeasure aimed at the wrong cause. Direct observation can reveal actual sequence, timing, handoffs, variation, or conditions that were not visible in reports or discussion. It is also useful when the learner needs to verify whether a suspected obstacle exists in practice. Once the process is better understood, the learner can design a more focused exploratory test or hypothesis test. Observation is not an excuse for endless analysis; it should answer a defined question and lead to a next learning step.
Study instruction:
- Identify a proposed countermeasure that rests on assumptions and specify what direct observation should precede it.
Key point:
- Observe first when the process is not sufficiently understood.
- Go-and-see activity should be purposeful and connected to the next experiment.
Source links:
35. Q: What is an exploratory experiment?
A: Show answer
An exploratory experiment introduces a change into a process and observes how the process responds. Its purpose is to probe the situation and reveal features that may otherwise remain hidden, rather than to validate a fully specified countermeasure. For example, a learner may try to run the process as described in the target condition, even while expecting that it will not yet work. The attempt can expose concrete obstacles that need attention. The value lies in the response and the learning it creates. The learner should still make a prediction about what the probe may reveal and ensure that the test is appropriately limited and safe.
Study instruction:
- Design a low-risk exploratory test that would expose a hidden process constraint without requiring a permanent change.
Key point:
- Exploratory experiments probe how the process reacts to a change.
- They help reveal obstacles before a precise countermeasure is known.
Source links:
36. Q: What is a hypothesis-testing experiment?
A: Show answer
A hypothesis-testing experiment introduces a specific change or countermeasure together with a prediction of the result. The test is designed to validate or refute that particular idea. For example, if a team believes that a particular handoff delay is caused by missing information, it may introduce a defined information check and predict how the handoff measure will change. The learner then compares the observed evidence with the prediction. The test should be narrow enough to make the result interpretable and should not be treated as proof beyond the conditions in which it was conducted. Its purpose is to learn whether the proposed relationship holds in the focus process.
Study instruction:
- Write a hypothesis in “If we do X, then Y will happen” form and specify the evidence that would support or refute it.
Key point:
- A hypothesis test links a specific countermeasure to an explicit prediction.
- The result must be evaluated against evidence, not confidence in the idea.
Source links:
37. Q: How do go and see, exploratory experiments, and hypothesis tests differ?
A: Show answer
Go and see gathers information through direct observation without changing the process. An exploratory experiment introduces a change to probe the process and reveal how it responds. A hypothesis test introduces a specific countermeasure to examine a defined prediction. These types form a progression from learning about an unfamiliar situation, to probing its behavior, to testing a more explicit cause-and-effect idea. All can qualify as experiments when they are conducted with an intent to learn and the learner uses the experimenting record to state the expected learning and evaluate the result. The learner should select the type that matches the current knowledge gap rather than choosing the most sophisticated-sounding method.
Study instruction:
- Classify five proposed improvement steps into the three experiment types and justify each classification by its learning purpose.
Key point:
- Choose the experiment type that fits the current question.
- All three types require deliberate learning and evaluation.
Source links:
38. Q: Why should a hypothesis test change only one factor at a time when possible?
A: Show answer
Changing one factor at a time can make cause and effect easier to interpret because fewer variables change between the baseline and the test. If the result differs from the prediction, the learner has a clearer basis for examining the factor that was changed. This is sometimes described as a single-factor or controlled comparison. However, the chapter recognizes that changing only one factor is not always practical and that a hypothesis may involve several factors. When multiple factors must change, the learner should document them and interpret the result cautiously rather than claim that one factor caused the outcome without sufficient evidence.
Study instruction:
- Redesign a multi-change proposal as a single-factor test where feasible, and state the limitations if the factors cannot be separated.
Key point:
- Single-factor tests can improve interpretability of cause and effect.
- Multi-factor experiments may be necessary but require cautious conclusions.
Source links:
39. Q: Why should experiments be conducted as quickly and simply as possible?
A: Show answer
Quick, simple tests shorten the time between a question and the evidence needed to answer it. They allow the learner to revise an idea sooner, make more learning cycles possible before the target date, and avoid spending substantial resources on a solution that has not been tested. A provisional test or mock-up may be sufficient to answer an early question. Simplicity should not remove the measurement or safety needed to make the result useful. The design challenge is to find the smallest practical test that provides credible information while remaining within the constraints of the experimenting zone.
Study instruction:
- Reduce a complex test proposal to the simplest safe version that can still answer the main learning question.
Key point:
- Simple and timely tests accelerate learning.
- Speed must preserve meaningful measurement and safe execution.
Source links:
40. Q: How does small-scale testing limit the risk of unintended consequences?
A: Show answer
A small-scale experiment limits the number of people, products, transactions, or process areas exposed if the idea has an unexpected effect. This reduces the potential “blast radius” while the learner is still discovering flaws and adjusting the design. Where necessary, the team can create a safety buffer to protect the customer of the focus process or test the idea offline in a simulation. A full-scale change made before its assumptions are checked can spread a defect or disruption widely. Scale should therefore increase only as evidence and controls justify it, not simply because the team is confident in the proposed solution.
Study instruction:
- Identify possible unintended effects of a proposed experiment and design a limited pilot, buffer, or offline test to contain them.
Key point:
- Small-scale testing contains the consequences of being wrong.
- Protect the process customer and affected people before expanding a change.
Source links:
41. Q: How should existing knowledge and external examples be used when designing experiments?
A: Show answer
Existing manuals, research, specifications, and experience from other parts of the organization can provide useful starting information and prevent unnecessary reinvention. However, evidence from another setting does not establish that the same idea will work in the learner's specific focus process. Local conditions, people, equipment, timing, and interactions may differ. The learner should therefore use existing knowledge to inform the hypothesis, then test and verify the idea in the actual context of the target condition. External examples can shape the question, but they do not replace local experimentation.
Study instruction:
- Select a borrowed solution or benchmark and list the local assumptions that must be tested before adopting it.
Key point:
- Reuse knowledge intelligently, but verify it in the specific process.
- A successful example elsewhere is not automatic proof of local effectiveness.
Source links:
42. Q: How should a leader calibrate the team before conducting an experiment?
A: Show answer
The leader should explain that the purpose of the experiment is not to prove that the idea will work perfectly on the first attempt. The team should expect that the first version may need adjustment and focus on carefully observing what happens and learning how to make the process work better. This changes the team's mindset from judging the idea as a success or failure to investigating the process response. The leader should invite team members to use their knowledge to notice what is not working and suggest what to test next. Calibration helps direct the team's creative effort toward learning rather than defending or dismissing the initial proposal.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a short pre-experiment briefing that explains the learning question, expected uncertainty, observation responsibilities, and how results will be discussed.
Key point:
- Set expectations that the first attempt may require adjustment.
- Engage the team in observing and learning rather than defending an idea.
Source links:
43. Q: Why should teams treat every step toward the target condition as an experiment?
A: Show answer
Each step changes or probes the situation and produces consequences that can be observed. Even when a step is small, it can reveal something about the process, alter the learner's view of the obstacle, or expose a new constraint. Treating steps as experiments encourages people to remain curious, examine results, and adjust rather than assume they already know the full route. It also discourages planning too far ahead based on an incomplete picture. The learner should concentrate on the next testable step and remain open to an unexpected result that may point toward a more effective direction.
Study instruction:
- Review a sequence of improvement actions and identify the learning question and observable consequence associated with each step.
Key point:
- Each step can advance the work and improve understanding simultaneously.
- Keep attention on the next experiment rather than pretending the entire path is known.
Source links:
44. Q: How can a team remain open to unexpected results without losing direction?
A: Show answer
The team should keep the target condition and its constraints visible while treating each proposed step as provisional. Before the experiment, define the prediction and the evidence that will be collected. Afterward, record what happened accurately, even if it conflicts with the preferred explanation. The team then evaluates the result in relation to the obstacle and decides what to learn or test next. Openness does not mean following every surprising observation without judgment; the evidence must be relevant, reliable, and interpreted in context. The target condition provides direction while the learning cycle provides a disciplined way to adapt the route.
Study instruction:
- Create a decision rule for responding to confirming, refuting, mixed, and inconclusive results while keeping the target condition unchanged.
Key point:
- Openness to surprise and commitment to direction can coexist.
- Evidence should shape the next step within the target's boundaries.
Source links:
45. Q: How can teams experiment with processes that have long cycle times or operate infrequently?
A: Show answer
Long-cycle processes make rapid learning difficult because each real process cycle may take substantial time and several data points may be needed to evaluate a change. The chapter suggests using low-cost laboratory simulations between actual process runs. These simulations can test aspects of an idea more frequently, allowing the team to gain interim learning before conducting a larger experiment under real operating conditions. The real process test remains necessary for information that only the actual environment can provide. The team should therefore distinguish what the simulation can validly test from what still requires confirmation in the live process.
Study instruction:
- For an infrequent process, identify which variables can be tested in simulation and which outcomes require a real operating cycle.
Key point:
- Simulation can accelerate learning between infrequent real-world tests.
- Validate important conclusions under actual process conditions.
Source links:
46. Q: What should a learner do when one experiment takes a long time to prepare?
A: Show answer
Ideally, the learner conducts one experiment at a time on a focus process so that cause and effect remain clear. However, waiting for a lengthy preparation can waste valuable learning time. The chapter allows the learner to conduct another experiment against a different and unrelated obstacle while the first experiment is being prepared. The experiments should not interfere with one another or make it difficult to interpret results. The learner should maintain separate records and ensure that each test remains tied to its own obstacle and prediction. This approach preserves learning cadence without creating confounding changes in the same process.
Study instruction:
- Identify a preparation delay and determine whether a separate experiment on an unrelated obstacle can safely proceed without affecting interpretation.
Key point:
- Keep one experiment active per focus issue when cause and effect could be confused.
- Use waiting time for genuinely unrelated learning opportunities where appropriate.
Source links:
47. Q: What should be checked when planning an experiment?
A: Show answer
Planning begins by linking the experiment to a specific obstacle and identifying what must be learned at the current threshold of knowledge. The learner should define the test, consider whether one factor can be changed, and find the quickest simple method that can produce useful evidence. The learner must assess whether an unexpected result could cause harm and create a buffer or simulation if needed. Before the test, record the prediction and decide how the outcome will be measured. Where possible, build on the previous experiment's learning. Finally, the learner must be willing to accept evidence that does not match the prediction. These checks make the experiment focused, measurable, safe, and useful for the next decision.
Study instruction:
- Use the chapter's planning checklist to review a proposed experiment and identify any missing prediction, measure, obstacle link, or safety provision.
Key point:
- Good planning connects obstacle, knowledge gap, test, prediction, measure, and risk control.
- The learner must be prepared to learn from results that contradict expectations.
Source links:
48. Q: Why is one data point rarely enough to evaluate an experiment?
A: Show answer
A single observation may reflect an unusual event rather than a repeatable process response. Measuring several cycles helps the learner see whether the result is consistent, variable, or dependent on particular conditions. The chapter recommends collecting multiple observations and using a run chart to make the sequence visible. Where useful, data can be stratified by time, person, item, or machine to reveal patterns that an overall total may hide. The learner should avoid relying on averages alone because an average can conceal variation and important differences between operating conditions. The appropriate number of observations depends on the process and the learning question; the key is to collect enough evidence to evaluate the prediction responsibly.
Study instruction:
- Build a run chart from several observations and compare it with a single-point conclusion to show how additional data changes interpretation.
Key point:
- Evaluate patterns across several cycles rather than overinterpreting one observation.
- Run charts and useful stratification can reveal variation hidden by summary figures.
Source links:
49. Q: Why must recording facts be separated from interpreting experiment results?
A: Show answer
Recording facts means documenting what was observed or measured without immediately explaining why it happened. Interpretation is the later process of drawing conclusions from those facts and deciding what they mean for the hypothesis and next step. Combining the two can cause assumptions to be written as if they were observations, making it difficult for a coach or another reviewer to assess the evidence independently. The experimenting record separates the compilation of facts and data from the formation of conclusions. This discipline helps the learner distinguish what the process did from what the learner thinks caused it.
Study instruction:
- Review an experiment report and mark each statement as an observation, measurement, inference, or conclusion; rewrite any inference presented as a fact.
Key point:
- Preserve an objective record before forming an explanation.
- Conclusions should be traceable to the evidence collected.
Source links:
50. Q: What are the possible outcomes when an experiment is evaluated?
A: Show answer
The evidence may support the prediction, fail to support it, come close to the expected result, or remain inconclusive because more information is needed. If the result supports the prediction, the learner may consider standardizing the step, subject to checking whether the result is sufficiently reliable and sustainable. If it does not support the prediction, the learner should treat the difference as useful information and reconsider the hypothesis or obstacle. A result close to expectation may indicate a specific adjustment to test next. An inconclusive result calls for better measurement, more observations, or a revised experiment. The learner should not force every outcome into a simple success-or-failure label.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a response for each of the four outcome types and identify the next action that would be justified by the evidence.
Key point:
- Results can confirm, refute, nearly confirm, or remain inconclusive.
- The next step should follow from what the evidence actually supports.
Source links:
51. Q: What should happen when an experiment supports the prediction?
A: Show answer
A confirming result may justify standardizing the tested step, but the learner should consider whether the evidence covers enough cycles and conditions to support that decision. A successful trial does not automatically prove that the change will remain effective when the process is used more broadly or over time. The team should consider how to institutionalize and maintain the change, including whether responsibilities, work methods, training, or supporting arrangements must be adjusted. The chapter warns that changing a document or simply telling people is rarely sufficient. Additional experiments may be needed to establish that the new way of working is reliable and sustained.
Study instruction:
- For a successful test, prepare a standardization plan and identify the further checks needed to confirm that the change is maintained in normal operations.
Key point:
- Confirmation can support standardization, but sustainability must still be tested.
- A document update or announcement alone may not institutionalize a change.
Source links:
52. Q: How should a learner respond when an experiment does not support the prediction?
A: Show answer
The learner should first preserve the observed facts and resist changing the record to make the original idea appear correct. Next, compare the actual result with the prediction and ask what assumption may have been wrong, what condition was overlooked, or what new obstacle has become visible. The learner may need to revise the hypothesis, gather more information, or design a different experiment. A refuted prediction is not a reason to abandon the target condition automatically. It is evidence that the current understanding or proposed step needs adjustment. The learner should explain the learning clearly to the coach and use it to determine the next test.
Study instruction:
- Write a short evaluation of a hypothetical refuted prediction, separating the data from the revised interpretation and next experiment.
Key point:
- Do not conceal or rationalize an unexpected result.
- Use refutation to improve understanding and the next experiment.
Source links:
53. Q: How should a learner respond when the result is close to the prediction but not conclusive?
A: Show answer
A near result may indicate that the proposed idea is directionally useful but needs refinement, or that the test conditions and measurements were not precise enough to support a firm conclusion. The learner should examine the size and pattern of the difference, consider whether the prediction was sufficiently specific, and identify what would need to change in the next test. The response should not be to declare success merely because the outcome was favorable in part. A further experiment can test the adjustment or collect more evidence. The learner should explain exactly what was close, what remains uncertain, and how the next test will address that uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- Take a near-confirming result and formulate a revised prediction that addresses the remaining uncertainty without changing several factors unnecessarily.
Key point:
- A near result can guide refinement rather than justify premature closure.
- State what remains uncertain and design the next test accordingly.
Source links:
54. Q: What should a learner do when the available evidence is insufficient to determine whether a prediction was confirmed?
A: Show answer
The learner should acknowledge that the result is inconclusive rather than force a conclusion. Review whether the measure was appropriate, the observation period was long enough, enough process cycles were observed, and relevant conditions were recorded. The next experiment may involve collecting additional data, improving the measurement method, or narrowing the learning question. If the process is infrequent, simulation may help explore some aspects while waiting for real operating evidence. The coach should be shown the limitation clearly so the next step is based on the actual state of knowledge rather than a false sense of certainty.
Study instruction:
- Diagnose an inconclusive experiment by checking the prediction, measurement method, sample of cycles, and missing contextual information.
Key point:
- Inconclusive means more or better evidence is needed, not that the idea has been proved.
- Improve the test before drawing a stronger conclusion.
Source links:
55. Q: Why should experiment data sometimes be stratified by time, person, item, or machine?
A: Show answer
Stratification can reveal patterns that disappear when all observations are combined. A change may work on one shift but not another, for one machine but not another, or for one product type but not another. Grouping observations by relevant conditions helps the learner identify where the process behaves differently and whether a proposed countermeasure is sensitive to context. The chapter recommends considering stratification alongside run charts and warns against relying on averages alone. The categories should be selected because they may explain meaningful process variation, not because more categories automatically make the analysis better.
Study instruction:
- Take a small process dataset and stratify it by one relevant factor; explain what additional question the stratified view raises.
Key point:
- Stratification can expose differences hidden in aggregated data.
- Choose categories that relate to plausible process conditions.
Source links:
56. Q: Why does the chapter advise avoiding averages when evaluating experiment results?
A: Show answer
An average can compress different observations into one figure and conceal the variation that matters for understanding a process. For example, a mean cycle time may appear acceptable even though certain shifts or items experience substantial delays. Averages may therefore hide the conditions under which the process performs differently. A run chart preserves the sequence of observations, while stratification can show whether variation is associated with time, person, item, or machine. This does not mean averages are never useful; it means they should not replace examination of the underlying data when the purpose is to learn how a process behaves.
Study instruction:
- Compare a run chart with a single average for the same observations and identify variation or patterns the average conceals.
Key point:
- Summary averages can hide important process variation.
- Inspect the underlying sequence and relevant strata before drawing conclusions.
Source links:
57. Q: How can a learner determine whether an experiment has produced enough learning to move to another obstacle?
A: Show answer
The learner should evaluate whether the current obstacle has been sufficiently understood or overcome to justify changing focus. Evidence may show that a countermeasure has removed the barrier, that the remaining issue is now different, or that the obstacle is no longer relevant to the target condition. If the obstacle still blocks progress or the result remains uncertain, another experiment against it may be more appropriate. The learner should avoid moving to a new obstacle simply because the first experiment was completed. The parking lot and experimenting record should reflect the current understanding, and the decision should be discussed during the coaching cycle.
Study instruction:
- Define evidence that would justify closing, continuing, or reframing an obstacle and apply the criteria to a current improvement effort.
Key point:
- Completion of a test is not the same as resolution of an obstacle.
- Move focus based on evidence about the obstacle's status.
Source links:
58. Q: How should a team maintain an improvement after a successful experiment?
A: Show answer
The team should determine how the tested change will become part of normal work and how its effectiveness will be maintained. This may require additional experiments to identify reliable work methods, clarify who performs and monitors the new step, and determine what training or support is necessary. Simply revising a procedure or announcing the change may not ensure that people consistently use it. The team should observe the process after adoption, check whether the expected condition persists, and respond when actual practice differs from the intended method. Institutionalizing the change is therefore a further learning and control challenge, not an administrative afterthought.
Study instruction:
- Develop a sustainment plan for one successful experiment, including standard work, responsibility, training, monitoring, and response to deviation.
Key point:
- A successful test must be translated into a maintained operating practice.
- Standardization and sustainment may require further experiments.
Source links:
59. Q: How can a learner improve the quality of the experimenting method itself?
A: Show answer
After evaluating the process result, the learner should also reflect on the quality of the experiment procedure. Consider whether the learning question was clear, the prediction was specific, the test was appropriately limited, the measurement captured the relevant outcome, and the evidence was collected consistently. The learner can also ask whether the experiment was unnecessarily complicated or whether a simpler test could have produced the same learning sooner. This reflection improves the learner's ability to design future experiments, not just the performance of the focus process. The chapter encourages learners to become better experimenters by examining what they could have done differently in conducting the test.
Study instruction:
- Complete a post-experiment review of the test design itself and identify one improvement to the next experiment's method.
Key point:
- Reflect on both the process outcome and the quality of the experiment.
- Better experiment design increases the value of future learning cycles.
Source links:
60. Q: How can the experimenting routine be adapted without losing its scientific thinking pattern?
A: Show answer
Learners should first practice using the experimenting record as designed so they internalize its sequence and purpose. Once that pattern is understood, the form may be adapted to fit the work environment. Different layouts can be acceptable if they preserve the essential functions: connect the experiment to a specific obstacle, record the prediction before the test, capture evidence after the test, distinguish facts from interpretation, and use coaching to review the learning and next step. Changing the form before understanding its logic risks removing the very structure that makes the routine useful. Adaptation should therefore preserve the underlying scientific learning cycle.
Study instruction:
- Compare a proposed customized record with the standard pattern and verify that every essential learning function remains visible.
Key point:
- Learn the routine before changing its format.
- Adaptation is acceptable when the core prediction-test-data-evaluation pattern is preserved.
Source links:
61. Q: What are the consequences of implementing a change and moving on without evaluating it?
A: Show answer
The team loses the opportunity to learn whether the change produced the expected result, what conditions influenced it, and what should happen next. A change may appear to work temporarily while creating hidden problems, or it may fail for a reason that could have been corrected through a further test. Moving on without evaluation also makes it harder to distinguish the effect of the change from normal process variation or other events. The chapter identifies this behavior as an unscientific mode of working. The learner should therefore complete the evidence and evaluation stages before deciding whether to standardize, adjust, repeat, or abandon the proposed step.
Study instruction:
- Examine an improvement action that was implemented without follow-up and list the questions that can no longer be answered reliably.
Key point:
- Action without reflection produces weak learning and uncertain conclusions.
- Evaluate each step before deciding what to do next.
Source links:
62. Q: How should managers balance urgency with the need for careful experimentation?
A: Show answer
Managers should increase the speed and frequency of learning while preserving the boundaries of the experimenting zone. Urgency is addressed by choosing simple tests, reducing unnecessary preparation, using prototypes, and removing avoidable delays. Care is maintained by defining predictions and measures, limiting the scale of tests, protecting safety and quality, and checking results across sufficient observations. A rushed full-scale implementation can create more delay if it causes harm or requires extensive rework. Conversely, excessive analysis can consume the time needed to learn. The practical balance is to run the smallest safe and informative experiment as soon as possible, then adapt from its evidence.
Study instruction:
- Review a delayed improvement effort and distinguish unnecessary analysis from preparation that is genuinely required for safe, valid testing.
Key point:
- Speed comes from shorter learning cycles, not from bypassing controls.
- The best next test is both timely and sufficiently safe and measurable.
Source links:
63. Q: What would you do if a team insists that a favored solution will work because it succeeded elsewhere?
A: Show answer
Acknowledge the prior experience as useful information, then identify which conditions made it successful and whether those conditions exist in the current focus process. State the assumptions that remain unverified and design a local test that can reveal whether the idea works under the current target condition and constraints. Begin with a provisional or small-scale version if possible, define the prediction, and measure the result. If the evidence differs from the prior example, investigate the local process factors rather than forcing the implementation to match the external case. This approach respects existing knowledge without treating it as a substitute for direct verification.
Study instruction:
- Choose a borrowed improvement and prepare a local validation experiment that tests its most important assumptions.
Key point:
- Prior success is a source of ideas, not proof of local effectiveness.
- Test transferred solutions in the context where they will be used.
Source links:
64. Q: What would you do if an experiment might expose customers or employees to an unintended harmful effect?
A: Show answer
Do not proceed at the proposed scale until the risk is controlled. Identify who or what could be affected, what failure could occur, and how serious the consequences might be. Redesign the test as a smaller trial, create a safety buffer, use an offline simulation, or select a different experiment that can answer the learning question without exposing others to unacceptable harm. Confirm that the test remains within safety and quality requirements and that the responsible people understand the safeguards. If no safe design is available, defer or reject the experiment and seek an alternative. The need to learn does not override the obligation to protect the process customer and affected people.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a pre-test risk review for a proposed experiment and specify the containment measures or alternative test required.
Key point:
- Unexpected results must not be allowed to create uncontrolled harm.
- Use buffers, simulations, or smaller tests to limit exposure.
Source links:
65. Q: What would you do if a process change alters the current condition after each experiment?
A: Show answer
Treat the changed process as the new current condition and update the storyboard and supporting evidence before designing the next experiment. A process change can affect cycle time, sequence, variation, constraints, or the relevance of earlier observations. Reusing the old current-condition description may lead to a prediction based on conditions that no longer exist. Collect fresh observations, update run charts where needed, revise the Obstacles Parking Lot, and use the latest experiment's reflection to determine the next step. This keeps the learning cycle anchored in the process as it currently operates.
Study instruction:
- After a hypothetical process change, identify which current-condition measures, charts, and obstacle descriptions need to be refreshed.
Key point:
- A changed process requires an updated current-condition picture.
- The next experiment should be based on the latest evidence, not outdated baseline assumptions.
Source links:
66. Q: How can teams use run charts to support coaching and experiment evaluation?
A: Show answer
A run chart displays observations in sequence so the learner and coach can see how the process behaves over time and how it responds to an experiment. It helps prevent an evaluation from relying only on a remembered impression or one selected result. The learner can use the chart to discuss whether the prediction was supported, whether variation changed, and whether additional observations or stratification are needed. The chart should be based on the latest process data and should be updated when experiments change the current condition. It is an aid to evidence-based discussion, not a substitute for understanding what was changed and under which conditions.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a run chart for a small set of process observations and use it to explain the result of a specific experiment during a mock coaching cycle.
Key point:
- Run charts make sequential process evidence visible for evaluation.
- Interpret the chart together with the experiment, context, and prediction.
Source links:
67. Q: How can an organization develop scientific thinking capability beyond a single improvement team?
A: Show answer
Organizations can develop this capability by teaching people to recognize knowledge thresholds, make predictions explicit, conduct small and frequent tests, record evidence, distinguish observations from interpretations, and reflect on results. Leaders should model curiosity about unexpected outcomes and avoid treating every refuted prediction as a personal failure. The experimenting record and Coaching Kata provide repeatable routines that help make the pattern visible and teachable. As more people practice the routine, everyday work can become a source of structured learning rather than a sequence of unexamined actions. The chapter presents cumulative small improvements by everyday experimenters as an important source of organizational innovation.
Study instruction:
- Design a practice routine for a team that includes short experiments, record review, coaching, and reflection on experiment quality.
Key point:
- Scientific thinking is a capability that can be developed through repeated routines.
- Organizational innovation can accumulate through many small, evidence-based steps.
Source links:
68. Q: What is the relationship between experimentation, adaptiveness, agility, and resilience?
A: Show answer
Experimentation develops these capabilities by helping people recognize when their knowledge ends and respond through practical learning rather than rigidly following assumptions. Adaptiveness comes from revising the route when evidence changes the understanding of the situation. Agility comes from designing and running useful tests quickly. Resilience comes from treating unexpected outcomes as information and continuing to learn within the target's constraints. These capabilities depend on disciplined routines, not simply on encouraging people to be flexible. The learner needs a clear target, explicit predictions, timely tests, reliable evidence, and a willingness to adjust the next step.
Study instruction:
- For a changing work environment, identify how one experiment could improve adaptability, speed of response, and ability to recover from an unexpected result.
Key point:
- Experimentation turns uncertainty into a structured opportunity to adapt.
- Agility and resilience depend on fast learning within clear boundaries.
Source links:
69. Q: How should a learner explain an experiment during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should use the updated storyboard and experimenting record to explain the obstacle being addressed, what was predicted, what step was taken, what facts and data were collected, and what conclusions were drawn. The learner should then describe the proposed next experiment and why it follows from the latest understanding. Initial answers can be read directly from the storyboard, with the learner pointing to the relevant information as the coach asks the five Coaching Kata questions. The explanation should distinguish observations from interpretations and openly identify what remains uncertain. This allows the coach to assess the learner's thinking rather than merely hear a status report.
Study instruction:
- Rehearse a coaching-cycle explanation using one completed experimenting record and the corresponding updated storyboard.
Key point:
- Use the record and storyboard to make reasoning and evidence visible.
- Explain both what was learned and why the next experiment is appropriate.
Source links:
70. Q: What are the most important lessons from experimenting toward the target condition in Step 4?
A: Show answer
Step 4 is a disciplined way to make progress through uncertainty. The learner does not need to know the complete route in advance, but must keep the target condition and its constraints clear. Work at the threshold of knowledge, select one obstacle, record a prediction, run the smallest safe and informative test, collect evidence across enough observations, and evaluate facts separately from interpretation. Use unexpected results to improve understanding, update the current condition and obstacle picture, and design the next experiment. The learner owns the experimenting record and uses it with the Coaching Kata to reflect and receive feedback. Successful changes require further attention to standardization and sustainment. The central capability is learning the way forward one evidence-based step at a time.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter's complete routine and conduct a practice cycle from obstacle selection through prediction, test, data, evaluation, coaching, and next-step design.
Key point:
- Experimentation is a repeatable method for learning and advancing toward a challenging target condition.
- The quality of improvement depends on disciplined predictions, evidence, reflection, safety, and adaptation.

Table 8
The Toyota Kata Summary Reflection and Continuous Learning
1. Q: What is a summary reflection in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
A summary reflection is a structured review conducted by the learner and coach when the target condition is achieved or its achieve-by date arrives, whichever occurs first. Its purpose is to examine the entire four-step Improvement Kata cycle, identify what worked and what did not, evaluate the learning gained through experimentation, and determine what should be improved in the next cycle. It is not simply a review of operational results. It is also an assessment of how effectively the learner practiced scientific thinking and how effectively the coach developed the learner's problem-solving capability. Both participants should identify specific behaviors, skills, and routines they want to emphasize, experiment with, or change during their next improvement cycle.

Study instruction:
- Review the four steps of the Improvement Kata and explain how a summary reflection connects the completed cycle to the next cycle.

Key point:
- Summary reflection converts improvement experience into learning for the next cycle.
- Both learner development and coaching effectiveness must be examined.

Source links:
- Mike Rother, The Toyota Kata Practice Guide, Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
2. Q: Why is a summary reflection necessary after completing an Improvement Kata cycle?
A: Show answer
A summary reflection prevents improvement activities from becoming a series of disconnected experiments without accumulated learning. During an improvement cycle, the learner encounters obstacles, tests assumptions, observes actual outcomes, and develops a better understanding of the process. Without a structured reflection, valuable knowledge may be lost when attention shifts to another target condition. The reflection helps identify successful approaches, ineffective assumptions, recurring obstacles, gaps in scientific thinking, and opportunities to improve the coaching relationship. It also helps distinguish between achieving a target condition and developing the capability to achieve future target conditions. The organization benefits when learning is retained and incorporated into subsequent improvement activities rather than remaining dependent on individual memory or informal discussions.

Study instruction:
- Identify three examples of learning that could be lost if an improvement cycle ends without a formal reflection.

Key point:
- Improvement results are valuable, but accumulated learning builds sustainable improvement capability.
- Reflection creates a deliberate connection between past experiments and future actions.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
3. Q: When should the learner and coach conduct a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
Conduct the summary reflection as soon as either of two events occurs: the learner achieves the target condition before the planned achieve-by date, or the achieve-by date arrives even though the target condition has not yet been achieved. The first event indicates that the intended condition has been reached earlier than expected. The second indicates that the planned time has expired without achieving the intended condition.
