Both are appropriate reflection points because they provide an opportunity to compare expectations with actual outcomes. The learner should not automatically wait until the original deadline when the target condition has already been achieved. Equally, the learner should not postpone reflection simply because additional time might produce the desired outcome.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying the correct reflection trigger in scenarios involving early achievement and an unachieved target condition at the deadline.

Key point:
- Reflect when the target condition is achieved or the achieve-by date arrives, whichever happens first.
- Both outcomes provide opportunities for learning.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
4. Q: What should happen when the target condition is achieved earlier than planned?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should conduct the summary reflection immediately rather than waiting for the original achieve-by date. Early achievement is an unexpected outcome relative to the original plan and deserves examination. They should establish which experiments contributed to the result, whether the assumptions behind the improvement were correct, what conditions made early achievement possible, and whether the target condition is genuinely being sustained. They should also examine whether the improvement exposed a new obstacle or opportunity that should influence the next target condition. Early completion should not be treated merely as permission to stop practicing. The next improvement cycle should begin with an updated understanding of the current condition and a newly established target condition.

Study instruction:
- Develop a scenario in which a production process achieves its target condition two weeks before the deadline and identify the reflection activities required.

Key point:
- Early achievement is a learning opportunity, not a reason to skip reflection.
- Verify the actual condition before establishing the next target.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
5. Q: What should happen when the achieve-by date arrives but the target condition has not been achieved?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should stop the current cycle and conduct the summary reflection. They should not simply continue experimenting under the original target condition without reviewing what happened. The reflection should establish the actual level of achievement, identify which obstacles remain, examine why previous experiments did not produce the expected results, and assess whether the learner's understanding of the process was accurate. The original target condition remains an important reference for evaluating the outcome, even though it has not been achieved. The learner should use the information gained to reassess the current condition and establish the next target condition. An unsuccessful cycle can still produce valuable knowledge if the experiments were conducted systematically and their results were examined honestly.

Study instruction:
- Practice documenting an unachieved target condition by distinguishing completed experiments, remaining obstacles, actual results, and new knowledge.

Key point:
- An unachieved target condition is not automatically a failed learning cycle.
- The deadline triggers reflection and renewed planning, not indefinite extension.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
6. Q: Why should the learner avoid extending an achieve-by date simply because the target condition is almost achieved?
A: Show answer
An achieve-by date establishes a meaningful commitment to a specific improvement cycle. If the learner routinely extends the date whenever the target condition is not achieved, the date gradually loses its value as a planning and learning reference. Repeated extensions can also encourage avoidance of difficult reflection, conceal weaknesses in experimentation, and reduce accountability for understanding the actual condition. Instead of extending the existing cycle, the learner should examine what prevented achievement and use that knowledge to establish the next target condition. This does not mean that management should ignore legitimate operational constraints. It means that changes to the improvement cycle should be handled transparently rather than casually rewriting the original commitment to make the outcome appear successful.

Study instruction:
- Compare the learning consequences of extending a deadline repeatedly with those of conducting a reflection at the original deadline.

Key point:
- Preserve the original achieve-by date as a reference for learning.
- Do not conceal an unexpected outcome by repeatedly changing the deadline.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
7. Q: What are the main objectives of a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The main objectives are to evaluate the completed Improvement Kata cycle, identify positive and negative experiences, understand the learning produced by experiments, evaluate the learner's scientific-thinking behavior, evaluate the coach's coaching behavior, identify skills requiring further development, and prepare for the next improvement cycle. The reflection should also help the participants recognize whether their understanding of the current condition, obstacles, target condition, and experimental process improved. It should produce actionable learning rather than a general statement that the project went well or poorly. The result should be a clear understanding of what to continue, what to change, and what to practice next.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a summary reflection template with separate prompts for improvement results, learner development, coaching development, and next-cycle priorities.

Key point:
- Reflection should produce specific learning and future actions.
- Both process improvement and capability development matter.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
8. Q: What should the learner evaluate during the summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The learner should evaluate how effectively they practiced the Improvement Kata throughout the cycle. This includes how well they understood the challenge, studied the current condition, established a measurable target condition, identified obstacles, formulated hypotheses, designed experiments, predicted expected results, observed actual outcomes, and adjusted their understanding based on evidence. The learner should also consider whether they relied on assumptions, jumped to solutions, avoided difficult experiments, or failed to investigate unexpected results. The purpose is not to criticize the learner but to identify specific behaviors that can be strengthened through deliberate practice. For example, a learner may have become better at collecting process data but still need to improve the ability to formulate testable predictions.

Study instruction:
- Review one completed improvement cycle and identify at least two scientific-thinking skills that improved and two that require further practice.

Key point:
- Evaluate how the learner thinks and works, not only the final operational result.
- Identify specific behaviors for deliberate practice.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
9. Q: What should the coach evaluate during the summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The coach should evaluate how effectively they practiced the Coaching Kata and helped the learner develop scientific-thinking capability. Relevant considerations include whether the coach consistently asked the coaching questions, listened to the learner's reasoning, encouraged evidence-based investigation, avoided supplying solutions prematurely, and helped the learner identify the next experiment. The coach should also assess whether they provided appropriate procedural guidance without taking ownership of the learner's problem-solving responsibilities. If the learner repeatedly struggled with a particular skill, the coach should consider whether the coaching approach adequately addressed that development need. The reflection provides an opportunity for the coach to identify behaviors they should emphasize, modify, or practice during the next cycle.

Study instruction:
- Review a coaching interaction and identify where the coach encouraged independent reasoning and where the coach may have supplied too much direction.

Key point:
- Coaching effectiveness must also be reflected upon.
- The coach develops the learner's capability rather than simply delivering solutions.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
10. Q: What are the four steps of the Improvement Kata that should be reviewed during a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The four steps are: (1) Understand the direction or challenge, (2) grasp the current condition, (3) establish the next target condition, and (4) experiment toward the target condition. During reflection, participants should examine how effectively each step was performed and how the steps connected. For example, they should determine whether the target condition genuinely supported the challenge, whether the current condition was based on facts and data, whether the target condition was measurable and time-bound, and whether experiments systematically addressed obstacles. Reviewing all four steps helps identify weaknesses in the overall improvement process instead of attributing every problem to the execution stage. It also establishes a more reliable foundation for the next improvement cycle.

Study instruction:
- Draw the four-step Improvement Kata pattern and write one reflection question under each step.

Key point:
- The reflection covers the complete improvement pattern, not just the experiments.
- Weaknesses in earlier steps can affect later improvement activities.

Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
11. Q: What are plusses and minuses in a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
Plusses are aspects of the improvement and coaching process that worked effectively or generated useful learning. Examples include accurate current-condition analysis, well-designed experiments, clear predictions, effective obstacle identification, and constructive coaching conversations. Minuses are aspects that did not work as intended or revealed opportunities for improvement. Examples include incomplete data, poorly defined target conditions, experiments without clear predictions, delayed observation, and coaching that encouraged dependence on the coach. Plusses and minuses should be supported by actual experiences and evidence rather than personal preferences. Their purpose is to identify behaviors worth reinforcing and behaviors requiring adjustment in the next cycle.

Study instruction:
- Create a two-part reflection record and classify at least five actual experiences from a completed improvement cycle.

Key point:
- Plusses identify practices worth reinforcing.
- Minuses identify learning opportunities and improvement needs.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
12. Q: How should the learner distinguish between an operational result and a learning result?
A: Show answer
An operational result describes what happened to the process, such as reduced processing time, fewer defects, improved delivery performance, or increased throughput. A learning result describes what the learner discovered about the process, the obstacles, the relationships between process variables, and the validity of previous assumptions. The two are related but not identical. An experiment may fail to improve performance while still revealing an important process relationship. Conversely, a favorable operational result may occur without a clear understanding of why it happened. A strong summary reflection examines both dimensions so that the organization does not confuse temporary success with improved scientific-thinking capability.

Study instruction:
- Select three experiments and document their operational outcomes separately from the knowledge gained.

Key point:
- Operational performance measures what changed.
- Learning explains what was discovered and how future decisions should change.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
13. Q: How should the learner identify the most valuable lessons from completed experiments?
A: Show answer
The learner should review the sequence of experiments and compare each prediction with the actual observation. The most valuable lessons are often found where reality differed from expectations because those differences reveal gaps in the learner's understanding. The learner should identify what was tested, why the experiment was selected, what was expected, what actually happened, and what was learned. They should also determine whether the learning changes their understanding of an obstacle, process relationship, or potential improvement approach. Lessons should be expressed as specific statements that can influence future decisions rather than vague conclusions such as needing better communication or working harder.

Study instruction:
- Reconstruct an experiment using its prediction, actual result, interpretation, and implication for the next experiment.

Key point:
- Differences between expectations and actual results are valuable sources of learning.
- Every experiment should contribute to better understanding.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
14. Q: How should unexpected outcomes be handled during a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
Unexpected outcomes should be treated as opportunities to improve understanding rather than automatically classified as failures. Participants should establish what was expected, what actually occurred, which assumptions were incorrect or incomplete, and what new information has become available. If a target condition was achieved earlier than planned, they should investigate the factors that enabled early achievement. If the deadline arrived without achievement, they should investigate the remaining obstacles and the reasons experiments did not produce the expected progress. In either case, the objective is to update the learner's understanding and establish a better-informed next step. The reflection should not distort the original prediction or rewrite historical records to make the outcome appear consistent with expectations.

Study instruction:
- Practice analyzing an experiment that produces an outcome opposite to the learner's prediction.

Key point:
- Unexpected results are information that can improve the next experiment.
- Record actual outcomes honestly.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
15. Q: What skills should the learner consider developing in the next Improvement Kata cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should select skills based on evidence from the completed cycle. Relevant skills include observing processes directly, collecting reliable data, distinguishing facts from assumptions, defining measurable target conditions, identifying obstacles, formulating hypotheses, predicting experimental outcomes, conducting controlled tests, and interpreting unexpected results. The learner may also need to improve the ability to recognize when a problem exceeds their current knowledge and design an experiment to investigate it. The next-cycle development priorities should be specific and limited enough to receive deliberate practice. Selecting every possible weakness as a development priority can dilute attention and make it difficult to observe meaningful improvement in behavior.

Study instruction:
- Select two scientific-thinking skills from a previous cycle and design practical exercises for developing them.

Key point:
- Development priorities should arise from observed learning needs.
- Focused practice is more useful than an excessively broad development plan.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
16. Q: How can the coach improve their Coaching Kata through a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The coach should examine whether their coaching interactions consistently helped the learner think through the improvement process. They should assess whether they asked clear questions, encouraged direct observation, requested supporting evidence, challenged assumptions constructively, and allowed the learner to develop their own hypotheses and experiments. They should also examine whether they intervened too quickly, provided answers instead of questions, or focused excessively on performance results. The next cycle should include specific coaching improvements, such as allowing more time for learner reasoning or asking the learner to explain the evidence supporting a conclusion. Coaching improvement should be treated as a deliberate learning process rather than an informal assessment of interpersonal performance.

Study instruction:
- Review several coaching sessions and identify one coaching behavior to maintain and one to change.

Key point:
- Coaches must continuously develop their own coaching capability.
- Effective coaching strengthens independent scientific thinking.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
17. Q: What is the relationship between summary reflection and PDCA?
A: Show answer
PDCA stands for Plan, Do, Check, and Act. It is the scientific problem-solving cycle used to test ideas and learn from actual results. Summary reflection operates at a broader level by reviewing the complete improvement cycle and the cumulative learning produced by multiple experiments. Individual PDCA cycles help the learner investigate obstacles and move toward the target condition. The summary reflection then examines the effectiveness of the overall Improvement Kata practice and determines what should be carried forward into the next cycle. It therefore complements rather than replaces daily experimentation and review. The learner should retain the discipline of evaluating individual experiments while also periodically evaluating the larger improvement process.

Study instruction:
- Draw the relationship between repeated PDCA experiments and the larger Improvement Kata cycle.

Key point:
- PDCA generates learning through individual experiments.
- Summary reflection consolidates learning across the completed improvement cycle.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
18. Q: How should the learner evaluate whether the original challenge remains appropriate?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should revisit the overall direction or challenge after the summary reflection and consider whether it remains relevant to the organization's intended direction. They should examine whether the completed improvement cycle has changed their understanding of the process, exposed new constraints, or revealed opportunities that affect the original challenge. A challenge should not be abandoned simply because one target condition was difficult to achieve. Equally, it should not be followed blindly if important changes in organizational priorities or operating conditions have made it inappropriate. The decision should be based on the current situation, the intended direction, and the learning gained through improvement activities.

Study instruction:
- Compare the original challenge with the latest process understanding and identify whether the direction still makes sense.

Key point:
- The challenge provides direction across successive target conditions.
- Reflection can inform whether the direction should remain unchanged or be reconsidered.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
19. Q: What should happen to the learner's storyboard after the summary reflection?
A: Show answer
After the reflection, the learner's storyboard is normally cleared and reset so that it can support the next improvement cycle. The storyboard is a visual management tool that helps organize information about the challenge, current condition, target condition, obstacles, and experiments. Resetting it prevents outdated information from being confused with the next cycle's planning and execution information. However, clearing the active storyboard should not mean destroying useful historical learning. Relevant experiment records, measurements, reflection notes, and supporting evidence should be retained in an appropriate location. The new storyboard should represent the next cycle's actual direction and current understanding rather than simply reproducing the previous cycle's information.

Study instruction:
- Design a process for closing an improvement storyboard while preserving historical experiment records and reflection findings.

Key point:
- Reset the active storyboard for the next cycle.
- Preserve historical learning and supporting evidence.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
20. Q: Does clearing and resetting the storyboard mean that the previous improvement work should be discarded?
A: Show answer
No. Resetting the storyboard refers to preparing the visual management tool for the next improvement cycle, not discarding the knowledge acquired during the previous cycle. Historical records may include the original challenge, current-condition measurements, target condition, obstacles, experiment predictions, actual results, and summary reflection. These records can help the learner and coach understand how their knowledge has developed and avoid repeating experiments without considering previous findings. Organizations should establish suitable document-management practices to preserve relevant information while keeping the active storyboard clear and useful. The exact retention method may vary, but historical learning should remain accessible when it can inform future decisions.

Study instruction:
- Develop a simple archive structure that separates active improvement information from completed-cycle records.

Key point:
- Resetting the storyboard is not the same as deleting improvement history.
- Preserve information that supports continuity and organizational learning.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
21. Q: Why can the next Improvement Kata cycle be completed more quickly when the learner continues working on the same challenge and focus process?
A: Show answer
The learner may already possess substantial knowledge about the challenge and focus process because of the experiments completed in the previous cycle. This knowledge can make it easier to grasp the current condition and establish the next target condition. Previous observations may have revealed process patterns, performance constraints, relationships between variables, and obstacles that were not initially understood. However, the learner should not automatically assume that all previous information remains valid. Current-condition information must still be checked against the actual process, particularly if operating conditions have changed. Faster planning should result from accumulated understanding, not from skipping the necessary investigation and measurement.

Study instruction:
- Compare the information available at the beginning of the first cycle with the knowledge available when beginning the next cycle.

Key point:
- Previous experiments can reduce uncertainty and accelerate subsequent planning.
- Verify that historical knowledge still reflects the actual process.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
22. Q: What is the difference between a summary reflection and a daily experiment review?
A: Show answer
A daily experiment review examines an individual experiment and its immediate learning. It typically considers what was planned, what was expected, what actually happened, and what should be tested next. A summary reflection examines the completed Improvement Kata cycle as a whole. It evaluates patterns across experiments, the effectiveness of the four-step improvement process, the learner's development, and the coach's development. The two activities operate at different levels and serve complementary purposes. A daily review maintains the discipline of scientific experimentation, while the summary reflection consolidates learning and prepares the participants for the next improvement cycle.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a comparison using an individual experiment and a completed improvement cycle as examples.

Key point:
- Daily reviews examine individual experiments.
- Summary reflections examine the entire improvement cycle.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
23. Q: What is the difference between a summary reflection and a performance appraisal?
A: Show answer
A summary reflection is a learning-oriented review of the improvement process and the development of scientific-thinking skills. A performance appraisal is generally a broader management process used to assess an individual's work performance against organizational expectations. Although both may discuss behavior and results, their purposes are different. Summary reflection should encourage honest discussion of mistakes, unexpected outcomes, assumptions, and development needs without turning every difficulty into a judgment of personal competence. Using the reflection primarily as a performance-rating exercise may discourage the learner from reporting unsuccessful experiments or admitting uncertainty. The organization should preserve a learning-oriented environment while addressing formal performance-management responsibilities through appropriate separate processes.

Study instruction:
- Identify how a learning-focused discussion differs from an evaluation of an employee's performance against formal objectives.

Key point:
- Summary reflection develops improvement capability.
- Performance appraisal serves a different management purpose.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
24. Q: How can a summary reflection prevent repeated mistakes in future improvement cycles?
A: Show answer
It helps participants identify recurring weaknesses in their thinking, experimentation, and coaching behavior before beginning another cycle. For example, a learner may repeatedly establish target conditions without sufficiently understanding the current condition, or conduct experiments without documenting expected results. The coach may repeatedly provide solutions instead of encouraging the learner to investigate obstacles. By identifying these patterns, participants can select specific development actions and deliberately practice different behaviors in the next cycle. Historical reflection records can also help determine whether a previously identified weakness has improved or continues to appear. The objective is to change the underlying working habits rather than simply correct the immediate operational problem.

Study instruction:
- Create a recurring-issue register that links each identified weakness to a practical development action and a subsequent review point.

Key point:
- Repeated mistakes often reveal weaknesses in thinking patterns or routines.
- Reflection should lead to specific behavioral changes.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
25. Q: How should a learner select the next target condition after a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
The learner should return to the Improvement Kata planning process, beginning with the overall challenge and an updated understanding of the current condition. The next target condition should represent the next meaningful step toward the challenge, based on what has been learned from previous experiments. It should describe a desired future process condition clearly enough to guide observation and experimentation. It should also include measurable characteristics and an appropriate achieve-by date. The learner should avoid selecting a target simply because it is easy to achieve or because it repeats the previous target. The next target condition should reflect the actual knowledge gained and the remaining obstacles on the path toward the overall challenge.

Study instruction:
- Use a completed reflection to develop a new measurable target condition and explain how it moves the process toward the challenge.

Key point:
- The next target condition should be based on updated knowledge.
- Successive target conditions move the organization toward its longer-term challenge.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
26. Q: Why does the Improvement Kata require several successive target conditions to achieve a larger challenge?
A: Show answer
A challenging long-term objective often involves uncertainty, obstacles, and process conditions that cannot be fully understood in advance. A single target condition is therefore usually insufficient to achieve the overall challenge. The learner establishes an achievable next condition, experiments toward it, and uses the resulting knowledge to establish another condition. Each cycle moves the process closer to the desired direction while developing a better understanding of what is required. This approach avoids relying on an elaborate predetermined implementation plan when important details are still unknown. Summary reflection supports this progression by ensuring that learning from each completed cycle influences subsequent target conditions and experiments.

Study instruction:
- Develop a sequence of three target conditions that progressively move a process toward a challenging long-term objective.

Key point:
- The challenge provides long-term direction.
- Successive target conditions provide manageable steps through uncertainty.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
27. Q: How can a summary reflection improve the quality of future experiments?
A: Show answer
The reflection can reveal weaknesses in the way previous experiments were designed and conducted. Participants may discover that predictions were too vague, experiments changed several variables simultaneously, measurements were insufficient, or observations were delayed. These findings can be translated into practical improvements in experimental discipline. For example, the learner may decide to document a measurable prediction before every experiment, observe the process immediately after testing, or record unexpected results more systematically. The coach may also adjust questioning techniques to encourage clearer hypotheses. The objective is to make subsequent experiments more informative and improve the learner's ability to use evidence when deciding what to do next.

Study instruction:
- Select one weak experiment and redesign it to produce a clearer prediction, observation, and learning outcome.

Key point:
- Experiment quality improves when previous experimental weaknesses are identified and addressed.
- Better predictions and observations strengthen scientific learning.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
28. Q: How should the learner distinguish between an obstacle that remains and a new obstacle discovered during experimentation?
A: Show answer
A remaining obstacle is a difficulty already recognized during the previous improvement cycle that has not yet been sufficiently addressed. A newly discovered obstacle emerges from additional observation or experimentation and may not have been visible when the original target condition was established. The distinction is useful because it shows how the learner's understanding of the process has developed. Participants should review whether previous obstacles were correctly defined, whether experiments reduced their impact, and whether new information has changed their relative importance. The next improvement cycle should address obstacles based on their current relevance to the target condition rather than simply repeating an old obstacle list without reassessment.

Study instruction:
- Prepare an obstacle register that distinguishes unresolved obstacles from newly discovered obstacles and records the evidence supporting each classification.

Key point:
- Obstacles evolve as process knowledge improves.
- The next cycle should use an updated understanding of the obstacles.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
29. Q: What evidence should support a credible summary reflection?
A: Show answer
A credible reflection should be based on the records and observations generated throughout the improvement cycle. Useful evidence includes the original challenge, current-condition measurements, target-condition description, achieve-by date, obstacle records, experiment documentation, predictions, actual results, process observations, and relevant performance trends. Coaching records or notes may also help participants assess how the Coaching Kata was practiced. The reflection should distinguish recorded facts from personal interpretations and avoid making conclusions unsupported by available evidence. For example, stating that an experiment improved productivity should be supported by appropriate process measurements rather than an unsupported impression. The purpose is to make the reflection accurate, transparent, and useful for future improvement decisions.

Study instruction:
- Assemble an evidence checklist for a completed improvement cycle and identify which documents support each reflection conclusion.

Key point:
- Evidence makes reflection more reliable and actionable.
- Distinguish observed facts from interpretations.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
30. Q: What common mistakes should be avoided during a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include postponing reflection after achieving the target condition, extending the achieve-by date merely to avoid reviewing an incomplete outcome, focusing only on operational performance, blaming individuals for unsuccessful experiments, ignoring the coach's own development, relying on opinions instead of evidence, and failing to identify concrete development actions. Another mistake is treating the reflection as an administrative meeting that produces documentation without changing future behavior. Participants should also avoid assuming that a completed target condition means the overall challenge has been achieved. The reflection should maintain an honest learning orientation and lead to a clear transition into the next improvement cycle.

Study instruction:
- Review five hypothetical reflection failures and explain how each could be corrected.

Key point:
- Reflection must be timely, evidence-based, and learning-oriented.
- Every completed cycle should inform the next cycle.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
31. Q: How should a coach respond when the learner considers an incomplete target condition a personal failure?
A: Show answer
The coach should help the learner distinguish between an operational outcome and the learning process. The fact that a target condition was not achieved does not automatically mean that the learner performed poorly. The important questions are whether the learner understood the actual condition, selected meaningful obstacles, conducted disciplined experiments, compared predictions with observations, and adjusted their thinking based on evidence. The coach should encourage an honest examination of the results without minimizing genuine weaknesses in execution. If the learner failed to conduct experiments systematically, that should become a development priority. The discussion should focus on improving the learner's problem-solving capability rather than protecting or attacking personal performance.

Study instruction:
- Practice coaching a learner who is disappointed because the target condition was not achieved by the deadline.

Key point:
- An incomplete target condition can still produce valuable learning.
- Honest evaluation and development are more important than disguising an unfavorable outcome.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
32. Q: How should a coach respond if the learner achieved the target condition but cannot explain why it happened?
A: Show answer
The coach should encourage the learner to examine the evidence and determine which experiments, process changes, or external conditions contributed to the result. Achievement alone does not establish that the learner understands the causal relationships involved. The learner should review the sequence of experiments, the expected outcomes, the actual observations, and any changes in operating conditions. If the cause remains uncertain, the learner should acknowledge that uncertainty rather than claim an unsupported explanation. Further observation or experimentation may be necessary to understand whether the improvement is repeatable and sustainable. The next target condition should be established using the best available understanding of the process.

Study instruction:
- Develop a coaching dialogue that helps a learner investigate a successful result with insufficient causal evidence.

Key point:
- Achieving a result does not automatically establish understanding.
- Evidence-based explanations strengthen future improvement decisions.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
33. Q: What should the learner do if the process condition changes significantly before the next improvement cycle begins?
A: Show answer
The learner should re-examine the current condition rather than relying entirely on measurements and assumptions from the previous cycle. Significant changes may include new equipment, staffing changes, supplier disruptions, demand fluctuations, changes in operating procedures, or altered customer requirements. These changes can affect the validity of earlier observations and the relevance of previously identified obstacles. The learner should establish what is currently happening, identify which previous findings remain valid, and determine whether the next target condition still makes sense. The challenge may remain unchanged even when the immediate process conditions have changed, but that decision should be based on updated evidence.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a scenario in which a production line undergoes an equipment change between two improvement cycles and identify the information that must be reassessed.

Key point:
- Historical learning is useful but must be checked against current reality.
- Reassess the current condition when significant changes occur.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
34. Q: How can an organization make summary reflection a consistent part of its continuous improvement culture?
A: Show answer
Organizations can incorporate summary reflection into their standard Improvement Kata routines by defining clear reflection triggers, assigning responsibility to the learner and coach, preserving relevant experiment records, and ensuring that the next cycle begins with updated knowledge. Managers should demonstrate that honest reflection is expected even when a target condition is not achieved. Coaching routines should reinforce the importance of examining both results and scientific-thinking behavior. Organizations can also monitor whether reflection findings are translated into practical development actions. The purpose is not to create another administrative reporting requirement but to establish a repeatable management behavior that strengthens learning and adaptation throughout the organization.

Study instruction:
- Design a standard operating routine describing who conducts the reflection, when it occurs, what evidence is reviewed, and how the next cycle is initiated.

Key point:
- Consistent reflection supports organizational learning.
- Management behavior must reinforce honest experimentation and continuous development.

Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
35. Q: How can summary reflection be used to improve knowledge transfer between learners and coaches?
A: Show answer
Summary reflection provides a structured opportunity to communicate what the learner discovered and how the coach contributed to the learning process. The learner can explain important process observations, experimental findings, and changes in their problem-solving approach. The coach can identify development progress, recurring weaknesses, and effective coaching techniques. These insights can be documented and used during subsequent coaching relationships or when responsibility for the process changes. Knowledge transfer should preserve the reasoning behind important findings rather than merely transferring a list of completed actions. This reduces dependence on individual memory and helps maintain continuity in improvement activities.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a handover note that explains the previous target condition, important learning, unresolved obstacles, and recommended next development priorities.

Key point:
- Transfer the reasoning and learning behind improvement activities, not only the recorded results.
- Reflection can strengthen continuity when responsibilities change.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
36. Q: What is meant by Starter Kata, and why does it matter when conducting a summary reflection?
A: Show answer
Starter Kata are structured practice routines designed to help people develop scientific-thinking skills and habits. In the Improvement Kata, they provide a repeatable pattern for understanding a challenge, grasping the current condition, establishing a target condition, and experimenting toward it. In the Coaching Kata, they provide a repeatable coaching pattern for helping learners develop those behaviors. Summary reflection is important because it allows participants to examine how effectively they practiced these routines and what they should improve. The routines are not intended to become rigid procedures that prevent adaptation. As people internalize the underlying scientific-thinking patterns, they can adapt their application to the needs of their organization and operating environment.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish between following a practice routine mechanically and developing the underlying scientific-thinking capability.

Key point:
- Starter Kata develop repeatable scientific-thinking habits.
- The ultimate purpose is to internalize and appropriately adapt the underlying patterns.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
37. Q: How can an organization distinguish between following the Starter Kata and blindly following a prescribed procedure?
A: Show answer
Following the Starter Kata means deliberately practicing a repeatable pattern that develops scientific thinking. Blindly following a procedure means performing prescribed steps without understanding their purpose or adapting decisions to actual evidence. A learner should understand why the current condition must be investigated, why target conditions need to be specific, why experiments require predictions, and why actual outcomes must be examined. As the underlying patterns become more familiar, participants can adapt their application while preserving the essential scientific-thinking principles. Summary reflection helps identify whether the routines are developing genuine capability or merely producing procedural compliance.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate an improvement activity and identify whether participants are applying the underlying reasoning or simply completing a checklist.

Key point:
- The objective is scientific-thinking capability, not mechanical compliance.
- Adapt the routine while preserving its underlying learning principles.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
38. Q: What is the relationship between summary reflection and organizational adaptability?
A: Show answer
Organizational adaptability depends on the ability to learn from actual conditions, recognize unexpected developments, test responses, and modify future actions based on evidence. Summary reflection strengthens this capability by making learning from completed improvement cycles explicit. It helps organizations identify ineffective assumptions, recognize new process constraints, and improve the routines used to respond to changing conditions. Instead of treating every unexpected outcome as a disruption to an established plan, the organization can use it as an opportunity to improve understanding and decision-making. Repeated reflection and experimentation can therefore support a working culture that is better able to respond to uncertainty and changing operational requirements.

Study instruction:
- Identify an operational change and explain how repeated Improvement Kata cycles and summary reflections could help an organization adapt.

Key point:
- Adaptability depends on learning and adjusting based on actual conditions.
- Reflection helps convert unexpected outcomes into improved future decisions.

Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
39. Q: How can summary reflection be applied to a manufacturing improvement scenario?
A: Show answer
Consider a manufacturing team whose target condition is to reduce machine changeover time from 60 minutes to 35 minutes within six weeks. At the achieve-by date, the team has reduced the average to 42 minutes. The learner and coach should conduct the summary reflection rather than extending the original deadline automatically. They should review the experiments conducted, compare expected and actual results, identify which changeover activities produced improvements, and investigate the obstacles preventing further reduction. They should also assess the learner's ability to observe the process, formulate hypotheses, and evaluate experimental evidence. The next cycle should use this learning to establish a new target condition and determine which obstacle should be investigated next.

Study instruction:
- Write a reflection covering the original target, actual outcome, experimental learning, unresolved obstacles, and proposed next target condition.

Key point:
- Partial achievement can still generate valuable learning.
- Use actual experimental evidence to determine the next improvement step.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
40. Q: How can summary reflection be applied to an administrative or office-process improvement scenario?
A: Show answer
Consider an organization attempting to reduce invoice-processing time from an average of eight working days to four working days. At the achieve-by date, the average processing time has decreased to five days. The learner and coach should review the experiments conducted, including changes to document verification, approval routing, and invoice tracking. They should identify which changes contributed to the improvement, whether the measurements were reliable, and what remaining obstacles prevent achievement of the target condition. They should also assess whether the learner used evidence to distinguish between delays caused by incomplete documents and delays caused by internal approvals. The next cycle can then focus on a specific unresolved obstacle rather than introducing broad changes without sufficient investigation.

Study instruction:
- Develop a sample reflection for an invoice-processing improvement cycle using processing-time data and documented experiments.

Key point:
- The Improvement Kata can be applied to administrative processes as well as manufacturing.
- Investigate specific process obstacles instead of assuming their causes.

Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
41. Q: How can summary reflection be applied when an improvement cycle produces inconsistent results?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should investigate the variability rather than relying exclusively on average performance. They should review the timing and conditions of experiments, identify whether different operators or shifts produced different results, and examine whether external factors influenced the observations. They should also assess whether the measurement method was consistent and whether the experiment was sufficiently controlled to support meaningful conclusions. If the results are inconsistent, the learner should avoid claiming that an improvement has been established without adequate evidence. The next target condition or experiment should address the uncertainty revealed during the reflection.

Study instruction:
- Analyze a process with fluctuating performance and identify what additional observations or experiments are needed before drawing conclusions.

Key point:
- Variability must be investigated rather than hidden by averages.
- Uncertain results should inform subsequent experiments.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
42. Q: How can a summary reflection help identify weaknesses in the learner's understanding of the current condition?
A: Show answer
The learner and coach should examine whether the original process analysis accurately represented actual operating conditions. They should consider whether data was collected directly, whether measurements covered sufficient operating periods, whether relevant process variations were considered, and whether the learner distinguished observed facts from assumptions. If experiments repeatedly produced unexpected results because the initial understanding was incomplete, the reflection should identify that weakness. The learner may need to improve process observation, data collection, or the ability to recognize relationships between process variables. The next cycle should begin with an updated understanding rather than relying on an inaccurate description of the previous current condition.

Study instruction:
- Review a current-condition analysis and identify unsupported assumptions, missing measurements, and possible sources of bias.

Key point:
- Accurate understanding of the current condition supports better target conditions and experiments.
- Unexpected results may reveal weaknesses in the original process analysis.

Source links:
- Improvement Kata – Scientific Pattern
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
43. Q: How can summary reflection strengthen the relationship between learner and coach?
A: Show answer
A well-conducted reflection creates an opportunity for both participants to discuss their experiences openly, recognize effective behaviors, and identify development needs. The learner can explain where coaching was helpful and where additional guidance may be needed. The coach can explain what they observed about the learner's reasoning and suggest specific ways to strengthen scientific-thinking skills. Both participants should approach the discussion as contributors to a shared learning process rather than treating it as a one-sided evaluation. Clear and respectful discussion can improve mutual understanding and help establish appropriate expectations for the next improvement cycle.

Study instruction:
- Prepare questions that allow both the learner and coach to reflect on their own contribution to the improvement process.

Key point:
- Summary reflection is a joint learning activity.
- Both participants have development responsibilities.

Source links:
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
44. Q: How can an organization measure whether summary reflections are improving its Improvement Kata practice?
A: Show answer
The organization can examine whether reflection findings lead to observable improvements in subsequent cycles. Relevant indicators include more accurate current-condition analysis, clearer target conditions, better-defined experiments, stronger predictions, more reliable observation, improved identification of obstacles, and greater learner independence. The organization can also assess whether coaches become more consistent in using the Coaching Kata and whether recurring weaknesses are addressed. These indicators should be used to support learning rather than create an incentive to manipulate results. Counting the number of reflection meetings alone does not demonstrate that scientific-thinking capability has improved. The more meaningful question is whether the quality of improvement behavior changes over time.

Study instruction:
- Develop a simple review framework that tracks observable improvement behaviors across successive cycles.

Key point:
- Measure changes in improvement capability, not merely completion of reflection meetings.
- Evaluate whether reflection findings influence future behavior.

Source links:
- Lean Enterprise Institute – Kata
- The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9
45. Q: What is the most important lesson from Chapter 9 of The Toyota Kata Practice Guide?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that reaching a target condition or reaching its achieve-by date should trigger deliberate reflection before beginning the next improvement cycle. The learner and coach should examine both the positive and negative aspects of their Improvement Kata and Coaching Kata practice, identify the skills they want to develop, and carry the resulting learning forward. The achieve-by date should not be casually extended to avoid an unexpected outcome. After reflection, the learner's storyboard is normally reset, and the improvement process returns to understanding the direction, grasping the current condition, and establishing the next target condition. The deeper purpose of the Starter Kata is to develop scientific-thinking skills and habits that participants can eventually adapt to their own organizational circumstances.

Study instruction:
- Summarize Chapter 9 in your own words and explain the relationship between reflection, learning, storyboard resetting, and the next Improvement Kata cycle.

Key point:
- Reflect at the target-condition achievement or the achieve-by date, whichever occurs first.
- Use learning from the completed cycle to improve the next cycle.
- Develop scientific-thinking capability rather than merely completing prescribed routines.

Source links:
- Mike Rother, The Toyota Kata Practice Guide – Chapter 9: The Summary Reflection
- McGraw Hill – The Toyota Kata Practice Guide
- Improvement Kata Handbook – Executing Phase
Rows: 45
Table 9
Coaching Kata: Running Effective Coaching Cycles
1. Q: What is the overall purpose of a Coaching Kata cycle?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle reviews how the learner is practicing the Improvement Kata and provides useful feedback on the learner’s procedure. The coach checks whether the learner is following a scientific pattern of thinking and acting, then reinforces sound practice or makes a course correction where needed. The purpose is not to supply the solution to the process problem or judge the learner’s personal worth. A cycle should help the learner become more capable of observing, testing, learning, and choosing the next step independently. Feedback may include limited content help, such as showing how to construct a run chart or organize a storyboard, but it should not replace the learner’s own investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between coaching the learner’s Improvement Kata procedure and solving the process problem for the learner."
Key point:
- The coach develops the learner’s scientific practice, not dependence on the coach’s answers.
- Useful feedback is based on observed behavior and supports the next learning step.
Source links:
2. Q: What does it mean to coach within the corridor of scientific thinking and acting?
A: Show answer
The coach observes whether the learner is practicing the procedure specified by the Improvement Kata: clarifying a target condition, understanding the current condition through facts, identifying obstacles, experimenting at the knowledge threshold, and learning from results. The corridor is a disciplined way of proceeding, not a demand that the learner already know the correct answer. When the learner drifts into unsupported assumptions, premature solutions, broad action lists, or untested implementation, the coach gives a specific correction that returns the work to observation and experimentation. The learner remains responsible for discovering how the process works and what will move it toward the target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Review the Improvement Kata sequence and identify behaviors that indicate scientific practice versus solution-jumping."
Key point:
- The coach protects the process of learning rather than prescribing the answer.
- Course corrections should return the learner to observable facts, hypotheses, and experiments.
Source links:
3. Q: How should feedback be focused on the learner’s practice rather than personal characteristics?
A: Show answer
Feedback should describe what the coach observed the learner doing or not doing, and explain how that behavior affects the Improvement Kata procedure. For example, say that the current-condition chart has not been updated with recent measurements, rather than calling the learner careless. The first statement is specific, verifiable, and actionable; the second judges a personal trait and gives little guidance. The coach should avoid labels about intelligence, attitude, confidence, or ability. Keeping feedback on practice makes correction less threatening and helps the learner understand exactly what to change in the next attempt.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite personal judgments as factual observations about a specific action, record, question, or experiment."
Key point:
- Describe observable practice, not the learner’s identity or character.
- Specific feedback gives the learner something concrete to modify.
Source links:
4. Q: What is reinforcing feedback, and when should a coach use it?
A: Show answer
Reinforcing feedback identifies a part of the learner’s practice that was done well and encourages the learner to strengthen that behavior. It should be specific about what was effective and why it supports scientific thinking. For example, the coach might note that the learner wrote a prediction before the experiment and then compared it with measured results. Simply saying “good job” is less useful because it does not identify the behavior worth repeating. Reinforcement is appropriate when the learner has followed the method correctly, particularly when a beginner is building a new habit and needs to recognize which part of the routine is sound.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare two examples of reinforcing feedback that name the observed behavior and explain its value."
Key point:
- Praise the practice precisely so the learner knows what to repeat.
- Reinforcement strengthens useful habits without evaluating personal worth.
Source links:
5. Q: What is corrective feedback, and how should it be delivered?
A: Show answer
Corrective feedback identifies a specific aspect of the learner’s practice that should be changed to avoid developing an unhelpful habit. The coach should state what was observed, explain why it is a weakness in the Improvement Kata procedure, and give a clear instruction for proceeding differently. For example, if the learner describes a solution as an obstacle, the coach can point out that this is an intended countermeasure rather than the process problem and ask what observable problem the proposed solution is meant to address. Correction should be purposeful and proportionate, not a general criticism or an attempt to take over the work.
Study instruction:
- "Practice a three-part correction: observation, reason the behavior weakens the method, and specific alternative."
Key point:
- Correct a defined behavior, not the person.
- Explain the rationale so the learner understands the thinking pattern behind the correction.
Source links:
6. Q: What are the two categories of feedback provided during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The chapter distinguishes small, immediate corrective input during the dialogue from forward-looking feedback about the learner’s next step. Immediate input is a brief pause-and-fix intervention when the learner is responding in an unscientific way to one of the Coaching Kata questions. Forward-looking feedback is the main adjustment made when the current knowledge threshold is recognized and the coach helps improve the design of the next experiment. Both can occur in one cycle, especially with a beginner, but they serve different purposes: one corrects the current practice in the moment, while the other shapes the next learning action.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of coaching feedback as immediate dialogue correction or next-step feedback."
Key point:
- Immediate feedback corrects how the learner is practicing now.
- Forward-looking feedback improves the next experiment at the knowledge threshold.
Source links:
7. Q: When should a coach pause the coaching cycle to correct the learner’s response?
A: Show answer
Pause when the learner’s current response displays unscientific thinking or behavior that could become a bad habit if allowed to continue. Examples include presenting an assumption as a fact, treating an action item as an obstacle, or describing a prediction only after the result is known. The coach should briefly identify the issue, explain the adjustment, and rehearse the response again. This is analogous to interrupting a music practice to correct a faulty movement before it becomes ingrained. The interruption should be short and directly related to the current Coaching Kata question, not an invitation to launch into a lengthy lecture.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three response errors that should be corrected immediately rather than allowed to pass unchallenged."
Key point:
- Correct important unscientific responses before they become habits.
- A pause should be brief, relevant, and instructional.
Source links:
8. Q: How should a coach use the pause-and-restart technique without making it punitive?
A: Show answer
After explaining the correction, the coach should return to the last Coaching Kata question and ask the learner to try that part again; if necessary, the dialogue can restart from an earlier question. The tone should be friendly and matter-of-fact, similar to saying, “Let’s fix this now,” rather than signaling failure or embarrassment. The purpose is rehearsal: the learner immediately practices the desired response while the context is still clear. The coach should not use the technique to display authority, shame the learner, or demand perfect wording. The learner needs to experience correction as part of skill development.
Study instruction:
- "Rehearse a neutral restart phrase and use it to correct one example of an unsupported claim."
Key point:
- Restarting embeds the desired response through immediate practice.
- The intervention should feel supportive, not punitive.
Source links:
9. Q: Why should a coach avoid interrupting the learner too frequently?
A: Show answer
Although immediate correction prevents bad habits, repeated interruptions can fragment the dialogue, overload the learner, and make the cycle feel like an examination. The coach should choose priorities rather than correct every minor imperfection. A useful priority is the first significant weakness that affects the quality of later answers, particularly when it appears at the current knowledge threshold. The coach should distinguish a procedural error that needs immediate rehearsal from a less important wording issue that can wait. This preserves the flow of the five-question pattern while still protecting the essential scientific behaviors.
Study instruction:
- "Review a coaching transcript and select only the one or two corrections that materially affect the learner’s practice."
Key point:
- Correct selectively; do not turn the cycle into constant interruption.
- Prioritize weaknesses that undermine later reasoning or the next experiment.
Source links:
10. Q: When might a coach deliberately allow the learner to make a small mistake?
A: Show answer
The coach may sometimes allow an error to become a learning experience when the proposed step is small, inexpensive, quick, and relatively harmless. This is a judgment call, not a general rule to ignore poor planning. The coach should consider whether the mistake could harm a person, customer, product, service, or critical process. If the risk is material, the coach should correct the plan before the learner acts. If the likely consequence is limited and the result will reveal something useful, allowing the learner to see the effect can strengthen understanding more effectively than an extended explanation.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a proposed experiment for cost, duration, reversibility, and potential harm before deciding whether to correct it or let the learner learn from it."
Key point:
- Experiential learning is suitable only when the downside is limited.
- Safety and customer protection override the value of learning from a mistake.
Source links:
11. Q: What is the recommended structure for giving useful feedback?
A: Show answer
First describe clearly and specifically what the coach observed: the learner’s action, statement, omission, or use of evidence. Next explain the concern that the observation raises and why it matters to the Improvement Kata procedure. Then provide a purposeful instruction for how the learner should proceed differently. For example, identify that the actual-condition chart reflects measurements from two weeks earlier, explain that stale data weakens obstacle identification, and ask the learner to gather current measurements before choosing a countermeasure. This structure makes feedback understandable, evidence-based, and actionable rather than vague or authoritarian.
Study instruction:
- "Write one feedback statement using observation, rationale, and a specific next behavior."
Key point:
- Feedback should explain both what was observed and why it matters.
- A clear instruction converts feedback into a practice adjustment.
Source links:
12. Q: What is the role of a second coach during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The second coach observes the coach’s practice rather than coaching the learner directly. The second coach watches the dialogue, notes whether it follows the five-question pattern, identifies where the learner reaches a knowledge threshold, and observes where the coach notices or misses a weakness. After the cycle, the second coach gives feedback to the coach about the quality of the coaching. The second coach should not take over the learner dialogue or provide feedback to the learner during the cycle. This separation allows the coach to develop coaching skill while keeping the learner’s relationship with the primary coach clear.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between the learner, primary coach, and second coach, including who receives feedback from whom."
Key point:
- The second coach develops the coach, not the learner.
- Observation should focus on the dialogue pattern, threshold, and coach’s interventions.
Source links:
13. Q: What should a second coach observe during the dialogue?
A: Show answer
A second coach should watch whether the dialogue follows the five-question pattern, where the learner’s knowledge threshold appears, and what the point of weakness is. The observer should notice where the learner’s procedure departs from the Improvement Kata, where the dialogue becomes unstructured, and what the coach does or does not recognize. These prompts keep observation focused on the process rather than on personalities or general impressions. The second coach should record concrete moments that can be discussed afterward, such as a stale chart that goes unchallenged or a proposed solution that the coach allows to become the next action.
Study instruction:
- "Use the six observation prompts from the chapter to take notes during a practice cycle."
Key point:
- Observe specific moments in the dialogue, not just its overall impression.
- The coach’s noticing and response are central subjects for second-coach feedback.
Source links:
14. Q: What did Tina and Dan’s example reveal about a weak coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
Dan’s current-condition information was not current, his obstacle description was vague, and he moved quickly toward automation as a possible solution. Tina asked useful questions but continued probing an obstacle that Dan did not yet understand, then accepted a broad next step to ask IT to examine the process. The proposed step did not define a precise test, a measurable prediction, or a short learning interval. The example shows how an early weakness can affect later questions: when current data are unreliable, obstacle identification becomes speculative and the next experiment becomes difficult to design.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the chain from outdated actual-condition data to vague obstacles and an oversized proposed step."
Key point:
- A weak early answer can undermine the rest of the coaching cycle.
- Do not accept a solution-oriented action as a well-designed experiment.
Source links:
15. Q: Where was Dan’s threshold of knowledge in the example, and why?
A: Show answer
The clearest threshold appeared when Dan could not identify the obstacle beyond calling data entry complex and saying it differed from order to order. He did not yet know what specifically caused the time variation. A related weakness appeared earlier because the latest measurements were two weeks old, so the current process condition was uncertain. These gaps were connected: without fresh data, Dan could not reliably identify the obstacles, and without a specific obstacle he could not state a measurable prediction. The right response was not to pressure him for an answer but to recognize what was not yet known and plan an investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the first point in a coaching dialogue where the learner can no longer support an answer with current facts."
Key point:
- A genuine “I don’t know yet” can define the knowledge threshold.
- Fresh process evidence may be a prerequisite for identifying specific obstacles.
Source links:
16. Q: Why should coaching feedback focus on the first point of weakness in the dialogue?
A: Show answer
The five Coaching Kata questions are nested and progressively narrow the discussion, much like a funnel. If the learner has not answered an earlier question well enough, later answers are likely to be weaker because they depend on the earlier information. For example, stale current-condition data can make the obstacle list unreliable; vague obstacles then make the next experiment vague. Correcting a downstream answer without fixing its upstream basis may create a superficial plan that cannot produce useful learning. The coach should therefore identify the earliest consequential weakness and address it before moving further into the sequence.
Study instruction:
- "Map the dependencies between target condition, current condition, obstacles, next experiment, and follow-up timing."
Key point:
- The first consequential weakness often determines the quality of later answers.
- Correct upstream information before refining downstream plans.
Source links:
17. Q: What should a coach do when the learner reaches the knowledge threshold before Question 4?
A: Show answer
The coach should not feel obliged to complete every remaining question in sequence. Once the threshold is clear, the coach and learner can move directly to planning the next step under Question 4. If the learner does not know the current process condition, the next step may be to observe and measure it. If the obstacle is unclear, the next step may be to investigate the process and gather evidence. The cycle should focus on what needs to be learned next, agree on a single step, and then set the next coaching time. The five-question pattern is a guide to disciplined thinking, not a rigid script that prevents useful action.
Study instruction:
- "Practice identifying a knowledge threshold at different points in the dialogue and formulate a direct next step."
Key point:
- Move to Question 4 when the threshold is reached, even if earlier questions remain unresolved.
- The next experiment should address the point of weakness that limits learning.
Source links:
18. Q: How should a coach put a beginner learner at ease before starting the five questions?
A: Show answer
Begin with a greeting and a brief human exchange rather than immediately interrogating the learner with the five questions. When possible, stand beside the learner and face the storyboard together instead of sitting directly opposite one another. With a new learner, explain that the coaching cycle is a practice routine intended to develop skill, not a test of whether the learner has done something wrong. Normalize being a beginner and acknowledge that the coach is also practicing coaching. This creates a setting in which uncertainty and mistakes can be discussed without being interpreted as personal failure.
Study instruction:
- "Plan a brief opening that explains the purpose of the cycle and normalizes learning through practice."
Key point:
- Trust and clarity help learners participate openly.
- The coaching cycle is a dialogue for development, not a performance judgment.
Source links:
19. Q: Why is it useful to stand beside the learner and look at the storyboard together?
A: Show answer
Standing beside the learner directs both people toward the process evidence and the shared work rather than creating a face-to-face contest. The storyboard becomes the common reference point for the target condition, current data, obstacle parking lot, and experimenting record. This physical arrangement can reduce the feeling that the coach is judging the learner and encourages both to examine the same facts. It also makes it natural for the learner to point to the relevant information and for the coach to ask “show me” questions. The arrangement is a practical way to reinforce that the discussion concerns the process and the learner’s method.
Study instruction:
- "Set up a practice coaching cycle with both participants oriented toward the storyboard and its evidence."
Key point:
- A shared visual reference supports a collaborative, evidence-focused dialogue.
- The learner should point to the records rather than rely only on verbal summaries.
Source links:
20. Q: Why should the learner restate the challenge before the five-question dialogue?
A: Show answer
The learner should name the focus process and the overarching challenge so that the target condition is understood in the context of the larger objective. The challenge anchors the cycle and explains why the learner is working on this particular process. Without that connection, the target condition can become an isolated local improvement that does not contribute to the wider value stream or organizational direction. Restating the challenge is brief, but it helps the coach confirm that the learner’s work remains aligned with the intended direction before examining the details of the current cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Practice stating the focus process, overarching challenge, and link between them in a concise opening."
Key point:
- The challenge supplies the larger direction for the learner’s work.
- The coaching dialogue should remain anchored to the purpose of the improvement effort.
Source links:
21. Q: Why should Question 1 about the target condition be asked in every coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
Question 1 frames the dialogue by reminding the learner and coach of the desired process condition. Even when the target has not changed, repeating it ensures that later discussion about current performance, experiments, and obstacles remains connected to the intended destination. Skipping the question can allow the cycle to drift into reviewing activities or discussing problems without a clear reference point. The learner should point to the target-condition form and read what is written there. The few seconds required are worthwhile because the target condition provides the standard against which the current condition and next learning step are understood.
Study instruction:
- "Include a target-condition review in a simulated cycle even when the target is unchanged."
Key point:
- The target condition anchors all later questions.
- A short repeated review prevents the dialogue from drifting into unrelated activity.
Source links:
22. Q: What should a well-formed target condition describe?
A: Show answer
A target condition should describe a measurable destination for how the focus process is intended to operate. It should make the desired process pattern and relevant sequence understandable, rather than prescribe a project activity or a particular countermeasure. It should not be written as a list of verbs, an action item, or a statement that something will be absent. For example, “install a new system” describes an implementation action, while a target condition describes the desired operating pattern after improvement. The learner should be able to explain the condition clearly enough that the coach can understand what the process will look like when the target is achieved.
Study instruction:
- "Review a draft target condition and remove implementation tasks, vague wording, and descriptions framed only as absence."
Key point:
- Describe the desired process state, not the means of implementing it.
- A target condition must be understandable and measurable.
Source links:
23. Q: How should outcome metrics and process metrics be used in a target condition?
A: Show answer
The target condition should include an outcome metric and a process metric that together indicate whether the condition has been achieved. The outcome metric shows the result that matters, while the process metric describes an observable characteristic of how the focus process operates. The process metric should be directly observable at the process itself, not inferred only from a distant result. For example, a service process might track both customer delivery performance and the time or sequence of specific process steps. The coach should ask what each metric measures, what value is intended, and how the learner will know the target pattern has been reached.
Study instruction:
- "For a process you know, define one outcome measure and one directly observable process measure."
Key point:
- Outcome and process measures answer different but complementary questions.
- The process metric must be observable at the focus process.
Source links:
24. Q: How can a coach test whether the target condition is clear and measurable?
A: Show answer
The coach should ask the learner to read the target condition as written, describe the intended process pattern and sequence, identify the achieve-by date, and explain the relevant metric values. Questions such as “What do you want to be happening?” and “How will you know?” reveal whether the statement describes a real destination. The coach should also be able to understand the condition without having to guess what the learner means.
