If the learner cannot explain it clearly, the target condition is probably not yet sufficiently defined. Asking the learner to run through the numbers can expose vague language or missing measures.
Study instruction:
- "Use the chapter’s target-condition prompts to test a draft for clarity, observable pattern, measures, and date."
Key point:
- If the coach cannot understand the target, the learner may not understand it either.
- Numbers and observable process details make the target testable.
Source links:
25. Q: What are common mistakes in defining the target condition?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include failing to prepare the storyboard, describing a solution or countermeasure instead of a desired process state, defining the target as removal of an obstacle, using only an outcome metric, and writing a vague or unmeasurable statement. A target such as “no delays” is not enough because it does not specify the operating pattern or how performance will be measured. Likewise, “implement training” is an action rather than a destination. The coach should help the learner rewrite the statement in terms of what should be happening, how the process should flow, what metrics will show the condition, and by when it should be achieved.
Study instruction:
- "Diagnose sample target-condition statements and classify each error before rewriting the statement."
Key point:
- A solution, action, or absence statement is not a target condition.
- The target needs an observable process pattern and suitable measures.
Source links:
26. Q: What should the learner present when answering Question 2 about the actual condition?
A: Show answer
The learner should describe how the focus process is operating now, using recent direct observation and measurement. At minimum, the current values of the process metric and outcome metric should be available and ideally shown in run charts on the storyboard. After the initial cycle, the learner should not simply repeat the original baseline; the description must reflect the condition now, after any changes made. The learner should point to the evidence and explain how it was obtained. The coach should check that the actual condition is genuinely current and can be compared directly with the target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare current process and outcome measurements before a practice cycle and display them in a form that can be compared with the target."
Key point:
- Question 2 concerns the process as it operates now, not its initial baseline.
- Current measurements are essential for reliable obstacle identification.
Source links:
27. Q: Why must current-condition data be updated before every coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The process may change as experiments and countermeasures are tried, so an old description can quickly stop representing reality. If the learner relies on measurements from an earlier period, the coach cannot know whether the current gap is still the same or whether new variation has appeared. Stale data can lead to incorrect obstacle identification and poorly designed experiments. The chapter’s example shows this dependency: Dan’s measurements were two weeks old, which made his statements about current data entry performance uncertain. Updated measurements keep the cycle grounded in the present process and allow the next experiment to be based on what is actually happening.
Study instruction:
- "Check the date, coverage, and source of each metric before using it to describe the actual condition."
Key point:
- An old chart is not automatically a current-condition chart.
- Data freshness affects the reliability of all subsequent reasoning.
Source links:
28. Q: How is Question 2 different from reviewing the actions the learner has taken?
A: Show answer
Question 2 is not an activity report. The learner should describe the operating condition of the focus process relative to the target, not list steps completed, meetings held, or countermeasures attempted. Those activities may matter elsewhere, but they do not establish how the process is performing now. For example, saying “we installed a new template” does not show whether processing time or process variation changed. The learner needs to show the current process metrics and relevant observations. This distinction prevents the coaching cycle from becoming a status meeting focused on task completion rather than scientific understanding.
Study instruction:
- "Convert an activity-based status update into a current-condition description supported by process evidence."
Key point:
- Activities are not substitutes for evidence of process performance.
- Question 2 asks how the process is operating now.
Source links:
29. Q: Why should the coach ask the learner to point to the storyboard and supporting records?
A: Show answer
Having the learner point to the target-condition form, run charts, block diagram, and other relevant records makes the discussion evidence-based and helps both participants inspect the same information. Verbal summaries can conceal missing data, outdated measures, or unsupported interpretations. The storyboard also provides a consistent sequence for the coaching dialogue, allowing the coach to see whether information is prepared and organized around the five questions. “Show me” and “tell me more” are useful prompts because they move the discussion from assertion to observable support and encourage the learner to explain how the information was obtained.
Study instruction:
- "Run a coaching review using only information the learner can point to on the storyboard or in supporting records."
Key point:
- The storyboard should make the learner’s thinking and evidence visible.
- Asking to see the data is stronger than accepting unsupported verbal claims.
Source links:
30. Q: How should a coach distinguish scientific uncertainty from unsupported conjecture?
A: Show answer
A learner may appropriately say “I do not know yet” when the available evidence does not support a conclusion. That response identifies a knowledge threshold and can lead to an investigation. By contrast, “I think this is happening” may be unscientific if it is presented as an explanation without evidence or a plan to test it. The same statement can be useful if it is explicitly framed as a hypothesis that will be tested. The coach should ask “How can you tell?” and “What evidence supports that?” to determine whether the learner is describing facts, acknowledging uncertainty, or proposing a testable prediction.
Study instruction:
- "Classify statements as observed fact, acknowledged uncertainty, interpretation, or explicit testable hypothesis."
Key point:
- Uncertainty is acceptable when it accurately describes the evidence available.
- An opinion becomes useful as a hypothesis when it is stated for testing.
Source links:
31. Q: What are the four reflection questions asked between reviewing the actual condition and identifying the current obstacle?
A: Show answer
The learner reflects on the last experiment by answering: What was your last step? What did you expect? What actually happened? What did you learn? These questions correspond to the four columns of the experimenting record. The learner should point to the completed row and supporting data rather than rely on an informal verbal account. The first two questions establish the planned experiment and prediction; the third presents only observed facts and data; the fourth records the interpretation and learning. This structured reflection converts the results of one step into the basis for deciding what to investigate or test next.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the four reflection questions and match each one to its corresponding experimenting-record column."
Key point:
- Reflection turns experiment results into the next learning step.
- Each question has a distinct role and should not be collapsed into a general recap.
Source links:
32. Q: What information belongs in the first column of the experimenting record?
A: Show answer
The first column records the learner’s last step or the proposed next step, stated clearly enough that another person can understand what was done or will be done. It should identify the action that constitutes the experiment, not a broad list of activities or an entire implementation program. During reflection, the learner reads the step that was actually taken. During planning, the learner writes the next single step before carrying it out. The coach can ask what knowledge threshold the step addressed and what was being tested. A precise description makes it possible to evaluate whether the experiment addressed the current obstacle.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a broad action list as one specific step that can be completed and examined."
Key point:
- The record should capture a defined step, not a bundle of unrelated tasks.
- A clear step allows the coach to assess its connection to the obstacle.
Source links:
33. Q: What belongs in the second column of the experimenting record, and when must it be written?
A: Show answer
The second column records the learner’s expectation or prediction about the effect of the step, including what the learner expects to happen or learn and how the effect will be measured. It must be written before the step is taken, so the prediction is not altered after the outcome is known. The coach should have checked this during the previous cycle. A prediction is not merely a restatement of the action; it describes an expected result that can be compared with what actually happens. This advance commitment creates the possibility of useful surprise and makes the experiment more than an unstructured attempt.
Study instruction:
- "Write a prediction before conducting a small experiment and specify the evidence that would support or refute it."
Key point:
- Predictions must precede action to avoid hindsight rewriting.
- A useful expectation describes a measurable result or learning.
Source links:
34. Q: What belongs in the third column of the experimenting record?
A: Show answer
The third column contains what actually happened: facts, measurements, and direct observations from the experiment. It should not contain interpretation, explanations, or conclusions. The learner may point to supporting records such as timing sheets, run charts, or observation notes. The coach should ask “How do you know?” and “Can you show me the data?” to ensure that the entry reflects evidence rather than memory or opinion. Keeping this column factual preserves a clear comparison between the prediction and the result, which is necessary for reliable learning.
Study instruction:
- "Review an experiment record and remove interpretations from the actual-results column, leaving only observable facts and data."
Key point:
- Column 3 records what happened, not what it means.
- Evidence should be traceable to measurements or direct observation.
Source links:
35. Q: What belongs in the fourth column of the experimenting record?
A: Show answer
The fourth column records what the learner learned from comparing the prediction with the actual result. It may describe a clearer understanding of process behavior, a revised view of an obstacle, an implication for the next step, or an honest statement that the learner still does not know. This is where interpretation belongs. It is often the most difficult column because the learner must move beyond reporting facts and explain what those facts suggest. The coach should help the learner distinguish a genuine conclusion from a repeated description of the data, without forcing a certainty the evidence does not support.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a prediction with observed results and write a separate interpretation that explains what has been learned and what remains unknown."
Key point:
- Column 4 interprets the evidence and identifies learning.
- A precise “not yet known” conclusion is more useful than an unsupported claim.
Source links:
36. Q: Why should prediction error be treated as a source of learning rather than failure?
A: Show answer
A result that differs from the learner’s prediction can reveal how the process actually operates and expose assumptions that were incomplete or wrong. The chapter emphasizes that some of the most valuable experiments produce unexpected results because they help the learner see what must be understood to reach the target condition. If the learner treats a refuted prediction as personal failure, they may respond by adding countermeasures without analyzing what happened. A no-blame, detective-like reflection makes it easier to examine the difference between expectation and evidence and to choose a more informed next step.
Study instruction:
- "Take one refuted prediction and explain what new information it provides about the process or the obstacle."
Key point:
- An experiment can be valuable even when the prediction is not confirmed.
- The learning comes from analyzing the difference, not defending the prediction.
Source links:
37. Q: How can a coach create a no-blame atmosphere during reflection?
A: Show answer
The coach should frame the review as a joint effort to understand the process, not as an assessment of whether the learner succeeded or failed. Unexpected results should be treated as occurrences that can teach something about the process and the improvement method. The coach should ask for facts, invite the learner to explain the comparison, and avoid language that labels the outcome as good or bad. This does not mean lowering expectations or abandoning the achieve-by date. It means separating the value of learning from personal judgment so the learner can continue experimenting rapidly and honestly.
Study instruction:
- "Replace evaluative comments about an experiment with neutral questions about the prediction, result, and learning."
Key point:
- No-blame reflection makes unexpected results discussable.
- A learning orientation supports rapid improvement without removing accountability.
Source links:
38. Q: Why should the learner complete the experimenting record before the coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should enter the step, prediction, actual facts, and learning before the coaching discussion. This preparation allows the learner to answer by pointing to the record rather than reconstructing events from memory during the meeting. It also gives the coach a chance to inspect whether the prediction was written in advance and whether actual results are separated from interpretation. If the record is incomplete, the reflection may become speculative and the next step may be based on a weak understanding of what happened. The coach can correct the record during the cycle when necessary, but the learner owns its preparation and content.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a completed four-column experimenting record before a practice coaching cycle and check that each column contains the right type of information."
Key point:
- Preparation protects the quality of reflection.
- The learner should bring evidence, not rely on an improvised verbal report.
Source links:
39. Q: Why should each experimenting record normally be dedicated to one obstacle?
A: Show answer
A separate record for each obstacle preserves a clear connection between the problem being investigated, the experiments performed, the predictions made, and the learning obtained. If the learner changes to a different obstacle but continues using the same record, the sequence becomes difficult to interpret and the learning history may mix unrelated issues. When the focus changes, a new record should be started. This does not mean an obstacle must be solved in one experiment; several steps may be needed to understand and overcome it. The dedicated record helps maintain continuity while keeping the investigation focused.
Study instruction:
- "Check whether the obstacle named on an experimenting record remains the same across its sequence of experiments."
Key point:
- One record normally tracks one obstacle and its learning sequence.
- Changing focus calls for a new record so evidence remains interpretable.
Source links:
40. Q: What is the purpose of the obstacles parking lot?
A: Show answer
The obstacles parking lot is a place to capture perceived and encountered obstacles while the learner works toward the target condition. It acknowledges what the learner has noticed without turning every item into an immediate task. The learner reviews the list, adds newly discovered obstacles, removes items that are no longer relevant, and marks the one currently being addressed. This reduces the pressure to solve everything at once and helps the learner see that early perceptions may change as evidence accumulates. The objective is to focus the investigation on the next obstacle that matters for progress toward the target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Update an obstacle parking lot by adding newly observed issues, crossing off resolved or irrelevant ones, and marking one current focus."
Key point:
- The parking lot stores perceived obstacles; it is not an action-item register.
- The current focus should be visible and linked to the experimenting record.
Source links:
41. Q: How should an obstacle be distinguished from an action item or a proposed solution?
A: Show answer
An obstacle is a problem or condition that stands in the way of reaching the target condition. An action item describes something someone intends to do, while a proposed solution or countermeasure describes a possible way to address a problem. For example, “lack of training” is framed as a missing remedy, whereas “some operators enter the customer details incorrectly” describes an observable process problem. “Ask IT to automate data entry” is an action or solution proposal, not an obstacle. The coach should ask what problem the proposed action would solve and guide the learner back to the specific condition that causes difficulty.
Study instruction:
- "Sort sample statements into obstacle, action item, or proposed countermeasure and rewrite any solution statements as observable problems."
Key point:
- Obstacles describe problems; steps and experiments address them.
- A proposed remedy must not be mistaken for an explanation of the obstacle.
Source links:
42. Q: Why should obstacles be specific and measurable?
A: Show answer
A specific obstacle helps the learner understand what problem is preventing progress and what evidence would show whether it is changing. Vague descriptions such as “the process is complex” do not identify which part of the process causes difficulty or how the difficulty can be observed. A measurable obstacle can be connected to a process metric, timing observation, error rate, queue, or other relevant evidence. The quality of obstacle identification also depends on the target condition: if the target is vague, obstacles will tend to be vague. When the learner cannot describe the obstacle precisely, the next step may need to be direct observation and measurement rather than another round of speculation.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite vague obstacle descriptions so that they identify a specific process condition and a way to observe or measure it."
Key point:
- Specific obstacles create a basis for testable experiments.
- Vague obstacles often signal insufficient understanding of the focus process.
Source links:
43. Q: Why is it generally better for a learner to work on one obstacle at a time?
A: Show answer
Focusing on one obstacle narrows the learner’s attention and makes it easier to design a meaningful experiment and interpret its result. Beginners in particular can become overwhelmed if they try to work on every item in the obstacle parking lot. The learner does not necessarily need to start with the largest or most important-looking obstacle; choosing one and beginning an investigation can reveal other obstacles or show that some initial perceptions were mistaken. Several experiments may be needed to overcome the chosen obstacle. The aim is focused learning, not an assumption that one action will solve the entire problem.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one obstacle from a sample parking lot and explain why it is a reasonable focus for the next learning step."
Key point:
- One-at-a-time focus supports clearer experiments and learning.
- The initial choice need not be the biggest obstacle, especially for a beginner.
Source links:
44. Q: When may a learner change the obstacle currently being addressed?
A: Show answer
The learner may shift focus when new learning shows that another obstacle is more relevant or that the current obstacle is no longer the best place to work. An experiment on one obstacle may also eliminate other obstacles or make them irrelevant. The obstacle parking lot should be updated to reflect these discoveries, and a new experimenting record should normally be used when the focus changes. The coach should not insist that the learner finish every item in the original order. The purpose is to reach the target condition through evidence-based learning, not to complete a static checklist.
Study instruction:
- "Review a changing obstacle list and decide whether the current focus should remain, be removed, or be replaced based on new evidence."
Key point:
- Focus can change when learning justifies the change.
- The obstacle list is a living record, not a fixed sequence of tasks.
Source links:
45. Q: How should a coach handle legitimate problems that are not relevant to the next target condition?
A: Show answer
The coach should acknowledge the concern and record it on a separate list outside the storyboard rather than allowing it to distract the current investigation. This assures the learner that the issue has been heard while making clear that it is not part of the present target-condition work. The coach should avoid dismissing the concern as unimportant; it may be valid but outside the current scope. If the issue involves immediate safety, legal, ethical, or other mandatory escalation, it must still be handled through the appropriate channel rather than deferred merely because it is outside the improvement focus.
Study instruction:
- "Practice separating concerns that belong to the current obstacle parking lot from valid issues that require a separate route or escalation."
Key point:
- Acknowledge out-of-scope issues without losing focus on the target condition.
- Urgent safety or compliance concerns still require their proper response.
Source links:
46. Q: What is the threshold of knowledge, and why does it determine the next experiment?
A: Show answer
The threshold of knowledge is the point at which the learner no longer has enough reliable understanding to decide what should happen next by reasoning alone. It may appear because current data are missing, an obstacle is unclear, or the cause of an observed result is not understood. The next experiment should be designed to cross that threshold by obtaining the information needed to see further. The coach should ask what needs to be learned next before discussing the action. This prevents the learner from choosing a countermeasure simply because it sounds plausible and keeps the improvement effort anchored in evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the current knowledge limit in a process example and state what information would move understanding forward."
Key point:
- The next step should be determined by what is not yet known.
- The threshold is a learning boundary, not a reason to guess.
Source links:
47. Q: Why should the learner state what needs to be learned before describing the next step?
A: Show answer
Starting with the desired learning prevents the learner from jumping directly to an implementation idea. The learner should explain the current knowledge threshold and what question needs to be answered; only then should the learner describe how to obtain that knowledge. For example, instead of saying “install an automated entry tool,” the learner might say “we need to identify which data-entry fields account for most of the observed time,” then propose a small timing and observation exercise. This sequence makes the step purposeful and allows the coach to judge whether it addresses the actual obstacle.
Study instruction:
- "For a proposed action, first write the learning question it is intended to answer, then redesign the action as an experiment."
Key point:
- Learning objective comes before the proposed action.
- A step is useful when it is designed to answer the current question.
Source links:
48. Q: What distinguishes a next experiment from an ordinary action item?
A: Show answer
An ordinary action item says what someone will do, often to implement a presumed solution. A next experiment is a deliberate step designed to test a prediction or obtain information at the current knowledge threshold. It includes what will be done, what the learner expects to happen or learn, and how the result will be measured. For example, asking IT to automate a process is an implementation request; timing several examples to determine which fields create the largest delay is an experiment. The coach should help the learner turn activity into a test that can produce evidence, including evidence that refutes the initial idea.
Study instruction:
- "Convert an implementation task into an experiment with a clear question, prediction, observation method, and measurable result."
Key point:
- An experiment is designed to learn, not merely to complete a task.
- The expected result must be explicit and testable.
Source links:
49. Q: What should the coach verify when reviewing the learner’s proposed next experiment?
A: Show answer
The coach should check that the experiment addresses the current knowledge threshold and the obstacle being worked on, that the learner has written a prediction, and that the step is measurable. The coach should ask how the test will be conducted, how data will be collected, what process or outcome metric will be used, and whether enough data points will be gathered to interpret the result. The coach should also consider whether the step is small, quick, inexpensive, and safe if the prediction is wrong. Finally, the experiment should build on previous learning rather than being an unrelated activity.
Study instruction:
- "Use the full experiment checklist to review a proposed step before approving it."
Key point:
- A sound experiment is relevant, predicted, measurable, timely, and safe.
- The design should build a chain of learning from one step to the next.
Source links:
50. Q: Why should a prediction about the next experiment be written down in advance?
A: Show answer
Writing the prediction before the experiment makes the learner’s expectation explicit and prevents the result from being used to reconstruct what was supposedly expected. The comparison between the written prediction and actual facts can reveal useful surprises, expose assumptions, and clarify the next knowledge threshold. The prediction should be specific enough to be evaluated, not simply “it will work” or a restatement of the action. The learner may predict what will happen or what they expect to learn. The coach should ensure that the prediction and measurement method are recorded before the learner takes the step.
Study instruction:
- "Write a prediction that includes an expected direction or pattern and the evidence that will be collected to test it."
Key point:
- Advance predictions make comparison and learning possible.
- A prediction should be testable rather than a vague expression of hope.
Source links:
51. Q: When is a single-factor experiment desirable, and what if it is not practical?
A: Show answer
A single-factor experiment is desirable when changing one factor at a time makes it easier to understand what caused the observed result. The coach should ask whether the learner can isolate the variable being tested without creating an artificial or unsafe situation. The chapter recognizes that this is not always possible. If several factors must change together, the learner should describe the limitation, collect enough relevant data, and avoid claiming that one factor caused the result when the design cannot support that conclusion. The aim is to make the experiment as interpretable as the situation allows, not to force a laboratory design onto every real process.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the factor being tested in a proposed experiment and state what conclusions would or would not be justified if multiple factors change."
Key point:
- Isolating one factor can strengthen causal learning.
- When isolation is impractical, be explicit about the limits of interpretation.
Source links:
52. Q: How should the coach assess whether an experiment is measurable?
A: Show answer
The learner must be able to use facts and data to determine whether the prediction was supported, refuted, or remained inconclusive. The coach should ask what will be measured, how observations will be recorded, which process or outcome metric applies, and how many data points are needed. A vague step such as “talk to people and see what they think” may produce impressions but not necessarily evidence related to the obstacle. A measurable experiment might involve timing a defined set of process cycles, recording the sequence of work, or counting a specified type of error. The measurement method should be practical and proportionate to the question.
Study instruction:
- "For one proposed experiment, specify the unit of observation, metric, data source, and minimum evidence needed to evaluate the prediction."
Key point:
- If the learner cannot tell what happened from evidence, the experiment is poorly specified.
- Measurement should be matched to the question being tested.
Source links:
53. Q: Why must an experiment be designed to avoid harm if the prediction is wrong?
A: Show answer
Experiments should be arranged so that an unexpected result does not create an unsafe condition or damage the customer process. The chapter describes this as limiting the blast radius. Before approving a step, the coach should ask what could happen if the prediction fails and whether the test can be contained, reversed, or conducted on a small scale. A trial that could compromise safety, quality, service continuity, or customer commitments needs additional safeguards or a different design. Learning is not a justification for exposing people or customers to avoidable harm. The size of the experiment should be proportional to the risk and the uncertainty being investigated.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a simple failure-impact review for a proposed experiment and redesign any step that could harm people or customers."
Key point:
- Safety and customer protection are design constraints, not afterthoughts.
- Small, contained tests limit the consequences of an incorrect prediction.
Source links:
54. Q: Why does the chapter recommend small and rapid experiments?
A: Show answer
Small experiments focus on the current knowledge threshold and allow the learner to obtain information without committing prematurely to a broad solution. Large steps can overshoot what needs to be learned, combine too many activities, consume more resources, and make it difficult to identify what produced the result. A rapid test shortens the time between uncertainty and evidence, enabling the next coaching cycle to build on fresh learning. The coach can ask “What can we learn today?” to narrow an oversized proposal. Small does not mean careless; the experiment still needs a prediction, a measurement plan, and appropriate safeguards.
Study instruction:
- "Take a large proposed improvement and reduce it to the smallest safe experiment that can answer the current learning question."
Key point:
- Small tests accelerate learning and reduce unnecessary commitment.
- A rapid experiment still requires deliberate design and measurement.
Source links:
55. Q: Why can infrequent coaching cycles lead learners to plan experiments that are too large?
A: Show answer
When the next coaching meeting is far away, a learner may feel pressure to complete many activities before the coach returns. The learner can then bundle several actions into one oversized step, making it harder to isolate what was learned and increasing the risk of implementing assumptions without review. Frequent cycles create a shorter feedback loop and encourage the learner to take one small step, examine the result, and choose the next one. The chapter notes that daily cycles often help keep experiments small, although the exact cadence should fit the process and practical circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Review the planned interval between cycles and identify whether it encourages one testable step or a bundle of unreviewed activities."
Key point:
- Long gaps can encourage oversized steps and weaken learning discipline.
- Short feedback loops help maintain focus on one experiment at a time.
Source links:
56. Q: Why is “go and see” a legitimate next experiment?
A: Show answer
Direct observation can be the experiment when the learner’s knowledge threshold concerns how the process actually operates. Going to the focus process, watching work, timing steps, asking clarifying questions, or collecting a sample of events can reveal facts that are not visible from reports or assumptions. The next step does not always need to change the process; it may need to improve understanding of the process. The learner should still define what they intend to observe, what they expect to learn, and how observations will be recorded. This makes “go and see” a purposeful investigation rather than an unstructured visit.
Study instruction:
- "Design a short direct-observation experiment with a defined process, observation period, and recording method."
Key point:
- Gathering information can be the next experiment.
- Direct observation helps replace assumptions with facts about actual work.
Source links:
57. Q: What should happen if the learner needs more analysis before taking the proposed experiment?
A: Show answer
The next step may be additional analysis or preparation if the learner cannot yet design a useful test. For example, the learner may need to clarify the process sequence, identify the relevant metric, locate the data source, or understand the obstacle more precisely. The coach should not force an immediate trial simply to keep the cycle moving. However, the analysis itself should be defined as a bounded step with a clear learning purpose and a prompt turnaround. Once the necessary information is obtained, the learner can design the next experiment at the newly clarified knowledge threshold.
Study instruction:
- "Define a limited analysis step that will produce the information needed to design a testable experiment."
Key point:
- Preparation can be a valid next step when it removes a design uncertainty.
- Keep analysis focused and time-bounded so it leads to action and learning.
Source links:
58. Q: How much detail should the coach discuss about the learner’s next experiment?
A: Show answer
The coach should discuss enough detail to ensure that the next single step is clear, relevant, measurable, and safe. The learner should read the proposed step and prediction from the experimenting record, after which the coach can ask about execution, measurement, data collection, and risk. The coach should accept the proposal or help fine-tune it. Once the next step is clear, the cycle should not expand into a long discussion of every future action or speculative outcome. The learner needs to take the step so that new evidence can reveal what comes next.
Study instruction:
- "Practice ending a planning discussion once one well-designed next experiment is clear, without developing a premature action roadmap."
Key point:
- Plan one next step, not a full sequence of imagined future steps.
- Further visibility comes from taking the experiment and examining its result.
Source links:
59. Q: What is the purpose of Question 5 in the Coaching Kata?
A: Show answer
Question 5 prepares the next coaching cycle and creates a fast turnaround for learning. It is not primarily a request to ask when the learner will have completed a long task. The coach and learner should agree when they will next meet to examine what has been learned from the next step, and what information or data the learner will prepare on the storyboard. The next meeting is often the normal scheduled cycle, but an additional cycle can be arranged soon after an experiment, especially for a beginner. The goal is to avoid leaving the learner to perform several unreviewed steps before the next coaching conversation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain Question 5 as a learning-review commitment rather than a task-completion deadline."
Key point:
- Question 5 asks when the coach and learner can see what has been learned.
- The next cycle should follow the experiment quickly enough to sustain the learning loop.
Source links:
60. Q: How should the coach distinguish a learning orientation from a task orientation in Question 5?
A: Show answer
A task-oriented question asks when the learner will have something completed or what will have been accomplished. A learning-oriented question asks when the coach and learner can see what has been learned from taking the next step. The distinction matters because the Improvement Kata advances through evidence and learning, not simply through a growing list of completed activities. For example, “When will the automation be finished?” assumes implementation is the goal; “When can we review what the timing observations reveal?” keeps attention on knowledge. The coach should phrase Question 5 so the learner understands that the next meeting is about learning.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite task-completion questions as learning-review questions that specify the evidence to be examined."
Key point:
- Completion is not the same as learning.
- Question 5 should reinforce the purpose of the next experiment.
Source links:
61. Q: What must be agreed before ending a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The coach and learner should agree on a specific date and time for the next coaching cycle and on the data or information the learner must obtain and record on the storyboard before that meeting. The agreement should make clear what experiment will be taken, what evidence will be collected, and what records will be ready for review. A vague statement such as “sometime next week” is insufficient because it weakens the fast feedback loop and can allow several steps to accumulate. Confirming the next meeting and preparation requirements gives the learner a clear immediate commitment and gives the coach a basis for the next review.
Study instruction:
- "At the end of a practice cycle, state the next meeting date and time and list the evidence that must be ready."
Key point:
- A specific next-cycle appointment is part of completing the coaching routine.
- The learner should know exactly what information to prepare before returning.
Source links:
62. Q: How can a coach shorten the time between planning and conducting an experiment?
A: Show answer
The coach can challenge a distant proposed date by asking how the experiment could be performed sooner, whether it could be done today, or whether coach and learner could do it together immediately. If the learner is unsure how to conduct a small, inexpensive test, the coach may accompany the learner or check in as the step is taken. This is part of teaching rapid experimentation, not simply pressuring the learner to work faster. The coach should preserve safety, adequate measurement, and realistic access to the process while removing unnecessary delay between the knowledge threshold and the next observation.
Study instruction:
- "Take a proposed experiment scheduled for next week and identify a safe way to obtain the same learning sooner."
Key point:
- Shortening avoidable delay accelerates learning.
- The coach may model rapid experimentation when the learner needs help designing it.
Source links:
63. Q: Why should the coaching cycle end once the next step is clear?
A: Show answer
Continuing to discuss after the next experiment is defined encourages speculation about results that neither person can know yet. It can also turn the cycle into a broad planning meeting and tempt the learner to add more actions before the next review. The chapter advises ending the discussion once the next step is written and the next cycle is scheduled. The learner should then act quickly and return with evidence. Stopping at this point protects the logic of the Improvement Kata: learn from the next experiment before deciding what should follow it.
Study instruction:
- "Practice a concise close that confirms the next step and appointment, then ends the discussion without forecasting the result."
Key point:
- The cycle should stop at one clear next experiment and a scheduled review.
- Do not replace evidence from the next step with speculation.
Source links:
64. Q: What common errors occur when preparing the next coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
Common errors include asking when a task will be finished instead of when learning can be reviewed, allowing the next cycle to be too far in the future, discussing several steps at once, and ending without a specific date and time. Coaches may also fail to agree what data the learner should collect and prepare on the storyboard. These weaknesses reduce the speed and clarity of the learning loop. A reliable close confirms the single next experiment, its evidence requirements, and the next meeting. If the proposed timing is vague or distant, the coach should explore how to make the experiment and review happen sooner without compromising quality or safety.
Study instruction:
- "Audit a coaching-cycle close against the required step, evidence, date, time, and learning-review purpose."
Key point:
- A vague or distant follow-up weakens the coaching routine.
- The next meeting needs a defined learning purpose and preparation standard.
Source links:
65. Q: How should a coach improve their own coaching practice after each cycle?
A: Show answer
After each coaching cycle, the coach should review how the dialogue went and choose one aspect of coaching to improve in the next cycle. Feedback from a second coach can be especially valuable because the observer may notice weaknesses that the primary coach misses while participating in the conversation. The coach should also select an aspect that they personally want to strengthen; motivation to help learners develop scientific thinking can give practice additional purpose. Improvement of coaching skill follows the same disciplined logic: observe current performance, identify a weakness, choose a focused practice step, and examine what changes.
Study instruction:
- "After a simulated cycle, identify one observable coaching behavior to practice next time and specify how it will be noticed."
Key point:
- Coaches also need deliberate practice and reflection.
- Choose a focused improvement in coaching rather than trying to change everything at once.
Source links:
66. Q: What is the value of feedback from a second coach for the primary coach?
A: Show answer
A second coach provides an external view of the dialogue and can identify where the primary coach missed a weak answer, failed to recognize the knowledge threshold, or allowed the discussion to become unstructured. The primary coach may be focused on listening and responding in real time, making it difficult to observe the whole pattern. The second coach can therefore supply evidence about the coaching process that the primary coach may not have noticed. That feedback should be specific and used to select a manageable practice improvement, rather than treated as a general evaluation of the coach’s competence.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the primary coach’s recollection of a cycle with an observer’s notes and identify one missed cue."
Key point:
- An observer can reveal blind spots in the coach’s live practice.
- Second-coach feedback should lead to a specific coaching experiment.
Source links:
67. Q: What should a learner prepare before a routine coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner should update the storyboard so the information needed for the five-question dialogue is available and arranged in sequence. This includes the target condition, current process and outcome metrics, recent run charts or other direct evidence, an updated obstacle parking lot, and the experimenting record for the current obstacle. The record should contain the last step, advance prediction, actual facts and data, and learning. The learner should also prepare the proposed next step and its prediction before the cycle so the coach can review the plan. Preparation makes the conversation shorter, more factual, and more useful.
Study instruction:
- "Use a pre-cycle checklist to confirm that the target, actual condition, obstacle list, and experimenting record are current and complete."
Key point:
- The learner owns preparation of the storyboard and experiment record.
- Prepared evidence makes the coaching cycle a review of practice rather than a search for missing information.
Source links:
68. Q: How should a coach respond when the learner cannot clearly describe the target condition?
A: Show answer
The coach should not move on as though the target were understood. The coach should ask the learner to read the written condition, describe the desired pattern, identify the process and outcome metrics, and explain how the condition relates to the larger challenge. If the learner remains unclear, the target statement needs further work before it can anchor the rest of the cycle. A vague target tends to produce vague current-condition comparisons and obstacles, so correcting it early prevents later questions from being built on an unstable foundation. The coach should help clarify the destination without prescribing the solution.
Study instruction:
- "Take an unclear target statement and use the chapter’s questions to make its pattern, measures, and achieve-by date explicit."
Key point:
- Do not build obstacle analysis on an unclear target condition.
- The coach’s own understanding is a useful test of the learner’s clarity.
Source links:
69. Q: What should a coach do if the learner’s actual-condition description includes words such as “no,” “none,” or “lack”?
A: Show answer
The coach should examine whether the learner is describing the current process objectively or already framing the condition as the absence of something they believe should be removed. Language such as “no errors” or “lack of automation” may hide a preferred solution or an incomplete description. The learner should instead describe what is actually happening, using current observations and measures. The coach can ask for the process sequence, run chart, and direct evidence, then compare that information with the target condition. The aim is to prevent a proposed remedy from being embedded in the description of reality.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite absence-based actual-condition statements as neutral descriptions of observable process behavior."
Key point:
- Current condition should describe what is happening, not a solution framed as absence.
- Neutral descriptions make the gap to the target easier to examine.
Source links:
70. Q: How can a coach tell whether the learner is jumping prematurely to automation or another solution?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include describing the obstacle as a need to install, automate, train, or purchase something; proposing a specialist to redesign the process before the problem is understood; or treating a possible countermeasure as though it were the obstacle itself. In Dan’s example, “the process is complex” led quickly to asking IT to examine automation, even though the precise source of delay was not known. The coach should ask what specific problem the solution would address, what evidence shows that problem exists, and what the learner needs to learn next. This redirects the discussion from solution preference to process investigation.
Study instruction:
- "Review a proposed solution and ask what observable obstacle it is meant to address and what evidence currently supports that obstacle."
Key point:
- A solution proposal is not proof of an obstacle.
- Investigate the process before committing to a countermeasure.
Source links:
71. Q: How do the five Coaching Kata questions work together as a funnel?
A: Show answer
The questions progressively narrow the dialogue from the larger desired condition to the next concrete experiment. The target condition establishes the destination; the actual condition shows the present state; reflection examines what the last step taught; the obstacle question focuses attention on the current barrier; the next-step question defines a test at the knowledge threshold; and the final timing question creates a rapid review loop. Because each stage depends on the earlier one, weak information near the top of the funnel can distort everything that follows. The coach should preserve the sequence while moving to next-step planning as soon as the knowledge threshold becomes clear.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the logical dependency between each Coaching Kata question and explain what information each contributes to the next."
Key point:
- The dialogue narrows from direction to current evidence to one experiment.
- Later decisions depend on the quality of earlier information.
Source links:
72. Q: How should the coach balance a structured routine with a situation-dependent dialogue?
A: Show answer
The five-question pattern and its preparation provide structure, but the feedback itself must respond to the learner’s actual condition and level of practice. The coach should not mechanically ask every question in the same way if a knowledge threshold has already appeared or if an immediate correction is needed. At the same time, flexibility should not become an excuse to abandon the scientific sequence or drift into solution-giving. The routine supplies a stable frame; observation and judgment determine where to clarify, rehearse, or move directly to the next experiment. With practice, the dialogue can become natural while retaining its essential discipline.
Study instruction:
- "Practice following the five-question structure while adapting the dialogue to a deliberately introduced knowledge threshold."
Key point:
- Structure provides consistency; judgment makes feedback relevant.
- Flexibility should preserve the learning logic of the routine.
Source links:
73. Q: What should happen when the learner reaches the target condition or its achieve-by date?
A: Show answer
When the target condition is reached or its achieve-by date arrives, the four-step Improvement Kata pattern begins again, but the coach and learner should first conduct a summary reflection over the entire improvement process. They should examine why they used the Improvement Kata, what it helped them gain, what went well, what could be improved, and which aspects of the method should be practiced in the next cycle. This reflection captures learning about both the process and the way the improvement work was conducted. The next cycle can then apply those lessons to a new or renewed challenge and target condition.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a summary reflection using the chapter’s five questions before beginning another Improvement Kata cycle."
Key point:
- Reaching the target or achieve-by date triggers a broader learning review.
- The next cycle should use lessons from both process improvement and the way the work was done.
Source links:
74. Q: Why should the learner reflect on the Improvement Kata method itself, not only on the process result?
A: Show answer
A summary reflection should examine how the learner worked, not merely whether the process metric improved. Questions about why the pattern was used, what it enabled, what went well, and what could be better help the learner identify which scientific-thinking habits were strengthened and which need further practice. A process may improve despite weak experimentation, or a target may not be reached even though the learner developed valuable understanding. Reviewing the method helps distinguish outcome from capability and supports continuous development of the learner’s problem-solving skill. Those lessons can improve the next round of the four-step pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Separate lessons about process performance from lessons about how the Improvement Kata was practiced."
Key point:
- Method reflection develops repeatable problem-solving capability.
- A result alone does not reveal the quality of the learning process.
Source links:
75. Q: How can coaching cycles create learner energy and self-efficacy over time?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that coaching cycles may initially feel stiff and awkward for both coach and learner. As the routine becomes familiar, the dialogue develops a natural rhythm and the pair can see obstacles being understood and overcome through successive experiments. This visible progress can create energy and a stronger sense of capability. The coach contributes by keeping feedback specific, making mistakes safe to examine, and helping the learner take small steps quickly. The learner contributes by preparing evidence, making predictions, and owning the experiments. The result is not simply better process performance but increased confidence grounded in demonstrated learning.
Study instruction:
- "Identify evidence of growing learner capability, such as more precise obstacles, better predictions, and greater ownership of experiments."
Key point:
- Confidence grows through visible progress and successful learning habits.
- A supportive routine helps make scientific practice increasingly natural.
Source links:
76. Q: What is a practical end-to-end checklist for a well-run coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
A practical cycle begins by greeting the learner, reducing anxiety, and restating the focus process and challenge. The learner then reads the target condition and presents current process and outcome evidence. The pair reflects on the last experiment using the four-column record, distinguishes facts from learning, and reviews the updated obstacle parking lot. At the knowledge threshold, they define one next experiment, record its step and prediction, verify measurement and safety, and avoid planning a long sequence. They finish by agreeing what data will be prepared and setting a specific date and time for the next cycle. The coach then reflects on one aspect of coaching practice to improve.
Study instruction:
- "Run a full rehearsal using this sequence and verify that each stage produces the evidence or decision needed for the next."
Key point:
- A complete cycle links preparation, evidence, reflection, obstacle focus, one experiment, and follow-up.
- The coach should also select one improvement to their own practice after the cycle.
Source links:
Rows: 76
Table 10
Improvement Kata Coaching Cycles and Scientific Thinking
1. Q: What is the purpose of a coaching cycle in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle is a short, structured dialogue in which a coach helps one learner practice the scientific thinking pattern of the Improvement Kata. The coach reviews the learner’s current direction, recent experiment, evidence, obstacle, and proposed next experiment. The cycle is not intended to solve the process problem for the learner. It is intended to reveal how the learner is thinking, identify where that thinking departs from the Improvement Kata pattern, and provide timely procedural feedback. Repeated cycles help the learner turn deliberate scientific thinking into a more natural working habit while also allowing the coach to improve their own coaching.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a coaching cycle develops the learner’s thinking process rather than supplying the answer to the operational problem."
Key point:
- The coaching cycle teaches and reinforces scientific thinking through repeated practice.
- The learner remains responsible for discovering solutions and taking the experiments.
Source links:
2. Q: What happens during a typical coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The coach meets the learner at the learner’s storyboard and works through the five Coaching Kata question categories in order. The learner points to and reads the prepared information, while the coach listens, probes where needed, compares the learner’s responses with the Improvement Kata pattern, and gives focused feedback. The dialogue reviews the target condition and current condition, identifies the obstacle being addressed, reflects on the last step, and settles on one next experiment and a time to review what is learned. A typical cycle takes about 20 minutes or less. The experiment itself is performed between cycles, not during the discussion.
Study instruction:
- "Rehearse the sequence from storyboard review to agreement on one next experiment and the next coaching time."
Key point:
- A coaching cycle reviews learning and plans the next experiment; it does not conduct the experiment.
- The cycle should end when one clear next step is agreed.
Source links:
3. Q: Why should coaching cycles normally be scheduled every day?
A: Show answer
Daily practice gives a beginner learner frequent opportunities to reflect, receive feedback, correct errors, and take another small step. Without regular review, the learner is likely to revert to familiar habits because the new scientific pattern still feels awkward. Daily cycles also shorten the time between an experiment and reflection, making observations easier to recall and course corrections faster. The chapter contrasts short daily practice with infrequent, longer sessions: a consistent 20-minute routine is generally more useful for building a new skill than a much longer weekly discussion. The exact cadence may need to fit operational realities, but daily is the preferred target.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the learning effects of brief daily coaching with a single long weekly review, focusing on feedback delay and habit formation."
Key point:
- Frequency supports skill acquisition and rapid correction.
- Aim for daily coaching, especially while the learner is a beginner.
Source links:
4. Q: Why is early-workday scheduling recommended?
A: Show answer
Scheduling the cycle early in the workday gives the learner time to carry out the agreed next experiment that same day. It creates a predictable rhythm: review what happened, agree what to learn next, and then go to the process to learn it. If the cycle occurs late in the day, the learner may postpone the experiment, extending the interval before new evidence is available. A regular time also makes coaching easier to protect amid competing work demands. The aim is not merely calendar efficiency; it is to keep the learning loop moving with minimal avoidable delay.
Study instruction:
- "Review a team’s daily schedule and identify a recurring time that allows the learner to experiment immediately after coaching."
Key point:
- A stable early-day appointment creates time for same-day experimentation.
- Schedule coaching to support learning flow, not just administrative convenience.
Source links:
5. Q: What does the 20-minute guideline mean, and how should it be used?
A: Show answer
The 20-minute guideline is a practical boundary for keeping a coaching cycle focused. It is not a rigid quota that overrides the need to understand an important knowledge gap. Most cycles should be completed within that time because the coach is reviewing the learner’s experimenting process and agreeing on one next step, not holding a broad problem-solving meeting. If cycles repeatedly run long, examine whether the coach is allowing speculation, debating multiple factors, solving the problem, or discussing a list of actions. Add depth only where the learner’s evidence or thinking genuinely requires clarification.
Study instruction:
- "Time a practice cycle and identify which discussions are necessary to locate the next learning step and which are digressions."
Key point:
- The 20-minute target protects focus and pace.
- A cycle ends when one well-defined next experiment is clear, not when every issue has been discussed.
Source links:
6. Q: How is a coaching cycle different from a regular meeting?
A: Show answer
A regular meeting may cover several topics, coordinate work, make decisions, and distribute action items. A coaching cycle has a narrower purpose: it examines one learner’s thinking in relation to one focus process and guides the learner toward one next experiment. It follows a repeatable question pattern and relies on the learner’s storyboard as the evidence base. If the conversation becomes a broad status meeting, the coach loses the opportunity to observe the learner’s reasoning and the learner may leave with a list of tasks rather than a testable next step. Other operational matters can be recorded and handled separately.
Study instruction:
- "Take a recent status meeting and redesign the part intended to coach a learner into a focused five-question cycle."
Key point:
- A coaching cycle is a learning routine, not a general coordination meeting.
- Keep its scope to the learner’s process of experimenting and the next step.
Source links:
7. Q: Why should the coach avoid letting a cycle become a long theoretical discussion?
A: Show answer
The learner sees beyond the current knowledge threshold by taking an experiment, not by continuing to speculate in the coaching conversation. Long theoretical discussion can create the illusion of progress while leaving the underlying uncertainty unchanged. It may also encourage the learner to defend opinions, propose several actions, or seek the coach’s preferred answer. The coach should instead help identify what is not yet known and design a small step that can produce useful facts. Once the learner can state the next step and the prediction, the cycle should end so the learner can act and return with evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a point in a discussion where additional debate cannot replace observation or testing, and convert it into an experiment."
Key point:
- Discussion cannot substitute for learning from the process.
- Move from speculation to a small evidence-producing step.
Source links:
8. Q: Why are small steps and frequent course corrections valuable?
A: Show answer
Small steps limit the consequences of an incorrect assumption and make it easier to learn quickly. When the learner takes a modest experiment, the coach can review the result soon and correct the procedure before an ineffective habit becomes established. Small experiments also make it easier to isolate what is being tested and to respond to unexpected results. Infrequent coaching tends to encourage larger batches of activity because the learner has more time to act before review. That reduces the coach’s opportunity to give timely feedback and increases the cost of correcting a mistaken direction.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small test for a real obstacle and explain how its size makes learning faster and correction safer."
Key point:
- Small experiments make feedback and adjustment faster.
- Frequent cycles allow mistakes to become teachable moments rather than entrenched habits.
Source links:
9. Q: What should the learner internalize: the Starter Kata script or the scientific thinking pattern?
A: Show answer
The learner should internalize the scientific thinking pattern that the Starter Kata makes visible and repeatable. The five questions, storyboard, and related routines are practice structures, not the ultimate objective. A learner who memorizes the wording but continues to rely on assumptions has not yet acquired the intended capability. Conversely, as the pattern becomes more natural, the learner can apply the underlying sequence in different situations. The coach should therefore use the structure consistently while paying attention to whether the learner is learning to define a condition, recognize uncertainty, test predictions, and use evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish between mechanically reciting the five questions and demonstrating the scientific reasoning that the questions are designed to develop."
Key point:
- The Starter Kata is a scaffold for developing scientific thinking.
- Success is internalization of the pattern, not memorization of a script.
Source links:
10. Q: What are the main purposes of a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle serves several connected purposes. It lets the coach assess the current state of the learner’s thinking and choose appropriate feedback. It helps the learner identify the current knowledge threshold and design a next experiment that will reveal more. It provides procedural feedback so the learner can practice the Improvement Kata while working on a real process. It also gives the coach and learner a current view of the focus process and provides both with an opportunity to practice their respective routines. These purposes make the cycle both a learning mechanism and a practical management routine.
Study instruction:
- "List the cycle’s purposes and match each one to an observable activity during a coaching conversation."
Key point:
- The cycle develops the learner, informs the coach, and reveals the process.
- Feedback should address both the learner’s thinking and the quality of the next experiment.
Source links:
11. Q: Why are the five Coaching Kata questions described as a funnel?
A: Show answer
The questions narrow attention progressively. The conversation begins with what the learner is trying to achieve, then considers the current condition relative to that target. From that understanding, it identifies the obstacle currently being addressed and narrows further to the next experiment. Each question is nested within the previous one: the obstacle only makes sense in relation to the current condition and target, while the next experiment should address the selected obstacle. The funnel prevents the discussion from jumping directly to actions without first establishing direction and understanding.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the five-question funnel and explain why each later question depends on the information established earlier."
Key point:
- The sequence moves from direction to condition, obstacle, and next experiment.
- A weak answer early in the sequence can undermine the questions that follow.
Source links:
12. Q: Why should the coach initially ask the five main questions in their exact order?
A: Show answer
Using the exact questions and sequence establishes a stable practice pattern for both coach and learner. The repeated structure helps the learner anticipate the type of thinking required and helps the coach avoid reverting to habitual, solution-oriented questioning. It also reinforces the discipline of discussing the target condition before the current condition and of moving toward a specific obstacle and experiment. The wording may initially feel mechanical, but that discomfort is part of learning a new routine. Once the pattern is established, the coach can add clarifying questions between the main questions without abandoning the overall structure.
Study instruction:
- "Practice the five questions verbatim and record where you are tempted to skip, reorder, or replace them."
Key point:
- Consistency makes the desired inquiry pattern easier to learn.
- Add clarification between the main questions rather than casually replacing them.
Source links:
13. Q: What are the two principal functions of the five Coaching Kata questions?
A: Show answer
The five questions have two principal functions. First, they repeatedly reinforce the scientific pattern of the Improvement Kata by guiding the conversation through the same sequence. Second, they reveal how the learner is currently thinking, giving the coach a basis for specific feedback. The questions are therefore not simply a checklist for gathering status information. They are prompts that expose the learner’s reasoning, including where the learner has evidence, where assumptions have entered, and whether the proposed next step is a genuine experiment.
Study instruction:
- "For each main question, identify both the thinking pattern it reinforces and the evidence about the learner’s reasoning that it can reveal."
Key point:
- The questions teach the pattern and diagnose the learner’s current practice.
- The coach listens for reasoning, not merely completed answers.
Source links:
14. Q: What is the coach’s basic ask–listen–compare–instruct routine?
A: Show answer
The coach first asks a question and then stops talking long enough for the learner to respond. The coach listens for the learner’s actual reasoning, compares that response with the scientific pattern expected by the Improvement Kata, and provides a procedural adjustment when needed. This sequence prevents the coach from filling silence, answering their own questions, or prematurely steering the learner. The comparison is not a judgment of the learner’s worth; it is a way to identify a specific practice gap. The instruction should be limited to what will help the learner improve the next part of the process.
Study instruction:
- "Role-play the four stages and ensure the coach pauses after each question before offering feedback."
Key point:
- Listening is an essential stage, not a gap to be filled by the coach.
- Feedback should follow observation and comparison with the desired pattern.
Source links:
15. Q: Why must a coach listen carefully instead of concentrating only on asking all five questions?
A: Show answer
The learner’s responses are the main evidence of how the learner is thinking. A coach who focuses only on getting through the script may miss vague language, unsupported conclusions, mistaken causal assumptions, or signs that the learner has reached a knowledge threshold. The coach may then give generic feedback or accept a weak experiment. Deliberate listening requires pausing, considering what the learner actually said, and asking for detail where the response is imprecise. With practice, the wording of the main questions becomes familiar and the coach can devote more attention to the learner and the process.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recorded or observed coaching conversation and identify one point where a more attentive pause would have revealed a thinking gap."
Key point:
- The quality of coaching depends on what the coach hears in the answers.
- Do not rush through the questions at the expense of diagnosis.
Source links:
16. Q: Why is asking the five questions alone insufficient to make someone an effective coach?
A: Show answer
The wording is relatively easy to learn, but effective coaching requires understanding the intent behind the questions, recognizing patterns in the learner’s reasoning, and selecting useful feedback. A coach can ask every question and still fail to notice that the learner is guessing, confusing facts with interpretation, or proposing an action rather than an experiment. Coaching skill develops through practice, observation, reflection, and feedback from a second coach. The script provides structure; it does not automatically provide judgment. Mastery comes from using the questions to understand the learner and guide better practice.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a sample coaching exchange for both script compliance and the coach’s ability to diagnose and respond to the learner’s reasoning."
Key point:
- Correct wording is necessary but not sufficient.
- Coaching competence develops through deliberate practice and feedback.
Source links:
17. Q: What are clarifying questions, and why are they used?
A: Show answer
Clarifying questions are additional prompts used between the five main questions to explore the learner’s response, obtain missing detail, and locate the current knowledge threshold. They should relate to the main question just asked rather than open an unrelated topic. For example, when the learner describes the current condition, the coach may ask what data supports the description or ask the learner to show the latest measurement. Clarifying questions allow the coach to probe a weak point without abandoning the overall Coaching Kata structure.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare two clarifying questions for each of the five main question categories and explain what evidence or thinking each is intended to reveal."
Key point:
- Clarifying questions deepen the current question rather than create a new agenda.
- Use them to expose missing evidence and define the next learning need.
Source links:
18. Q: What is the difference between open-ended and closed-ended clarifying questions?
A: Show answer
A closed-ended question can usually be answered with yes or no, such as asking whether several measurements were taken. It may confirm a narrow fact but often reveals little about the learner’s reasoning. An open-ended question asks the learner to describe, explain, show, or examine something, such as asking for the latest process data. Open questions generally produce richer information and make it easier to identify assumptions or missing evidence. Closed questions still have a place for precise checks, but they should not dominate a coaching dialogue intended to reveal how the learner thinks.
Study instruction:
- "Convert several yes-or-no questions into open-ended prompts that ask the learner to describe or show the underlying evidence."
Key point:
- Open-ended questions reveal more of the learner’s thought process.
- Choose question form according to the information you need.
Source links:
19. Q: Why can repeatedly asking “Why?” be counterproductive?
A: Show answer
Although asking why can sometimes uncover reasoning, repeated use may sound accusatory or confrontational, especially when a learner is uncertain or feels evaluated by a manager. The learner may become defensive or try to produce an answer that satisfies the coach rather than acknowledge what is unknown. More neutral prompts such as “Tell me more,” “Can you show me?” or “How do you know?” invite evidence and explanation without implying blame. The purpose is to understand the learner’s thinking, not to interrogate or embarrass the learner.
Study instruction:
- "Replace a sequence of repeated “Why?” questions with neutral prompts that request observations, evidence, and explanation."
Key point:
- Question tone affects the learner’s willingness to reveal uncertainty.
- Use curiosity and evidence-seeking language rather than interrogation.
Source links:
20. Q: What does the phrase “Please show me” contribute to coaching?
A: Show answer
“Please show me” is a useful universal prompt because it redirects the discussion from verbal claims to observable information. The learner may point to the storyboard, a run chart, a process record, or the actual work. Showing the information makes gaps, outdated data, unclear definitions, and unsupported interpretations easier to detect. It also reinforces the expectation that important statements should be recorded and supported rather than improvised during the conversation. The phrase is not a demand for paperwork for its own sake; it is a way to anchor dialogue in evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Use “Please show me” during a practice cycle and note how the response changes when the learner must point to evidence."
Key point:
- Visible evidence is more useful than unsupported verbal assurance.
- The prompt helps keep the cycle concrete and data-based.
Source links:
21. Q: What is the purpose of the coach’s Five Question Card?
A: Show answer
The card provides a portable reminder of the main Coaching Kata questions and their supporting prompts. It helps the coach maintain the intended sequence, especially during the early stages when the routine still feels unfamiliar. The coach can refer to the card throughout the cycle rather than relying on memory and drifting into habitual conversation. The chapter recommends starting with the standard card and only later adding personal notes or tested clarifying questions. The card is a practice aid, not a substitute for listening or understanding the Improvement Kata.
Study instruction:
- "Use the standard card for several practice cycles before adding any personal prompts, and record which additions are genuinely useful."
Key point:
- The card supports consistency and readiness to coach.
- A prompt card does not replace coaching judgment.
Source links:
22. Q: Why should the learner also have access to the five questions?
A: Show answer
The learner should know the questions in advance because the cycle is a practice routine, not a surprise examination. A learner’s own card or a visible copy on the storyboard helps them prepare the relevant information and understand the pattern being practiced. Knowing the questions also supports learner ownership: the learner can anticipate what must be updated and bring written evidence rather than depend on the coach to lead every step. Shared visibility makes the conversation more transparent and reduces unnecessary anxiety.
Study instruction:
- "Check that both coach and learner can refer to the same five-question sequence and that the learner prepares before the meeting."
Key point:
- The question pattern should be transparent to the learner.
- Advance knowledge supports preparation and independent thinking.
Source links:
23. Q: How can a coach adapt the question card without weakening the Starter Kata?
A: Show answer
The coach should first become reliable at using the standard questions and sequence. After that, the coach can add clarifying prompts or notes that reflect experience and feedback from a second coach. These additions should deepen the learner’s thinking within the current question category, not replace the main pattern with a different coaching method. A folding card or annotated copy can provide space for tested prompts. The coach should retain the core headings so that the routine remains recognizable and consistent across cycles and learners.
Study instruction:
- "Review any added coaching prompts and classify whether each one clarifies a main question or diverts the cycle from its purpose."
Key point:
- Adaptation should strengthen, not displace, the core routine.
- Keep the five main questions visible and stable.
Source links:
24. Q: Why are the five Coaching Kata questions described as universal questions?
A: Show answer
The same question pattern can be used during all four steps of the Improvement Kata, not only during the executing phase. The details being discussed change as the learner moves through the Improvement Kata, but the coaching pattern remains broadly consistent: review the previous step, identify the current knowledge threshold, and ensure that the next step will produce learning. During planning, the questions may feel less natural because the learner is still building the target and current-condition picture. Even so, the same inquiry structure can help maintain scientific thinking throughout the Improvement Kata.
Study instruction:
- "Map each of the five questions to the four Improvement Kata steps and describe how the subject of the discussion changes."
Key point:
- The coaching pattern can support planning as well as execution.
- The content changes across steps while the learning-oriented dialogue remains consistent.
Source links:
25. Q: What is the learner’s storyboard?
A: Show answer
The storyboard is the learner’s working record of the application of the Improvement Kata to a particular focus process. It presents the unfolding story from left to right in a layout aligned with the five Coaching Kata questions. It records the target condition, current condition, obstacles, experiments, and other relevant information so that the coach and learner can discuss the process using a shared evidence base.
