The storyboard reinforces the scientific sequence and makes the learner’s current thinking visible. It is a living practice tool, not simply a presentation board for management.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the information that belongs on a storyboard and explain how its left-to-right structure supports the coaching dialogue."
Key point:
- The storyboard externalizes the learner’s ongoing thinking and evidence.
- It is used actively during every coaching cycle.
Source links:
26. Q: Who owns and updates the learner’s storyboard?
A: Show answer
The learner owns the storyboard and is responsible for updating it before each coaching cycle. The coach should not routinely maintain it on the learner’s behalf, because doing so can create dependence and obscure whether the learner can independently prepare and organize the information. The coach may give feedback and ask the learner to correct or clarify entries, but the learner makes the updates. This ownership is part of practicing the Improvement Kata: the learner must decide what information is needed and present it in a way that supports evidence-based reflection.
Study instruction:
- "Observe a coaching cycle and verify that the learner, not the coach, prepared and updated the board before the meeting."
Key point:
- Ownership belongs to the learner.
- Preparation is part of the learner’s skill development, not an administrative task delegated to the coach.
Source links:
27. Q: Why should each focus process have its own storyboard?
A: Show answer
A separate storyboard for each focus process keeps the target, current condition, obstacles, and experiments connected to one coherent improvement effort. Combining unrelated processes on one board can blur priorities and make it difficult to see which obstacle or experiment belongs to which target condition. If a learner works on several focus processes, separate storyboards preserve clarity and allow each process to be reviewed on its own terms. The learner should avoid mixing unrelated problems merely because they involve the same department or people.
Study instruction:
- "Review a learner’s improvement assignments and determine whether separate storyboards are needed to preserve a clear line of sight from target to experiment."
Key point:
- One storyboard should represent one focus process.
- Separate records prevent confusion between different improvement efforts.
Source links:
28. Q: Why should the storyboard be located near the focus process when possible?
A: Show answer
Locating the storyboard near the focus process makes it easier to connect the coaching discussion with actual work. The coach and learner can refer to the recorded condition and, when useful, go and see the process rather than relying solely on descriptions. Proximity can support observation, clarification, and rapid verification of what is happening. It also reinforces that the storyboard is a practical working tool linked to the real process, not an abstract report detached from operations.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether the storyboard’s location makes it practical to observe the process during or immediately after a coaching cycle."
Key point:
- Physical proximity can connect discussion with direct observation.
- The storyboard should remain anchored to the work being improved.
Source links:
29. Q: Why should the learner prepare the storyboard in writing before each cycle?
A: Show answer
Written preparation gives the learner time to think independently about the information required by the five questions. It allows the coach to see the learner’s reasoning before the coach’s comments influence it, and it reduces the risk that the learner will improvise an answer during the meeting. Preparing in advance also reveals missing information: if an important point is not on the board, it is less likely to be concealed by a long verbal explanation. This practice keeps the cycle focused on facts, data, and the current knowledge threshold.
Study instruction:
- "Before a practice cycle, require the learner to update each relevant storyboard section and identify any information they could not verify."
Key point:
- Advance written preparation makes the learner’s thinking visible.
- The board should be ready before the coach arrives.
Source links:
30. Q: Why should the learner point to and read the storyboard rather than tell the whole story from memory?
A: Show answer
Pointing to and reading the written information anchors each answer in the shared record. It discourages the learner from generating an improvised explanation that may contain opinions, omissions, or speculation. It also keeps the cycle concise and allows the coach to see exactly what has been recorded and what is missing. The method may initially feel mechanical, but it helps the learner distinguish documented facts from information that exists only in conversation. If an important point is absent from the board, the learner can identify what must be obtained and record it before the next cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Practice answering each coaching question by pointing to the corresponding storyboard entry and reading it before adding further explanation."
Key point:
- Reading from the board exposes what is documented and what is missing.
- Verbal fluency should not conceal gaps in evidence.
Source links:
31. Q: What makes a storyboard effective and useful?
A: Show answer
An effective storyboard is neat, current, comprehensible, and organized so that the coach can quickly understand the learner’s progress and reasoning. It should capture relevant facts, data, predictions, observations, obstacles, and next steps in a well-thought-out way. A cluttered or outdated board makes it harder to distinguish the current focus from historical information. Maintaining the board also develops the learner’s ownership of the Improvement Kata practice. Neatness is not an aesthetic objective alone; it supports accuracy, continuity, and efficient review.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a storyboard for clarity, currency, logical sequence, and whether another coach could understand the current experiment without a lengthy verbal explanation."
Key point:
- A useful storyboard makes the current state of learning easy to see.
- Maintenance and clarity are part of the learner’s practice.
Source links:
32. Q: When may the storyboard be corrected during a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The learner may correct or adjust an entry during the cycle when the change reflects feedback or a clarification that is already supported by known information. For example, a description may need clearer wording or a plan may need to be made more specific. The coach should not encourage the learner to alter factual records based on an unverified assumption. If information is missing, the learner should obtain the facts after the cycle and update the board before the next review. This distinction protects the integrity of the record and reinforces the difference between correcting a plan and changing evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Classify proposed storyboard changes as clarification, plan adjustment, or unsupported alteration of facts, and explain which can be made immediately."
Key point:
- Plans can be refined during coaching; facts must remain evidence-based.
- Do not replace missing data with a convenient assumption.
Source links:
33. Q: What is a knowledge threshold in the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
A knowledge threshold is the point at which the learner no longer has sufficient facts and data to proceed confidently and begins to rely on assumptions, generalizations, or speculation. It marks the boundary between what is currently known and what still needs to be learned. Every coaching cycle is likely to reveal such a boundary in relation to the obstacle being addressed. Recognizing it is not a sign of failure; it is a useful discovery because it identifies where the next experiment should focus. The learner’s task is to acknowledge the gap and determine how to obtain the missing information.
Study instruction:
- "Take a current improvement problem and state explicitly what is known, what is not known, and where the knowledge threshold lies."
Key point:
- A knowledge threshold marks the limit of current evidence.
- Finding the threshold identifies the next learning opportunity.
Source links:
34. Q: Why is acknowledging “I don’t know” essential to scientific thinking?
A: Show answer
Scientific thinking requires distinguishing evidence from belief. If a learner feels compelled to answer every question, they may invent explanations, overstate certainty, or hide the point where knowledge ends. Saying “I don’t know” honestly allows the coach and learner to define the gap and design an experiment to address it. The coach should make this response safe by emphasizing that uncertainty is expected and useful. The aim is not to reward ignorance, but to prevent unsupported conclusions from becoming the basis for action.
Study instruction:
- "Practice stating a knowledge gap without apology and follow it with a precise description of how the missing information could be obtained."
Key point:
- Honest uncertainty is a foundation for disciplined inquiry.
- The useful response to not knowing is to design a way to learn.
Source links:
35. Q: What language may signal that the learner has reached a knowledge threshold?
A: Show answer
Words such as “I think,” “probably,” “maybe,” “could,” and “most likely” can indicate that the learner is interpreting rather than reporting verified facts. Other warning signs include broad estimates such as proposing a 50 percent change without calculation, referring to old data, relying on anecdotes, or expressing certainty without being able to explain the evidence. These expressions do not automatically prove that the learner is wrong; they are cues for the coach to investigate. The coach should ask what supports the statement, how current the information is, and what observation or test would clarify it.
Study instruction:
- "Review recent coaching notes and highlight uncertain language, unsupported numerical estimates, stale evidence, and anecdotal claims."
Key point:
- Uncertain wording and unsupported confidence are cues to probe.
- Treat these signs as prompts for investigation, not as automatic proof of error.
Source links:
36. Q: How can outdated data reveal a knowledge threshold?
A: Show answer
Data may once have been accurate but no longer represent the process being improved. If the process, product mix, staffing, equipment, or operating conditions have changed, an old measurement may not support a current conclusion. A learner who relies on stale data may appear evidence-based while actually making an assumption about present conditions. The coach should ask when the data were collected, whether the process has changed since then, and what current measurement is needed. The next step may simply be to collect fresh observations at the focus process.
Study instruction:
- "For each data set used in a current improvement effort, record its collection date and assess whether operating conditions have changed since measurement."
Key point:
- Evidence must be relevant to the current process condition.
- Old data can disguise a current lack of knowledge.
Source links:
37. Q: How should a coach respond when the learner gives an anecdote instead of process data?
A: Show answer
The coach should acknowledge the observation but distinguish it from evidence that establishes a pattern. An anecdote may point toward a useful question, yet it may represent an unusual event or a limited sample. The coach can ask the learner to show the broader data, explain how observations were selected, or plan a direct measurement at the process. The goal is not to dismiss experience; it is to use experience as a source of hypotheses that can be tested. The learner should avoid treating one vivid example as proof of a general cause.
Study instruction:
- "Turn an anecdotal explanation into a measurable question and specify what additional observations would be needed to evaluate it."
Key point:
- Anecdotes can generate hypotheses but rarely establish process patterns by themselves.
- Use data and observation to test the pattern.
Source links:
38. Q: What should the coach do when a knowledge threshold appears during any of the five questions?
A: Show answer
The coach should pause the normal flow and focus on the gap rather than continue through the script as if the answer were sound. Clarifying questions can expose what is missing, and the learner can then identify a next step to obtain the necessary facts. The coach may move directly to planning the next experiment instead of completing every remaining question in sequence. The five-question structure is a guide for learning, not a rule that should override the need to investigate. The next cycle can resume with better information.
Study instruction:
- "Practice identifying a threshold in different question categories and decide when it is appropriate to pause and move to experiment planning."
Key point:
- Do not continue building conclusions on an unsupported answer.
- The structure should serve learning rather than obstruct it.
Source links:
39. Q: Why should a coach encourage the learner to capitalize on a knowledge threshold?
A: Show answer
A knowledge threshold is valuable because it reveals exactly where further learning is needed. Instead of treating uncertainty as a problem to hide, the coach can frame it as progress: the learner has found the boundary of current understanding and can now design a focused experiment. This response builds the habit of looking for what must be learned next rather than defending an incomplete explanation. The coach should encourage the learner to acknowledge the gap, define it precisely, and use the next step to see further.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a response that positively acknowledges a learner’s uncertainty and immediately helps translate it into an evidence-gathering step."
Key point:
- A threshold is a discovery that helps focus learning.
- Celebrate the clarity of the gap, then test it.
Source links:
40. Q: Why can a learner’s next step involve investigating something they thought they already knew?
A: Show answer
An earlier belief may have been based on incomplete observations, assumptions, or data that did not represent the actual process. When the coach probes the learner’s reasoning, the learner may discover that a supposedly settled point is uncertain. Returning to investigate it is not regression; it is correction of the knowledge base. The chapter emphasizes that learners may need to revisit familiar ground because the purpose is to see the process as it is, not to defend earlier conclusions. A reliable next experiment can therefore involve measuring or observing a condition that was previously taken for granted.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one assumption in an improvement project that has never been directly verified and design a small check of it."
Key point:
- Reinvestigation is appropriate when earlier knowledge proves weak.
- Scientific progress follows evidence, not attachment to previous beliefs.
Source links:
41. Q: What does the gasket example teach about premature causal conclusions?
A: Show answer
In the example, the learner observes damage near screw hole number three and concludes that installing the third screw causes the damage. The coach notices the learner’s uncertain wording and probes what is actually happening. The learner admits not knowing and proposes observing the installation process. Later observation reveals that screw number one can bump the gasket out of position, creating damage that appears at hole three. The example shows that the location where a defect is visible may not identify the action that caused it. Direct observation can overturn a plausible but untested explanation.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the gasket case as observation, initial interpretation, knowledge threshold, next experiment, and revised understanding."
Key point:
- The visible location of a defect is not necessarily its root cause.
- Observe the process before prescribing a countermeasure.
Source links:
42. Q: How should a coach distinguish coaching from taking over problem solving?
A: Show answer
The coach’s role is to help the learner practice a sound problem-solving process, not to decide the solution. The learner’s experiments determine what is discovered, and neither coach nor learner knows in advance which steps will ultimately reach the target condition. The coach should ask open questions to understand the learner’s reasoning, identify weaknesses in the procedure, and give feedback that improves the learner’s next experiment. If the coach starts directing the learner toward a preferred solution, the learner loses the opportunity to develop independent scientific thinking and may become dependent on the coach.
Study instruction:
- "Review a coaching exchange and mark each coach statement as inquiry, procedural feedback, or solution direction."
Key point:
- The learner owns the problem-solving journey.
- The coach improves the method of learning rather than supplying the answer.
Source links:
43. Q: What is the difference between being directive about procedure and directive about the solution?
A: Show answer
Being directive about procedure means helping the learner make the next step specific, measurable, connected to the current obstacle, and capable of producing useful learning. Being directive about the solution means telling the learner what countermeasure to implement or steering them toward the coach’s preferred explanation. The chapter supports the former but warns against the latter. A coach can insist that the learner observe the actual process, state a prediction, or collect relevant data without deciding what the data will show. This distinction allows the coach to maintain rigor while preserving learner ownership.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite solution-oriented instructions as procedural coaching prompts that specify how to learn without prescribing what the result must be."
Key point:
- Coaches can be firm about scientific procedure.
- Avoid predetermining the countermeasure or conclusion.
Source links:
44. Q: Why should the coach avoid leading questions that point toward a preconceived answer?
A: Show answer
Leading questions can make the learner search for the coach’s preferred conclusion rather than examine the process independently. The learner may agree with the implied answer, even when the evidence is weak, because the coach has authority or appears confident. This creates compliance rather than learning and can conceal alternative explanations. Open-ended questions should reveal how the learner sees the situation and what evidence supports that view. The coach may challenge the learner’s procedure, but should not use questions as a disguised way of imposing a solution.
Study instruction:
- "Identify leading questions in a sample dialogue and replace them with neutral prompts that allow multiple evidence-based answers."
Key point:
- A question should reveal the learner’s thinking, not plant the coach’s answer.
- Neutral inquiry protects independent discovery.
Source links:
45. Q: Why is encouragement an important part of coaching?
A: Show answer
Improvement Kata practice occurs in real work, where setbacks, plateaus, awkwardness, and slow progress are normal. A beginner may become discouraged if progress is judged only by immediate achievement of the target condition. Encouragement helps the learner recognize genuine learning and movement in the right direction. It should be specific and connected to observable development rather than generic reassurance. If the learner never experiences a sense of progress, the coach should examine whether the coaching approach, experiment size, feedback, or challenge level needs adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a learner’s recent improvement in thinking or experimentation and prepare specific feedback that names the observed change."
Key point:
- Encouragement supports persistence through the discomfort of learning.
- Praise should be grounded in observable learning and growth.
Source links:
46. Q: How should a coach give specific praise without implying that the coach already knows the solution?
A: Show answer
The coach should praise the learner’s improved reasoning, clearer observation, better experiment design, or increased ability to recognize uncertainty. Statements such as noting that the learner is beginning to distinguish evidence from assumptions are more useful than vague praise. The coach should avoid wording that suggests the learner is merely approaching a hidden answer known by the coach. Otherwise the learner may infer that the coach is withholding the solution. Specific praise should reinforce the scientific behavior being developed and leave ownership of discovery with the learner.
Study instruction:
- "Draft three examples of praise that identify a specific improvement in the learner’s process without hinting at a secret solution."
Key point:
- Praise the learning behavior, not an assumed proximity to the coach’s answer.
- Specific feedback strengthens self-efficacy and independent practice.
Source links:
47. Q: Why should progress be understood as uneven rather than a steady daily staircase?
A: Show answer
Learning a new thinking pattern does not produce measurable improvement every day. The learner may encounter setbacks, plateaus, and periods when the new routine feels slower or less natural than familiar habits. These experiences are expected when practicing outside established routines. The coach should not interpret every difficult day as failure or promise continuous visible progress. Instead, the coach should help the learner notice what has been learned, make the next experiment manageable, and continue practicing. The relevant trend is development over time, not a requirement that each cycle produce an immediate win.
Study instruction:
- "Review a learning timeline and distinguish short-term setbacks from longer-term development in the learner’s practice."
Key point:
- Skill development is uneven and may include plateaus.
- Maintain practice while using setbacks as information for adjustment.
Source links:
48. Q: How should a coach adjust the level of structure to the learner’s readiness?
A: Show answer
The coach should sense whether the learner needs close instruction or can work with a more flexible dialogue. Beginners generally benefit from strict adherence to the Starter Kata because they are still acquiring the pattern. As the learner develops, the coach can allow more natural discussion while retaining the essential sequence and purpose. The coach should respond to what the learner actually demonstrates rather than assume that everyone learns at the same pace. If uncertain, especially early in practice, the chapter advises staying with the Kata structure.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a learner’s current ability to prepare evidence, identify a threshold, and design an experiment, then select an appropriate level of coaching structure."
Key point:
- The learner’s demonstrated readiness should shape the delivery of feedback.
- When in doubt, preserve the Starter Kata structure.
Source links:
49. Q: How can a coach avoid making the routine robotic while still preserving its discipline?
A: Show answer
The coach should remain firm about the essential pattern but adapt the wording and depth of follow-up questions to the learner’s responses. The five main questions provide a stable framework; clarifying questions provide room for human judgment and exploration. A robotic cycle merely recites prompts without listening, while an undisciplined conversation abandons the pattern. Skilled coaching balances consistency with responsiveness: the coach knows what must be covered and uses the learner’s actual answers to determine where more detail or feedback is needed.
Study instruction:
- "Run two practice cycles: one using only the script and one using targeted clarifying questions, then compare structure and responsiveness."
Key point:
- Discipline comes from preserving the pattern, not from speaking mechanically.
- Adapt the dialogue without losing its learning purpose.
Source links:
50. Q: What is the coach’s responsibility when a learner struggles with the structured approach?
A: Show answer
The coach should distinguish normal discomfort from a genuine mismatch between the learner’s needs and the way the routine is being delivered. New practice often feels stiff and unnatural, so discomfort alone is not a reason to abandon the structure. The coach should observe whether the learner is gradually becoming more capable of preparing information, answering with evidence, and planning experiments. If the learner is truly struggling, the coach may adjust the level of instruction or clarify the purpose of the routine while maintaining its core pattern. The aim is to support adaptation, not remove the productive challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Record whether a learner’s difficulty reflects ordinary beginner discomfort or a specific obstacle to understanding and applying the routine."
Key point:
- Do not confuse normal learning discomfort with failure of the method.
- Adapt support while retaining the core scientific practice.
Source links:
51. Q: What is a coaching cycle notebook, and why is it useful?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle notebook is a record the coach maintains to track important points from coaching sessions and improve the coach’s own practice. It can capture the learner’s name, date, start and end time, focus process, next step, impressions of the learner’s practice, feedback given, and an issue the coach wants to work on next. This record is particularly useful when one coach supports multiple learners, because it preserves continuity and helps the coach prepare for the next interaction. It should be concise enough to maintain consistently and focused on learning rather than unnecessary personal commentary.
Study instruction:
- "Create a one-page coaching notebook template with fields for learner, process, next step, feedback, and the coach’s own improvement target."
Key point:
- The notebook supports continuity across cycles and learners.
- The coach records observations about both learner practice and personal coaching development.
Source links:
52. Q: What should the coach record about their own performance after a cycle?
A: Show answer
The coach should note which part of their own practice needs improvement, such as listening more carefully, recognizing a knowledge threshold earlier, using less leading language, or giving more focused feedback. This reflection prevents the coach from treating the cycle only as an assessment of the learner. The coach is also practicing a skill and should identify a concrete next improvement. Notes should be specific enough to guide behavior in the next cycle rather than simply state that the conversation went well or poorly.
Study instruction:
- "After a practice cycle, write one observable coaching behavior to retain and one specific behavior to improve in the next session."
Key point:
- The coach is also a learner.
- Self-reflection should produce a concrete coaching practice target.
Source links:
53. Q: What is the role of the second coach?
A: Show answer
The second coach observes the primary coach conducting coaching cycles and provides feedback to improve the primary coach’s practice. The second coach helps the coach see what they may miss while concentrating on the learner and the five questions. This arrangement recognizes that coaching is itself a skill requiring observation, practice, and correction. The second coach does not take over the learner’s problem solving and is not responsible for giving feedback directly to the learner. The primary relationship remains between the coach and learner; the second coach develops the coach’s capability.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the three roles in a coaching arrangement and identify who owns the learner’s improvement work and who develops the coach."
Key point:
- The second coach coaches the coach.
- The second coach’s feedback is directed to the primary coach’s practice.
Source links:
54. Q: Why is observation by a second coach important?
A: Show answer
A primary coach may be so focused on asking questions and managing the dialogue that they fail to notice the learner’s thinking pattern or their own habits. An observer can track the sequence, identify where the learner reaches a knowledge threshold, and see whether the coach recognizes it. The observer can also notice when the conversation becomes unstructured or when the coach takes over the problem. Feedback based on direct observation is more useful than general advice because it points to specific behavior that can be changed in the next cycle.
Study instruction:
- "Arrange for a second coach to observe a cycle and compare their notes with the primary coach’s own assessment."
Key point:
- External observation reveals blind spots in the coach’s practice.
- Feedback should be grounded in what the observer actually saw and heard.
Source links:
55. Q: What should a second coach look for while observing a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
The second coach should note whether the dialogue follows the five Coaching Kata question headings, where the learner’s knowledge threshold appears, and whether the primary coach notices it. The observer should also look for places where the learner’s procedure departs from the Improvement Kata pattern, whether the coach identifies that departure, and where the conversation becomes unstructured. The observer should record concrete examples rather than broad judgments. The purpose is to help the primary coach understand the effect of their behavior and select a useful improvement target.
Study instruction:
- "Use an observation sheet to record sequence, knowledge-threshold cues, procedural deviations, coach responses, and moments of lost focus."
Key point:
- Observe both the learner’s thinking and the coach’s response to it.
- Concrete observations make feedback actionable.
Source links:
56. Q: Why might a second coach time the parts of a coaching cycle?
A: Show answer
Timing can reveal how attention is distributed across the dialogue. After the participants gain experience, the review of the target and current condition should often be relatively brief, while discussion of obstacles and the next experiment may require more attention. If the opening sections consume most of the time, the coach may be overexplaining or failing to focus the discussion. If the cycle is rushed at the point of planning the experiment, the next step may remain vague. Timing is diagnostic rather than a rigid performance target; it should help the coach understand where the dialogue needs adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "Time each question category during an observed cycle and discuss whether the allocation of time supported a clear next experiment."
Key point:
- Timing can expose imbalance or drift in the dialogue.
- Use timing to improve focus, not to force identical durations.
Source links:
57. Q: What should be included in a second coach’s observation record?
A: Show answer
A useful observation record can include the date, coach, learner, focus process, duration, sequence followed, evidence of the learner’s knowledge threshold, procedural gaps, the coach’s response, and specific examples of effective or ineffective questioning. The second coach may also record feedback given after the cycle and the primary coach’s chosen practice target. The record should remain simple because note-taking must be quick while observing. Completed forms or concise notes can be kept together to show development across multiple observations.
Study instruction:
- "Design a concise observation form that captures behavior without distracting the second coach from listening to the cycle."
Key point:
- A simple record supports continuity and evidence-based feedback.
- Capture enough detail to identify a practice change, not every word spoken.
Source links:
58. Q: How should a second coach begin the feedback discussion after observing a cycle?
A: Show answer
The second coach should first ask the primary coach for their own impressions. Useful prompts include how the dialogue went, where the learner’s knowledge threshold was, what the coach was paying attention to, where the learner departed from the Improvement Kata pattern, and what the coach would change in a repeat attempt. This self-assessment helps the coach reflect before hearing another person’s view. It also reveals whether the coach noticed the same events as the observer and provides a basis for focused feedback.
Study instruction:
- "Practice opening an observation debrief with reflective questions before offering your own assessment."
Key point:
- Begin with the primary coach’s reflection.
- Self-assessment helps expose differences between intended and observed practice.
Source links:
59. Q: What is the recommended structure for feedback from a second coach?
A: Show answer
A practical structure has three parts. First, ask the primary coach for impressions of the cycle. Second, provide feedback using a concrete observation, the likely effect of that behavior, and a specific suggestion to practice. For example: “I observed that you answered your own question before the learner finished. I think this reduced the evidence you could hear. You might pause longer after asking the next question.” Third, agree when the next coaching cycle will be observed. This structure links evidence to impact and action, making feedback easier to apply.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare feedback using the sequence “I observed…”, “I think this results in…”, and “You might practice…”."
Key point:
- Effective feedback links observed behavior, its effect, and a practical adjustment.
- End with a date and time for the next observation.
Source links:
60. Q: Why should a second coach limit feedback to one or two key points?
A: Show answer
A long list of corrections can overwhelm the primary coach and make it difficult to practice any one behavior consistently. The purpose of feedback is to improve the next cycle, so the second coach should select the one or two changes most likely to improve the coach’s current performance. Other observations can be retained for later sessions. Focused feedback also makes progress easier to assess because the coach and second coach can look for the selected behavior in the next observation.
Study instruction:
- "From a sample observation, select the two highest-value coaching improvements and explain why the remaining notes can wait."
Key point:
- A small number of focused adjustments are easier to practice.
- Do not overload the coach with every observation at once.
Source links:
61. Q: Should the second coach give feedback privately or with the learner present?
A: Show answer
Both arrangements are used. Some coaches prefer private feedback, while others include the learner so that everyone can understand how coaching is being developed. If the learner is asked to leave, the coach and second coach should explain what they are doing so the learner does not assume they are discussing the learner behind closed doors. Because the people involved may have reporting relationships, the setting can affect trust. The choice should therefore be deliberate and transparent, taking account of local relationships and the purpose of the debrief.
Study instruction:
- "Decide how to conduct a second-coach debrief in a specific reporting environment and explain how you will prevent misunderstanding."
Key point:
- Transparency protects trust in the coaching arrangement.
- Choose the debrief setting with the relationship dynamics in mind.
Source links:
62. Q: Why is the second coach not responsible for giving feedback to the learner?
A: Show answer
The second coach’s assignment is to help the primary coach improve their coaching practice. If the second coach begins instructing the learner directly, the roles become blurred and the learner may receive competing direction. It can also weaken the primary coach’s ownership of the coaching relationship. The primary coach remains responsible for giving the learner procedural feedback and guiding the next experiment. The second coach observes and develops the coach, preserving a clear line of responsibility.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the reporting and feedback relationships in a three-person coaching arrangement and identify who gives feedback to whom."
Key point:
- The primary coach remains the learner’s coach.
- The second coach focuses on developing the primary coach.
Source links:
63. Q: How often should a beginner coach be observed by a second coach?
A: Show answer
The chapter recommends that a beginner coach practice coaching cycles daily and have a second coach observe each time, with corrective feedback. Frequent observation gives the beginner coach a short feedback loop for building the skill. As the coach develops, the organization may adjust the level of observation to fit capability and need, but the early stage benefits from close support. Observation should not be treated as surveillance or punishment; it is deliberate practice intended to improve coaching quality.
Study instruction:
- "Plan an initial observation schedule for a beginner coach and identify how the frequency could be reviewed as capability develops."
Key point:
- Frequent observation accelerates development during the beginner stage.
- Second-coach feedback is part of deliberate coaching practice.
Source links:
64. Q: What should a coach do if the learner is not learning the Improvement Kata or repeatedly missing target conditions?
A: Show answer
The coach should examine the quality of coaching before assuming the learner is unwilling or incapable. The chapter notes that when learners are not learning the Improvement Kata or consistently failing to achieve target conditions, the problem often lies in the coaching. The five questions can appear simple while their intent is difficult to master. The coach should seek observation and feedback from a second coach, review whether the dialogue reveals the learner’s thinking, and check whether experiments are properly designed. This does not mean every failure is caused by coaching, but coaching practice is a critical factor to investigate.
Study instruction:
- "When progress stalls, review the coaching process, the learner’s experiment design, and the feedback loop before assigning blame."
Key point:
- Stalled learner progress is a reason to inspect coaching quality.
- Use second-coach observation to diagnose the coaching process.
Source links:
65. Q: How do everyday managerial interactions reinforce the Improvement Kata?
A: Show answer
Formal coaching cycles establish the routine, but a manager’s other interactions can reinforce the same scientific thinking pattern. Questions in meetings, informal discussions, and support at the process should encourage people to define the target, understand current conditions, identify obstacles, and learn through experiments. Over time, the manager’s everyday behavior can model the pattern and help create a culture in which scientific thinking is normal. If the manager uses the pattern only during scheduled coaching but reverts to telling people what to do in all other interactions, the broader learning signal becomes inconsistent.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three routine managerial interactions and rewrite them to reinforce observation, evidence, obstacles, and experimentation."
Key point:
- Coaching cycles are the formal starting point, not the only opportunity to model the pattern.
- Everyday managerial behavior helps shape the organization’s thinking culture.
Source links:
66. Q: What is the relationship between coaching cycles and deliberate practice?
A: Show answer
A coaching cycle creates a repeatable opportunity for the learner and coach to practice a specific behavior, receive feedback, and try again. Deliberate practice involves working on a skill that is not yet fluent, noticing errors, and using those errors to guide improvement. The cycle makes this practical by focusing on the learner’s actual process and the coach’s actual behavior rather than relying on abstract training alone. Small experiments and frequent review make it possible to correct weaknesses while they are still manageable.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one specific coaching or learner behavior to practice deliberately over the next several cycles and define how improvement will be observed."
Key point:
- Skill develops through repeated practice with feedback.
- Real work provides the setting for deliberate practice.
Source links:
67. Q: How can a coach recognize whether the learner is becoming more independent?
A: Show answer
Independence is visible when the learner prepares the storyboard without prompting, identifies uncertainty honestly, distinguishes observations from interpretations, and proposes a small experiment with a clear prediction. The learner should be able to explain why the step addresses the current obstacle and what information will be learned. Independence does not mean the coach stops asking questions or providing feedback. It means the learner increasingly carries the thinking process rather than waiting for the coach to supply the next action.
Study instruction:
- "Use a cycle observation checklist to assess preparation, evidence use, threshold recognition, and experiment design without coach prompting."
Key point:
- Independence is demonstrated through the learner’s own preparation and reasoning.
- The coach supports capability rather than creating dependence.
Source links:
68. Q: How should a coach handle a learner who wants the coach to tell them what to do?
A: Show answer
The coach should resist immediately providing a solution and instead return the discussion to what the learner knows, what remains uncertain, and what experiment could produce the next useful information. A learner may initially interpret questions as a request to guess the coach’s preferred answer, especially when the coach is also the learner’s manager. The coach should clarify that the learner is expected to think and prepare independently. Procedural guidance can still be given when the learner’s proposed experiment is weak, but the coach should avoid taking responsibility for discovering the solution.
Study instruction:
- "Role-play a learner asking for the answer and practice redirecting the conversation toward evidence and a learner-owned experiment."
Key point:
- Do not reinforce dependence by routinely supplying the solution.
- Guide the learner’s process while keeping problem solving with the learner.
Source links:
69. Q: How should a coach maintain continuity when working with several learners?
A: Show answer
A coach supporting several learners needs reliable routines and records. Each learner should have a distinct storyboard for each focus process, a regular coaching time where practical, and a notebook entry that captures the process, next step, feedback, and coaching issue to revisit. The coach should avoid relying on memory to manage multiple experiments and commitments. Consistent use of the question card and a simple notebook makes it easier to move between learners without losing the structure or confusing one person’s obstacles with another’s.
Study instruction:
- "Design a weekly coaching calendar and notebook index that keeps learners, focus processes, next steps, and follow-up times distinct."
Key point:
- Standard routines reduce the risk of confusion across learners.
- Use simple records to preserve continuity.
Source links:
70. Q: What would you do if a coaching cycle follows the five questions but produces no clear next experiment?
A: Show answer
First, examine whether the learner’s answers were precise and supported by current evidence. Because the questions are nested, an unclear target or current condition may have produced an equally unclear obstacle. Locate the point where the reasoning became vague, ask open-ended clarifying questions, and identify the knowledge threshold. Then help the learner define one small step that will produce information about that gap. Do not finish with a broad action list or accept a vague intention such as “look into the problem.” Specify what will be observed or tested and what the learner expects to learn.
Study instruction:
- "Take a vague coaching outcome and rewrite the next step so it identifies the action, evidence to collect, and expected learning."
Key point:
- A cycle is not complete until the next learning step is clear.
- Trace vagueness back to the earliest weak answer in the nested sequence.
Source links:
71. Q: What would you do if the learner presents a confident explanation but cannot show supporting evidence?
A: Show answer
Treat the confidence as a cue to investigate, not as proof that the explanation is correct. Ask the learner to show the data, describe how the conclusion was reached, and distinguish observed events from interpretation. If the evidence is insufficient, name the knowledge threshold and plan a small experiment to verify the explanation. Avoid arguing about who is right or allowing authority to settle the question. The learner should return with observations that can support, refine, or refute the current belief.
Study instruction:
- "Practice challenging an unsupported confident statement with neutral requests for evidence and a testable next step."
Key point:
- Confidence does not replace evidence.
- A claim should remain provisional until it is tested against the process.
Source links:
72. Q: What would you do if the coach repeatedly supplies solutions during the cycle?
A: Show answer
The second coach should record specific instances where the primary coach directed the learner toward a solution or answered on the learner’s behalf. In the debrief, first ask the primary coach what they noticed and intended. Then explain the observed behavior, its effect on learner ownership, and one alternative prompt to practice. The primary coach can then focus on asking open questions and giving procedural feedback rather than deciding the countermeasure. The behavior should be revisited in the next observation to determine whether the adjustment is taking hold.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an observation-based feedback example that helps a coach replace solution-giving with process-focused questioning."
Key point:
- Solution-giving can undermine learner ownership.
- Coach the coach using concrete observations and one practical alternative.
Source links:
73. Q: How can an organization use coaching-cycle records without turning them into bureaucracy?
A: Show answer
Records should support preparation, continuity, and learning rather than become a reporting burden. The learner’s storyboard should contain the information needed to conduct the cycle, while the coach’s notebook and second-coach observation notes should capture only the details needed for follow-up and development. Avoid duplicating the same information across several forms or requiring extensive narrative that does not improve the next experiment. A useful record makes the current condition, obstacle, learning, next step, and responsibility easier to see. If the paperwork begins to dominate the coaching, simplify it while retaining the essential evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Review existing coaching forms and remove fields that do not support preparation, observation, feedback, or follow-up."
Key point:
- Documentation should serve learning and continuity.
- Keep records simple enough to maintain consistently.
Source links:
74. Q: How can the quality of a coaching cycle be evaluated beyond whether it finished on time?
A: Show answer
A high-quality cycle leaves the learner clearer about the target and current condition, more precise about the obstacle, honest about the knowledge threshold, and ready to conduct one well-designed experiment. The coach should have listened closely, identified the learner’s thinking gap, and provided focused procedural feedback where needed. The storyboard should reflect the current evidence and next plan, and the next review should be scheduled. Finishing quickly is not sufficient if the next step is vague; taking longer is not automatically better if the discussion becomes speculative.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a completed cycle against evidence quality, learner ownership, threshold recognition, experiment design, and follow-up rather than duration alone."
Key point:
- Quality is measured by learning and a clear next experiment.
- Time is a useful indicator, not the sole measure of effectiveness.
Source links:
75. Q: How do the coach, learner, and second coach form a learning system?
A: Show answer
The learner practices scientific problem solving on the focus process and records the work on the storyboard. The primary coach uses the five-question routine to reveal the learner’s thinking and provide procedural feedback. The second coach observes the primary coach and helps improve the coaching method. Each role therefore has a distinct learning loop, but the loops reinforce one another: stronger coaching supports better learner practice, while observation and reflection help the coach improve. Clear role boundaries prevent the second coach from taking over the learner’s work or the primary coach from becoming dependent on the observer.
Study instruction:
- "Diagram the three learning loops and identify the evidence each participant uses to improve their own practice."
Key point:
- All three participants practice, but each has a different responsibility.
- Role clarity allows the learning loops to reinforce rather than compete.
Source links:
76. Q: What are the most important management lessons from Chapter 11?
A: Show answer
Chapter 11 presents coaching cycles as a disciplined daily routine for developing scientific thinking through real work. The five questions provide a stable sequence, while careful listening and clarifying questions reveal the learner’s actual reasoning. The learner owns a prepared storyboard, and the coach focuses on the knowledge threshold and the next experiment rather than taking over problem solving. Encouragement and adaptive feedback help learners persist through uneven progress. A second coach develops the primary coach through observation and focused feedback. Together, these practices make coaching a management capability and help scientific thinking spread beyond formal sessions into everyday interactions.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter by linking the five-question routine, storyboard ownership, knowledge thresholds, learner independence, encouragement, and second-coach feedback."
Key point:
- Effective coaching develops scientific thinking through frequent, evidence-based practice.
- The coach’s own learning and everyday managerial behavior are essential to sustaining the routine.
